A. TRALIN, PUBLISHER, 12, RUE DU VIEUX-COLOMBIER, PARIS VIᵉ.

## By the same author:
---

Ernest HELLO. — *The Day of the Lord*. <note>original: "Le Jour du Seigneur"</note> New edition with a Foreword by Georges Goyau of the French Academy. <note>Georges Goyau (1869–1939) was a prominent French historian and specialist in religious history.</note>

1 duodecimo volume <term>in-12<gloss>a book size where each sheet is folded into twelve leaves</gloss></term> of 8 preliminary and 80 pages . . . . 2 francs.

It was exactly half a century ago, when a defeated France was making an effort to rebuild itself and sought the very foundations of a renewal within tradition, that **Ernest Hello**—with that prophetic tone that was innate to him and which proved that the supernatural was like a second nature to him—raised his voice in favor of one of those foundations: the law of Sunday rest. <note>Hello wrote this work following the French defeat in the Franco-Prussian War of 1870, advocating for Sunday as a day of rest for both spiritual and social health.</note>

And in a first hymn—for his thoughts always ended as a hymn—he celebrated its requirements and glorified its benefits in the name of the social right of God.

Then a second hymn was added, exalting the social right of the workers and the liberation promised and assured to them by the divine commandment of Sunday rest.

Sunday is the day of God and it is the day of the worker, the day that both love, the day when men feel like brothers to one another, where their shared heritage takes on a kind of fraternity toward the God-Man, who is a Son along with them of the Common Father.

At a time when the ruins that purchased our victory <note>This likely refers to the aftermath of World War I, suggesting this edition was published around 1920–1921, fifty years after Hello's original 1871 text.</note> require new social architectures no less imperatively than fifty years ago, the teachings of **Hello**, which have lost none of their eloquence, find all their relevance once again.

->(*Extract from the Foreword*).<-

<meta>This page contains the formal ecclesiastical approvals and the author's submission to Church authority, common in Catholic publications of the early 20th century. It establishes the work's compliance with canon law and provides the official date of authorization for this edition (April 1914).</meta>

We declare, in order to comply with the decrees of Urban VIII dated March 13, 1625, June 5, 1631, and July 5, 1634, concerning the canonization of saints and the beatification of the blessed, that we do not intend to give any of the facts or words contained in this work more authority than is given or will be given to them by the Catholic Church, to which we pride ourselves on being very humbly submitted.
<note>Pope Urban VIII (1623–1644) established strict rules regarding private revelations and the "cult" of uncanonized individuals to ensure that personal spiritual writings were not treated as official Church dogma without formal review.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the formal ecclesiastical approvals (the Nihil Obstat and Imprimatur) to the publisher's or editor's foreword for the 1914 sixth edition. It establishes the enduring popularity of Ernest Hello's translation of Angela of Foligno's work and positions Hello as a writer uniquely suited to capturing her spiritual depth.</meta>

# FOREWORD

## TO THE SIXTH EDITION

---

Thirty-five years have passed since the death of Ernest Hello; and the fame he was able to secure for the little book of Blessed Angela of Foligno remains so enduring that a new edition of it has become necessary.
<note>Ernest Hello (1828–1885) was a French Catholic writer and philosopher. His translation of Angela of Foligno, first published in 1868, is credited with modernizing her medieval prose for a 19th-century audience.</note>
<note>Angela of Foligno (c. 1248–1309) was an Italian mystic and member of the Secular Franciscan Order. She is known for her intense spiritual experiences and her "Book of Visions and Instructions."</note>

In the sublime regions where the text of Blessed Angela led him, Hello as a translator was not in unfamiliar territory; to be faithful to his author’s thought, he had to take flight, but was Hello’s intellectual life ever anything other than a dizzying ascent toward the infinite?

Everything we know about Angela is contained within the prologues of Brother Arnaud and the personal revelations of the Blessed herself. The biographers who, in 1909, on the occasion of her <term>sixth centenary</term><note>The 600th anniversary of her death in 1309.</note>, attempted to speak of her, were unable to do more than comment on these meager details, just as...
<note>Brother Arnaud was a Franciscan friar and Angela’s relative and confessor. He acted as her scribe, translating her spoken Umbrian dialect into the Latin text known as the "Memoriale."</note>

<summary>This foreword introduces the 1914 edition of Angela of Foligno's revelations, celebrating the translator Ernest Hello's ability to match the mystic's spiritual intensity. it highlights that the primary sources for Angela's life remain her own writings and the records of her scribe, Brother Arnaud.</summary>

<keywords>Ernest Hello, Angela of Foligno, Brother Arnaud, mysticism, 1914 edition, Franciscan spirituality, hagiography</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the foreword to the 1914 sixth edition. The previous page mentioned that biographers who attempted to write about Angela during her 1909 centenary could do little more than comment on the original texts, just as others had done before them.</meta>

# FOREWORD TO THE SIXTH EDITION

...the good priest *Jacobilli*, a fellow citizen of *Angela*, did so as early as 1747 in his *Lives of the Saints of Umbria* <note>original: "Vies des saints de l'Ombrie"</note>.

Foligno was in a state of joy in February 1909; for several days, *Angela* was celebrated there. Canon *Célestin Bordoni* published a new study on the illustrious mystic, titled *Teacher of Theologians, Angela of Foligno* <note>original Latin: "Magistrat theologorum, Angela da Foligno". The title "Teacher of Theologians" reflects the high regard for her spiritual insights despite her lack of formal education.</note>, which was deeply moving in its devotion.

Furthermore, Foligno possesses a prelate who, for many years and with a meticulous erudition that no amount of research can exhaust, has made himself the historian of *Angela’s* glory. I refer to *Monsignor Michele Faloci Pulignani*. As early as 1889, he published a bibliographic essay on the life and short works of the Blessed in the *Franciscan Miscellany* <note>original: "Miscellanea Francescana"</note>, for which he serves as director. In that essay, he cataloged forty-five headings of printed works concerning her and eight headings of manuscripts whose comparison would be useful for a definitive edition of her works; we believe that, since then, *Monsignor Faloci Pulignani’s* lists have grown even more extensive.

Already, the edition of the *Revelations* printed in Foligno in 1714 marked an improvement over the one published in 1643 by *Bolland* in the *Acts of the Saints* <note>original Latin: "Acta Sanctorum". The Bollandists were a group of Jesuit scholars who aimed to compile historically accurate biographies of the saints.</note>. It is likely that the learned efforts of *Monsignor Faloci Pulignani* will help—

<summary>This page lists the various historians and scholars, from the 18th century to the early 20th century, who worked to document Angela of Foligno's life and organize her writings. It highlights the scholarly contributions of Lodovico Jacobilli and Monsignor Michele Faloci Pulignani.</summary>

<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Foligno, Lodovico Jacobilli, Célestin Bordoni, Michele Faloci Pulignani, Franciscan Miscellany, hagiography, bibliography, Acts of the Saints, Bollandists</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page concludes the foreword to the 1921 sixth edition. It follows a discussion on the bibliographic history of Angela of Foligno's works, transitioning into a defense of Ernest Hello's specific style of translation.</meta>

# FOREWORD TO THE SIXTH EDITION

...subsequent editors will prefer, for certain passages, a more flawless version; it is possible that, later on, translators may emerge who are more committed than Hello was to following word-for-word the definitive text of the Blessed woman <note>Refers to Angela of Foligno (c. 1248–1309), the Italian mystic and author of the text.</note>.

But none, surely, will be able to rival Hello <note>Ernest Hello (1828–1885) was a French Catholic philosopher and writer whose 1868 translation of Angela's works, though not strictly literal, was highly influential in the 19th-century mystical revival.</note> in what he calls "accuracy according to the spirit, which infuses the author's blood from one language into another" — accuracy according to the spirit, which even tries "to translate tears."

->GEORGES GOYAU<- <note>A prominent French historian of religion and member of the Académie Française who wrote this preface.</note>

February 1921

<summary>This page concludes the preface by Georges Goyau, who argues that while future scholars may produce more literal translations of Angela of Foligno's work, Ernest Hello’s version remains unrivaled for capturing its emotional and spiritual essence.</summary>
<keywords>Ernest Hello, Georges Goyau, translation theory, spiritual accuracy, Angela of Foligno, mystical literature, 20th century Catholic history</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the formal Preface of the work, following the introductory publication notes and blank divider pages mentioned previously. It establishes the central metaphor of the book: the diversity of spiritual life.</meta>

# PREFACE

---

From afar, all the stars look alike. Our eyes are so weak that these worlds, hidden by distance, are for us merely points of gold which, during summer nights, tremble in the black azure with the same flickering. But, if it were permitted to approach, if it were possible to look closer, we would perceive with unknown wonder <note>original: "admirations inconnues"</note> unknown differences. We would see that the distance which separates the suns establishes between them singular relationships and contrasts. We would see that the hand of the Creator has sown different seeds in his fields, and that his feet have not left the same trace everywhere in the dust that his voice brought forth from nothingness <note>original: "néant"; this refers to the theological concept of *creatio ex nihilo*, or creation out of nothing</note>.

From afar, all the <term>elect: a theological term referring to those chosen by God for salvation or sainthood</term> look alike, and common opinion <note>original: "l'opinion vulgaire"; refers to the unrefined or general public view</note> believes it can lump them together in the same indifference. Ignorance, which is always so certain of itself, believes that the life of the elect is a monotonous thing; that to be among the elect, one must be cast in a certain mold, and that this mold, always the

<summary>This preface uses an astronomical metaphor to argue that just as stars appear identical but are uniquely diverse, the "elect" or saints possess individual characters despite appearing uniform to the ignorant observer.</summary>

<keywords>preface, stars, Creator, the elect, spiritual diversity, sainthood, creation</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface's argument regarding spiritual diversity. It transitions from the general metaphor of stars used on the previous page to the specific nature of the saints, concluding with the introduction of Angela of Foligno.</meta>

# PREFACE

same, promises uniformity to the figures it fashions.

Now, nothing is more false.

The world of the <term>elect: those chosen by God, specifically the saints</term> is a universe; larger than the material universe, but composed, like the latter, of unity and variety. To name the universe, one must name these two elements.

The elect are all elect; but each has their own proper virtue. Jesus Christ, who is their unity, their peace, their universal <term>type: here meaning the archetype or original model</term>, marks them, like a royal seal, with the sacred unity of the Spirit. But remembering that he made the violets, the lilies, and the roses differently capable of absorbing <note>original: "s'assimiler"</note> the rays of the same sun, he has left to each their own mark, their character, their form, and their name. There are not two leaves of a tree in the world that are exactly alike. All the stones of the eternal temple are the stones of the Jerusalem which shall not end; but not one among them is carved like its neighbor.

If Saint Gertrude <note>Saint Gertrude the Great (1256–1302) was a German Benedictine mystic known for her deep devotion to the humanity of Christ</note> was, says Olier <note>Jean-Jacques Olier (1608–1657) was a key figure in the French School of Spirituality and founder of the Sulpicians</note>, the saint of the humanity of Jesus Christ, and Saint Catherine of Genoa <note>A 15th-century Italian mystic (1447–1510) famous for her mystical experiences and her service to the sick</note> the saint of his divinity, it seems that the Blessed Angela of Foligno unites these two kinds of contemplation, of light and of adoration. It seems that she penetrated into the abysses of the heights, as into those of the depths. The double abyss, of which she sometimes speaks,

<summary>This page explains that saints (the elect) are not identical copies but unique individuals, much like different flowers or stones in a temple. It introduces Angela of Foligno as a mystic who balances the human and divine aspects of Christ.</summary>

<keywords>unity and variety, the elect, Jesus Christ, Saint Gertrude, Catherine of Genoa, Angela of Foligno, Jean-Jacques Olier, spiritual diversity, mysticism</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface's description of Angela of Foligno's mystical focus. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page, connecting the "abysses of the height" mentioned previously to the "double abyss" of her earthly life, inspired by the name of the apostle Thomas.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>

-># PREFACE<-

naming, without realizing it, one of the twelve apostles, <term>Thomas Didymus: an apostle of Jesus, also known as "Doubting Thomas"</term> <gloss>1</gloss>, the double abyss was the dwelling where she spent her earthly life. It was her palace, her temple, her royal residence. When she questions the depth, the <term>Passion of Jesus Christ: the suffering and crucifixion of Jesus</term> tells her fearsome secrets. She plunges a terrified and terrifying gaze into His human sorrows, and even into His physical pains. She sees as she loves; this is why she sees even the shape of the nails; she measures the pain by the number of their facets. She calculates the aggravations of this pain according to the details she has discovered.

Among these accounts of the <term>Passion</term>, there are terrible things that one forgets to consider. A person's life—which is, in any case, much too short to cast a lead line into the abysses <note>original: "jeter la sonde," a nautical metaphor for measuring depth</note>—is furthermore spent on other things. <term>Angela: Angela of Foligno (1248–1309), an Italian mystic and Franciscan tertiary</term> had a fearsome familiarity with the physical tortures of the <term>Passion</term>, which allowed her devouring eyes to follow the flesh of <term>Jesus</term>, the flesh of the feet and the hands, into the interior of the wood where the nails drove them. She witnesses the atrocious tension of the arms, legs, and nerves. She recounts as if she had seen—as if she had seen what even the executioners did not see.

<note>Footnote at the bottom of the page</note>
1. <gloss>Thomas Didymus in Hebrew signifies "double abyss." <note>The author provides a mystical etymology here; while "Thomas" and "Didymus" both traditionally mean "twin," the text links them to the concept of deep spiritual depths.</note></gloss>

<summary>This page describes Angela of Foligno's intense, almost microscopic focus on the physical sufferings of Jesus during the Passion, characterizing her life as a residence within a "double abyss" of contemplation.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Passion of Christ, mysticism, Thomas Didymus, spiritual depth, physical suffering, crucifixion</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface’s exploration of Angela of Foligno’s mystical experiences. Having described her focus on the physical suffering of the Passion on the previous page, the text now shifts to the psychological and spiritual nature of her "piercing" love and the frustration of expressing divine mysteries in human language.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>

-># PREFACE<-

Love is more piercing than hatred. It hears what is said. It hears what is not said. It hears silence, reads what is not written, and guesses what must be guessed in order to grow. It increases by its discoveries, enriches itself with its treasures, and then complains of its poverty, in order to extract new secrets.

When she <note>Refers to the soul of the mystic, Angela, or the personification of Love</note> questions the abyss of the height, her word is but a cry of helplessness, an eternal lamentation; she weeps over the limit that stops her in her flight at the moment of departure. Her eloquence consists of complaining that she cannot say what she feels, and this complaint, repeated at every moment, is never monotonous, because it is always true.

Striking in her flight against the <term>ineffable: that which is too great or sacred to be expressed in words</term> secrets, the unrevealed mysteries, she looks like an eagle which, having taken its flight from the top of the mountain where the snow is eternal, reaches regions where there is no longer, even for it, breathable air. Her thoughts fail her. She descends again, struggles against the words that in turn fail her, engages them in hand-to-hand combat, where she is at once defeated and victorious, and then she looks like an eagle which, clutching and shaking them in its talons—for it remembers the mountain and the desert—shakes the bars of its cage...

In the twenty-seventh chapter, several souls

<summary>This page describes the "piercing" nature of mystical love and the soul's struggle to articulate divine mysteries. It uses the metaphor of an eagle flying into thin air to illustrate how the mystic's thoughts and words fail when encountering the "ineffable."</summary>
<keywords>mystical love, ineffable mysteries, eagle metaphor, divine silence, spiritual struggle, eloquence of lament, Angela of Foligno</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Preface from page 12. The final sentence of the previous page ("In the twenty-seventh chapter, several souls") concludes here as those "who lack words" find sustenance in the mystical writings of Angela of Foligno. The text continues to explore the limits of human language in describing the divine, specifically referencing the theology of Father Faber.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>

-># PREFACE<-

who lack words will perhaps find bread for them. There are glimpses of abysses there, magnificent attempts to speak the <term>Ineffable: that which is too great or sacred to be expressed in words</term>, followed by a repentance more magnificent than the attempts themselves; the pardon that <note>Saint Angela of Foligno (1248–1309), a Franciscan mystic and author of the "Memorial."</note>Angela asks for her blasphemies, after having stammered the things of rapture, tears through the horizon like lightning in the dark night. Abysses open behind abysses; human intelligence appears short and brief, and the soul finds reassurance in its its thirst. For God declares Himself infinite, and the treasures of eternity will not be exhausted.

<note>Father Frederick William Faber (1814–1863), a prominent English Catholic priest and theologian known for his works on the spiritual life.</note>Father Faber speaks of this intimate life of God, this life he calls unimaginable, where attributes operate that have no name here below. Beyond, he says, what is probable, God lives His life of glory. It is the infinite union of things unknown.

If the mysteries we know, he says somewhere, are already so formidable, what must we think of those mysteries, even greater still, of which the slightest thought has never been given to man?

It is from this sublime authority that the thunderbolts flashed, whose distant reflections, dazzling Angela’s heart, threw her body to the ground motionless in her room. Striking in its superb flight against the mysteries unreveal-

<summary>This page discusses the spiritual depth of Angela of Foligno's writings, suggesting they provide "bread" for those struggling to express the divine. It quotes Father Faber on the unimaginable nature of God's life and the existence of mysteries beyond human comprehension.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Father Faber, mysticism, the Ineffable, divine mysteries, spiritual rapture, theology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Saint Angela of Foligno’s mystical experiences from page 13. The opening syllable "ed" completes the word "unrevealed" from the final sentence of the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>

-># PREFACE<-

ed, living in the fearsome familiarity of the shadow, she enjoyed them without knowing them. Struck down at every moment by some terrible joy, it is always, she says, for the first time; for the last flash of lightning eclipses all the others. All lights are but shadows next to the final light. The treasures through which her gaze searches are forever inexhaustible, and eternity promises to her ever-renewed joy fresh delights that will never end. When, after having piled the mountains of happiness enjoyed by the <term>elect: those chosen by God for eternal life in heaven</term> upon the mountains of happiness that all men would have enjoyed, if all false joys were changed into true joys and lasted, without interruption, until the end of the world, she searches in every direction, with the anxiety of helplessness, to reach, if it were possible, <note>In this context, the author suggests Angela is trying to find words that "overstate" to match the reality of her experience, though human language inevitably falls short.</note>exaggeration, and when, after having added together all known and unknown joys, she declares herself ready to abandon them all, if she had to choose between them and a single second of the ineffable glory for which there are no words—that glory which is her own glory, her blinding light and her being struck as if by lightning—when she strikes the air with her lips as if to tear from it sounds it does not contain, what we must admire most in her speech is the silence that lies beyond.

->## PREFACE<-

Passion, as if she were questioning the depths to wrench from them that unknown reason for worship which hides in the heights, she counts one by one the instruments of the <term>Passion: the physical and spiritual suffering of Jesus leading up to and during his crucifixion</term>, and since the narratives do not say everything, since the Gospel is very restrained, and since known details increase her thirst instead of quenching it, she approaches the <term>cross</term> of Christ face to face, in the secrecy of <term>prayer: specifically 'oraison,' an internal, contemplative form of prayer performed without spoken words</term>. There, as if in a private arena, alone with him, in the secret of the vision, she asks every thorn of the cross how the blood flowed from the brow of the <term>Fils de l'Homme: Son of Man, a title used by Jesus to emphasize his humanity and his role in salvation</term>. Questioning every instrument of torture about the nature of the torments, discerning through the intuition of love several unknown tortures behind the known ones, calling successively upon speech and silence to her aid, she recounts some of the compassions <note>In this mystical context, 'compassions' refers to the shared suffering or deep empathy felt between Jesus and those around him during his ordeal.</note> that accompanied the Passion: Jesus's compassion for himself, for his disciples, for his mother, and for his father. The inventions of love—for love is the greatest of inventors—lead <term>Angela: Saint Angela of Foligno (1248–1309), the Italian mystic whose visions are recorded here</term>, if one dares to speak this way, into the interior of the <term>wounds of Jesus: the marks left on Jesus's body by the nails and spear during the crucifixion</term>; with the audacity of adoration, she gazes fixedly, and her eye does not falter. For love is stronger than death, and if it knows the tremors of desire, it is ignorant of those of fear.

Father Faber <note>Father Frederick William Faber (1814–1863) was a prominent English Catholic priest and theologian whose writings focused heavily on the internal lives of Jesus and Mary.</note> remarks that the sorrows of the Virgin Mary were increased by the power she possessed to look at them directly without distraction, instead of fleeing from them as other creatures do, aided by their own weakness.

Angela of Foligno <note>Saint Angela of Foligno (1248–1309) was an Italian mystic known for her "Memorial," which records her intense visions and "ineffable" (indescribable) spiritual experiences.</note> sees the ineffable sorrow of Jesus, which was granted and dispensed to him, along with divine light, by the hand of God. This light—through which he himself saw what he was in himself, and what sin had made of him; this terrible light by which he saw in all their horror his death and the crime of his death, as well as sin and <term>Calvary: the site outside Jerusalem where the crucifixion took place</term>—this light which, she says, transformed Jesus Christ into sorrow, and into ineffable sorrow, seems to have revealed to the <term>contemplative: a person dedicated to a life of deep, focused prayer and spiritual meditation</term> something of what it revealed to the human soul of Jesus. And, in the thirst that devours her—a thirst for knowledge and love that grows more parched the more she drinks—she turns to question all creatures one by one about the <term>Passion: the period of suffering and the ordeal of the crucifixion endured by Jesus</term> of their crucified God. Challenging them in turn to describe it to her as she sees it, she lets out this sublime cry:

> "If someone told it to me, I would say: 'It is you, it is you who suffered it.'" And in the security of her transports <note>In mystical theology, "transports" or "raptures" refer to states of intense spiritual ecstasy where the soul feels carried away by God.</note>, if an angel predicted the death of her love, she would respond: "It is you who fell from heaven." <note>This implies that any messenger claiming God's love could die or fail must be a fallen angel (a demon), as such a claim is a lie against the nature of divine love.</note>

<summary>This page explores how divine light intensified the suffering of Jesus and Mary by allowing them to perceive the full horror of sin and the Passion, as seen through the mystical visions of Angela of Foligno.</summary>

-># PREFACE<-

Saint Dionysius the Areopagite <note>Saint Dionysius the Areopagite (c. 5th–6th century) was a Christian theologian whose writings on "apophatic" theology—the idea that God is better described by what He is not than what He is—profoundly influenced Christian mysticism.</note>, having experienced the insufficiency of words and of light, turns to the darkness to adore, in its depths, the unknown God: *Luminous darkness, he says, marvelous darkness that radiates in splendid flashes, and which, being neither seen nor grasped, floods with the beauty of its fires the holily blinded spirits* 1.

Those who are familiar with the great doctors of <term>mystical theology: the study of direct, subjective experiences of the divine</term>, with Saint Dionysius the Areopagite, with <term>Saint John of the Cross: a 16th-century Spanish mystic and poet famous for his writings on the "Dark Night of the Soul"</term>, etc., will recognize in Angela of Foligno the ardent and pure practice of the sublime theories that have brought renown to this high science.

Words always fail Angela, and more and more so, because the glory she contemplates retreats while rising ever higher. Speech is a blasphemy in her eyes, because beyond the things that words define, her eye contemplates those things that words cannot define.

This is somewhat like those streaks glimpsed on summer nights that reveal themselves as <term>nebulae: hazy clouds of gas or clusters of stars in deep space</term> when telescopes are improved.

Then above appears another streak of vague light, which is to become a new cluster...

After each explosion of light and love, Angela asks for forgiveness. Her sense of God makes her adoration seem like a blasphemy in the eyes of her own soul.

The sky is a figure, superb although limited, immense although finite. Like the female sinner of the desert <note>Likely a reference to Saint Mary of Egypt, a popular figure in medieval hagiography who lived as a hermit in the desert, often conflated in artistic tradition with the "sinful woman" (Mary Magdalene) who wiped Christ's feet with her hair.</note>, it spreads out a golden head of hair.

One might say that light, not finding itself pure enough to exist before the face of God, would wish to wipe the feet of the throne with its hair, and carry the repentance of suns higher than any gaze can reach.

Translation is always a difficult work. The translation of an intimate thing is a very difficult work. When it concerns a funeral oration or a formal speech, one can, to a certain extent, replace Latin <term>periods: long, complex sentence structures used in classical rhetoric to create a balanced, rhythmic effect</term> with English periods. But when it comes to penetrating the depths of the soul, when it comes to struggling with the intimacy of inner forces, when it is not merely words but cries that must be rendered—cries, silences, and sobs—the task becomes formidable: accuracy is the law of translation. But there are two kinds of accuracy: accuracy according to the letter, which renders words one after...

<page-num>19</page-num>

-># PREFACE<-

the others; accuracy according to the spirit, which infuses the author’s blood from one language into another. Without neglecting the first of these two types of accuracy, I have tried above all to commit myself to the second. I have tried to make live in English <note>original: "français." The author is writing the preface to his French translation of the Latin original; this English version mirrors that intent for the current reader.</note> the book that lived in Latin. I have tried to make the soul that cried in Latin cry out in English. I have tried to translate the tears.

Brother Arnaldo <note>Arnaldo of Foligno was a Franciscan friar and Angela’s confessor; he recorded her visions in the "Memorial," often struggling to capture her intense experiences in his formal Latin.</note>, who wrote under Angela’s dictation, placed the two prologues you are about to read at the beginning of his book. I have also tried to preserve for this excellent man the traits that distinguish him: his profound respect and his admirable sincerity.

Angela’s life is a drama in which the spiritual life manifests as a visible reality. The secret truth becomes something tangible and palpable. It is no longer possible to mistake it for a dream; it is a drama full of blood and fire. By contrast, the outward life of dishonest men—the life without light, without truth, the life far from the Spirit—appears as a shadow, as a figure, as a ghost, and as a nightmare.

The affinity between intimate things and sublime things is the light that illuminates this drama, where height and depth give each other the kiss of peace <note>This is a biblical allusion to Psalm 85:10, "Righteousness and peace have kissed each other," symbolizing the reconciliation of God and humanity.</note>.

<summary>The translator explains his goal of capturing the "spirit" and "tears" of the original Latin rather than just the literal words, while introducing Angela's life as a visceral, tangible drama.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes Ernest Hello's preface to his translation of Angela of Foligno's "Book of Visions and Instructions," transitioning from the technical challenges of translation to the spiritual intensity of Angela’s mystical experiences.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>

-># PREFACE<-

This drama takes place within the **Ineffable** <note>The "Ineffable" refers to the Divine or spiritual experiences that are too great to be expressed in human words.</note>. It is a flash of lightning that tears through a thick cloud. Angela's language is a hand-to-hand struggle with things that cannot be spoken. In the atmosphere into which she is introduced—like a common person terrified by the proximity of the sanctuary—human vocabulary retreats in silence. Held captive within human speech, Angela acts like Samson. Manoah <note>original: "Manué"</note> in Hebrew means "rest." Like Samson, the son of Manoah, Angela—the daughter of Ecstasy—takes the gates of her prison upon her shoulders and carries them up to the Heights <note>This is a reference to the biblical story in Judges 16:3, where Samson carries away the gates of the city of Gaza; Hello uses this as a metaphor for Angela breaking through the "prison" of human language to reach God.</note>.

— You who read this book, do not look upon it with the cold gaze of curiosity. Remember the glorious realities, remember the terrible realities, and pray to Angela's God for the translator of her book.

# PROLOGUE OF BROTHER ARNALD <note>Brother Arnald (or Arnaud) was a Franciscan friar and Angela’s relative who recorded her spiritual journey, translating her local Umbrian dialect into Latin.</note>

Lest the pride of worldly wisdom <note>This refers to the "Sagesse du monde"—human intellectual vanity that ignores spiritual truths.</note> should fail to receive the confusion it deserves from the eternal God, the Lord has raised up a woman accustomed to the things of the age [this world], bound by the obligations of the world, who had a husband, children, and a fortune; a simple woman, lacking in formal learning and strength; but who, having received and accepted deep within herself, along with the cross of Jesus Christ, the infused power <term>infused power: a theological term for grace or wisdom poured directly into the soul by God, rather than learned through study</term> of God, broke the bonds of the world, climbed the summit of evangelical perfection, renewed in its absolute fullness the folly of the cross <note>A reference to the biblical idea in 1 Corinthians that the message of the cross seems like "folly" to the world but is wisdom to believers.</note>, which is the wisdom of the perfect, and showed, in the abandoned path of the good Jesus—in that path declared impossible and unbearable by the words and examples of those who play the "great person"—showed, as I was saying, not only a possible life, not only an easy life, but unheard-of delights: the delights of the heights.

O Divine and Perfect Wisdom, how you have revealed in your servant the folly of all wisdom <note>The text ends mid-sentence, contrasting God's wisdom with the perceived "wisdom" of the secular world.</note>

# PROLOGUE OF BROTHER ARNALD

humanity! You have set a woman against men, a humble woman against the proud, a simple woman against the clever, an ignorant woman against the learned, a creature who despises herself against the hypocrites who admire themselves, the silence of the lips and the consuming, burning, astonishing activity of life against tongues full of words and hands empty of deeds! And the wisdom of warmth has been confronted with the wisdom of the mind, which is the <term>knowledge of Jesus</term> <note>original: *science de Jésus*. In this context, "science" refers to a deep, experiential knowledge or a spiritual discipline rather than modern laboratory science.</note>, and of Jesus crucified! In a strong woman, God has manifested his light, which was buried—as if in a sepulcher of human flesh—beneath the blindness of theorists.

Enfants of our sacred mother, beware of **human respect**! <note>The term "human respect" (*respect humain*) refers to the fear of being judged or mocked by others for one’s faith, which often prevents a person from doing what is right.</note> Learn from our Angela, learn from our angel, learn from the **Angel of the Great Counsel** <note>A traditional title for Christ, derived from the Septuagint version of Isaiah 9:6.</note>, the voice of magnificence and the wisdom of the cross! Learn the poverty, the pains, the reproaches, and the obedience of Jesus; learn Jesus Christ, learn His Mother, and when you have learned, teach this knowledge to men, teach it to women, teach it to every creature in the language of real, effective, and powerful deeds! And so that the glory of your vocation, children of high knowledge, may appear before your eyes, know, my beloved, that she who taught us God performed what she taught. Remember, my beloved, that

In the name of the most holy Trinity, in the name of the almighty God, in the name of Jesus Christ and the Virgin,

Here is the manifestation of the gifts of the Most High bestowed upon the spirit of my <term>mother</term> <note>A term of spiritual respect; Angela was Arnald's spiritual mother and he was her confessor.</note>, Angela of Foligno. This follows the word and the promise that He made in His Gospel:

> “If anyone loves me, he will keep my word, and my Father will love him, and we will come to him, and we will dwell in him;”

And:

> “He who loves me, I will manifest myself to him” (*John 14:21 and 23*). <note>original: *Joannes*</note>

The Lord has allowed us to experience the truth of this word ourselves. He has recently manifested Himself to some devoted souls, but most particularly to the spirit of my mother Angela.

I, Brother Arnald, of the <term>Order of Friars Minor</term> <note>Also known as the Franciscans, a religious order founded by St. Francis of Assisi focused on poverty and Gospel preaching.</note>, by dint of many supplications, wrested from her the secret of her eyes and her soul. Intimately united to her by

## SECOND PROLOGUE OF BROTHER ARNALD

...a daily familiarity and through the charity of Christ, I nevertheless required the most serious and sacred reasons in the world to compel her.

The gifts of God were locked within her by a formidable seal, and whenever I approached, whenever I went to ask, she would reply: “My secret is mine.” How many times I heard those words! In all likelihood, I would have failed forever, and humanity would have been deprived, had Angela not seen my immense sorrow. Angela took pity on me: compassion was what first moved her; then came her concern for human souls and the love she had for her neighbor; but finally and above all, she received a command from above: she was forced, and she yielded. I wrote what you are about to read.

Angela dictated, and I wrote; but she spoke in spite of herself. In the midst of her <term>revelations</term> <note>Divine truths or communications granted to a person through supernatural means, such as visions or internal locutions.</note>, she would interrupt herself to say to me:

> “Everything I have just articulated is nothing! All of this makes no sense! I cannot speak.”

Sometimes, in the most sublime moments, when words failed her—overcome by the height of these things—comparing what she was saying with what she wished she could say, she would stop and cry out to me:

> “I <term>blaspheme</term> <note>While typically meaning to speak irreverently of God, in mystical writing, this often refers to the "sin" of using limited, human words to describe the infinite, which the mystic feels is a form of falsehood or distortion.</note>! Brother, I <term>blaspheme</term>! Our

<meta>This page continues the "Second Prologue" by Brother Arnald. Previously, Arnald described his struggle to convince Angela of Foligno to share her mystical experiences and her initial resistance, noting that she often felt her words were "nothing" or even "blasphemous" compared to the reality of her visions. Here, he elaborates on the failure of human language and his own perceived inadequacy as her scribe.</meta>

“...poor human language,” she said, “is hardly suitable except for occasions dealing with bodies and ideas; beyond that, it can do no more. When it comes to divine things and their influences, speech dies away completely.”

Sometimes she used words that were absolutely unknown and foreign to me: it was immense, it was powerful, it was dazzling—it was a thousand times more wonderful than everything I have written. She could formulate nothing. I caught a glimpse of something <term>unheard of</term> <note>In French, *inouï*, meaning something so extraordinary it has never been heard or experienced before; a common theme in mystical literature regarding the "ineffable" nature of God.</note>; but, not knowing what it was, I remained there without writing. Sometimes I saw Angela in deep sorrow, because it was impossible for her to manifest anything.

As for me, to tell the truth, I understood only a very small part of her words. I often compared myself to a <term>sieve</term> <note>A tool for sifting; Arnald uses this metaphor to humbly suggest he is a poor vessel who lets the "fine flour" of spiritual truth slip away while keeping only the "coarse husks" of literal language.</note> that lets through and throws to the wind what is most precious in the substance, retaining only what is coarsest. I am clearly a completely incapable man; I do not understand divine things: here is the proof. After having written under her <term>dictation</term> <note>While Angela spoke in her local Umbrian dialect, Arnald translated her words into Latin as he wrote, which likely contributed to her feeling that the written text "did not recognize" her original experience.</note>, I would read it back to Angela in order to submit the work to her corrections. Very often she would say to me: “It is strange! It is astonishing! What have you written? I do not recognize that.”

One day she said to me:

# SECOND PROLOGUE OF BROTHER ARNALD

> “I do not know how you do it: what you have written there has no flavor.”

Another time, she made this remark to me:

> “The words you have written serve at most to remind me from afar of the memory of those I heard. But if I did not see these things in the <term>inner light</term> <note>In mystical tradition, the "inner light" refers to a direct, God-given understanding that transcends the physical senses and human reason.</note>, what you have written there would not give me the slightest idea of them.”

> “Everything that is lowly and insignificant in my words,” she told me, “you have written; but the <term>precious substance</term> <note>Angela suggests that Arnald is only capturing the literal, "lowly" words, while the actual spiritual essence—the "soul-thing"—is lost in the process of writing.</note>, the matter of the soul, you have not said a word of it.”

You see what kind of man I am. A thousand things were lost through my incapacity. I was there like an idiot, listening and not understanding. For example, I did not add a single word of my own.

It is intelligence that I lack. Sometimes I was unable to follow her speech while writing, and in the moment that followed, time or memory failed me to restore the text.

A thousand other causes also altered my work. Sometimes I would go to her with a <term>troubled conscience</term> <note>Arnald believes that his own spiritual state—feeling guilty or distracted—directly hindered his ability to perceive and record Angela's divine revelations.</note>; in such cases, all my work was a complete failure. I could not write two words with any consistency. I then took the habit of resorting, before approaching Angela, to the <term>sacrament</term> <note>This most likely refers to the Sacrament of Penance (Confession). Arnald sought to "clear" his conscience through confession to become a more worthy instrument for recording Angela's words.</note>

<summary>Brother Arnald records Angela’s harsh critiques of his writing, where she claims his transcriptions lack the "flavor" and "substance" of her divine experiences. Arnald confesses his own inadequacy, explaining that he eventually used the sacrament of confession to help him focus on his task.</summary>

# SECOND PROLOGUE OF BROTHER ARNALD

...of penance. It seems to me that after <term>absolution</term> <note>The formal forgiveness of sins by a priest, which Arnald believed cleared his mind and spirit to better receive Angela's words.</note> I was less incapable of hearing and reproducing her words: I felt the help of grace.

As it is, my work lacks order. And yet, knowing myself as I do, I find it marvelous to have accomplished even the little that you see here. The order that is found within it, however insufficient it may be, is due to supernatural help.

One of the great anxieties, one of the great sorrows of my life, is not to have succeeded more fully. And yet I felt, through the merits of my <term>blessed mother</term> <note>Arnald refers to Angela of Foligno as his spiritual mother, a common practice in Franciscan discipleship.</note>, I felt a spiritual grace, absolutely unknown and without precedent in my life.

I bear this witness to myself: that I have included nothing of my own; I affirm that I have not added a single word. I have set down the few words that I understood, but I have included nothing else.

Terrified by my daunting <term>ministry</term> <note>Arnald uses the word "ministry" (ministerium) to describe his role as scribe, viewing it as a sacred duty rather than a mere clerical task.</note>, I wrote with great trembling.

Often I had her repeat the word I was to write several times. I tried to reproduce the very words she had used, for fear of altering the idea by altering the expression. Sometimes <term>Angela</term> <note>Saint Angela of Foligno (c. 1248–1309), the Italian mystic whose revelations form the core of this book.</note> would say, upon rereading my work:

> “I would repent having disclosed these things, had I not heard these words: ‘The more you give light, the more you shall keep it.’”

<summary>Brother Arnald explains that any clarity in his writing is due to divine grace rather than his own skill, emphasizing his strict adherence to Angela's exact words. He describes the spiritual burden of his role as her scribe and Angela’s own reassurance regarding the sharing of her visions.</summary>

# SECOND PROLOGUE OF BROTHER ARNALD

> “Listen well,” she said again, “listen well, Brother Arnald. The voice of Heaven has commanded me several times to have written at the end of each chapter: ‘*Let the reader give thanks to God, since this chapter is written.*’” <note>original: "Que le lecteur rende grâces à Dieu, puisque ce chapitre est écrit." This phrase underscores the belief that the text itself was a gift of divine grace.</note>

Three <term>leagues</term> <note>A league is an old unit of distance; three leagues from Assisi to Foligno is approximately nine miles.</note> from Assisi, in Foligno, lived a woman who had just been converted. She had a husband and children. She entered into the way of an extraordinary <term>penance</term> <note>Acts of self-mortification or devotion performed to express sorrow for sin and a desire to reform one's life.</note>; I have the proof of it. Furthermore, she suffered in her soul and in her body temptations and torments. She suffered invisibly certain tortures to which several other souls have been subjected visibly. She suffered cruelly, for the demons know how to torture much better than men. A man worthy of trust <note>Arnald often refers to himself or other reliable witnesses in the third person to emphasize the objectivity of the testimony.</note> fell one day into a terrible astonishment because he had heard from Angela's mouth the tortures that her infernal enemy made her undergo. This man had a divine <term>revelation</term> <note>In this context, a supernatural communication or insight granted by God to confirm a spiritual truth.</note> which confirmed to him the reality of the fact. It is impossible to say by what compassion he was touched.

Angela was deep and ardent in prayer, very wise in confession. One day she confessed to me all the sins of her life with such a perfection of knowledge, such deep discernment, with such <term>contrition</term> <note>The state of feeling remorseful and penitent; in Catholic teaching, "perfect contrition" is sorrow for sin arising from the love of God rather than fear of punishment.</note>, with such tears, and these tears did not cease to flow for an instant from the first to the last—

# SECOND PROLOGUE OF BROTHER ARNALD

...words, with such power of humility, that I wept in my heart:
> “O my God,” I said, “Lord my God, even if You were to abandon the whole world to error, You would never allow such sincerity, such truthfulness, and such righteousness to be deceived!”

The following night, she was sick unto death. The next morning, she dragged herself with great difficulty to the church of the Brothers; I said <term>Mass</term> <note>The central act of worship in the Catholic Church, culminating in the sacrament of the Eucharist.</note> and gave her <term>communion</term> <note>The reception of the consecrated bread and wine, believed to be the body and blood of Christ.</note>. I know that she never received communion without receiving some immense grace, and each time a new grace. Such was the power of the <term>illuminations</term> <note>Spiritual "light" or sudden divine understandings granted to the soul during prayer.</note>, the illustrations, and the joys with which her soul was intoxicated, that all of this overflowed at every moment onto her body. Very often, when I wanted to read back to her what I had written under her dictation, <term>ecstasy</term> <note>original: "ravissement." A mystical state where the soul is "carried away" by God, often resulting in a trance-like loss of external senses.</note> would seize her, and she no longer heard a word. When she conversed with the Lord, joy gave Angela another face and another body; the delight of the Holy Spirit set her flesh on fire: I saw her eyes burning like the altar lamp; I saw her face resemble a purple rose.

Her head had, at times, an angelic richness, a fullness of life, a splendor, and a magnificence that raised her above the...

## SECOND PROLOGUE OF BROTHER ARNALD

...human condition; she would then forget to eat and drink; one would have said she was a spirit without a body, and yet her body was dazzling.

She had as a companion a Christian virgin who lived with her; this woman told me that one day she was on the road with Angela. I do not know where they were going. Suddenly, along the path, Angela’s head became resplendent, and her cheeks changed color; transfigured by joy, she no longer bore any resemblance to her usual self. Her eyes, larger than ordinary, were dazzling to look upon. Her companion was an extraordinarily simple <note>original: "naïve." In a medieval context, this implies a person of pure heart but little worldly or theological education.</note> woman, who, at that time, was not yet acquainted with the **thunderbolts of God** <note>original: "coups de foudre de Dieu." These represent sudden, overwhelming, and often physical strikes of divine grace.</note> and the habits of Angela. Ignorant of all this, this good woman was afraid of meeting someone. In the excess of her simplicity, she covered her own head.

> “Do as I do,” she said to Angela; “cover yourself, cover yourself. Do you not know then that your eyes are like two **candelabras**?” <note>Large, multi-branched candle-holders; she is describing Angela's eyes as emitting a literal, blinding light.</note>

> “But what is it, what is it that has happened to you! From now on, hide yourself from men. Oh! What is to become of us!”

## SECOND PROLOGUE OF BROTHER ARNALD

> ...encounter someone, God will watch over the encounter.”

Her companion eventually became accustomed to it, for Angela’s **transfiguration** <note>A change in form or appearance into a more beautiful or spiritual state; here, it refers to Angela’s face physically shining with divine light.</note> occurred at every moment. One day—I have this fact from the same person—Angela was lying down and in **ecstasy** <note>A state of mystical trance where the soul is believed to be caught up in a vision of God, often leaving the body motionless.</note>. Her friend saw a magnificent star on her side which, without being very large, combined an immense number of dazzling colors. Then it cast out rays of unheard-of beauty, some very fine, others thicker: they came out from Angela’s heart, folded back toward it, and then rose up to heaven. This phenomenon lasted for three hours.

When Angela was tormented by temptation, or seized by the **languors of love** <note>original: "langueurs d'amour." In medieval mysticism, this describes a physical wasting away or exhaustion caused by the intense, overwhelming desire for God.</note>, she would grow pale and waste away on her feet; she was a pitiable sight.

This woman had a weak body.

I, Brother Arnald, after having written this book, prayed that Angela ask God if I had written anything false or useless. I felt the need for God himself, in his mercy, to tell me if I had not been mistaken.

She replied:
> “I have asked God several times if in what I have said and in what you have written there was any falsehood or uselessness. Now, here is the answer...”

> “All of this, the Lord had said, is according to my will; all of this comes from me, and I will set my seal <note>original: "Sigillabo"</note> upon this book.” And as Angela did not understand this word: “I will set my seal,” the voice spoke again, and used another word: “I will confirm <note>original: "Firmabo"</note> my word.”

I, Brother Arnald, who wrote under her dictation, repeat that I have added nothing, but that I have omitted much; I have omitted many things too high to enter into my miserable understanding.

By the will of God, my book has been examined by two <term>Friars Minor</term> <note>The "Lesser Brothers," commonly known today as the Franciscans; the religious order founded by St. Francis of Assisi.</note> worthy of trust; they examined it in the company of Angela; they themselves heard what I wrote; they conferred on all these things with Angela herself in order to have more certain information. It was the Lord James of Colonna <note>Giacomo Colonna (c. 1250–1318), a powerful Italian Cardinal and protector of the Franciscan order who took a keen interest in Angela's revelations.</note> who took charge of it. ## SECOND PROLOGUE OF BROTHER ARNALD

...readers, inquisitors, and <term>custodes</term> <note>Provincial superiors or guardians responsible for several convents within the Franciscan order.</note>. They were all worthy of trust, modest, and spiritual. Not one of them attacked a single word of the book. They did nothing but humbly venerate and tenderly embrace it.

I urge the reader not to be surprised if the ardent words of love fill this book. Holy Scripture is full of them as well. The *Song of Songs* <note>original: "Cantique des Cantiques"; a book of the Old Testament often interpreted by mystics as an allegory of the love between God and the soul.</note> stands there to attest to it. The reader will feel, moreover, that in the midst of her ecstasies and sublimities, divine grace preserved Angela so perfectly from pride that the very height of these revelations only served to deepen the humility of her soul.

I have one more observation to make.

Angela declares several times, in the midst of her ecstasies and transformations, that she is raised forever to a new state of light, of joy, and of delight, and that this joy will be eternal.

This is, I believe, the sense in which these words should be understood:

A new divine <term>illustration</term> <note>In mystical theology, an "illustration" refers to a spiritual enlightenment or a "shedding of light" where God grants the soul a sudden, direct understanding of divine truths.</note> establishes her in a new state of divine transformation. This state is continuous. She enters into a new light, into a new feeling of God. She enters into a solitude that she has not yet inhabited.

<summary>Brother Arnald records the formal approval of Angela's revelations by Franciscan authorities and defends the work's passionate language by comparing it to the Song of Songs. He also explains Angela's "eternal joy" as a permanent state of spiritual transformation and divine light.</summary>

# SECOND PROLOGUE OF BROTHER ARNALD

Although this dwelling <note>original: "demeure"; referring here to a spiritual state or a "living in" God.</note> is permanent, and does not take on the appearance of an interrupted act, it is nevertheless capable of ever-new increases. Angela finds within it, at every moment, new ardors, new joys, new impressions, and new <term>sweetnesses</term> <note>original: "suavités"; a common term in mystical literature for the delightful sensory or emotional feelings that accompany the perception of the divine presence.</note>; and yet it is always the same <term>enlightenment</term> <note>original: "illustration"; as noted previously, this refers to the direct shedding of divine light upon the soul to grant understanding.</note> that continues, in regard to its unchanging principle. 

The transformation in itself is not a fleeting act; it is continuous, like a <term>habit</term> <note>original: "habitude"; used here in the theological sense of a "habitus"—a stable, lasting disposition of the soul—rather than a mere repetitive behavior.</note>; but <term>transports</term> <note>original: "transports"; moments of being "carried away" or ecstatic raptures of the soul toward God.</note> that are more and more sublime—sweetnesses, enlightenments, and visions that are higher and higher—can be produced within her and through her.

# ANGELA OF FOLIGNO

---

->"I," said Angela of Foligno <note>A 13th-century Italian mystic and member of the Third Order of Saint Francis, known for her "Memorial" of spiritual experiences</note>, "entering into the way of penance <term>penance: a religious practice involving repentance and self-discipline to seek forgiveness for sins</term>, took eighteen steps before knowing the imperfection of life."<-

---

## FIRST STEP

### ANGELA BECOMES AWARE OF HER SINS

I looked at my sins for the first time, and I acquired knowledge of them; my soul entered into fear; it trembled because of its damnation, and I wept, I wept much.

---

## SECOND STEP

### CONFESSION

Then I blushed for the first time, and such was my shame that I drew back from the confession <note>original: "aveu" — meaning a formal admission or acknowledgment of sins</note>. I did not confess, I did not dare to admit, and <meta>catchword: and</meta>

-># ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-

I went to the holy table <note>the "holy table" refers to the altar where the Eucharist, or Holy Communion, is received</note>, and it was with my sins that I received the body of Jesus Christ. That is why neither day nor night did my conscience cease to grumble. I prayed to Saint Francis to let me find the confessor I needed, someone who could understand and to whom I could speak. That same night, the old man <note>referring to Saint Francis of Assisi, who appeared to her in a vision</note> appeared to me. "My sister," he said, "if you had called upon me sooner, I would have heard you sooner. What you ask is done."

In the morning, I found a friar preaching in the church of Saint Felician <note>San Feliciano, the patron saint and cathedral of Foligno, Italy</note>.

After the sermon, I resolved to confess to him. I confessed fully; I received absolution <term>absolution: the formal release from guilt or punishment for sin, pronounced by a priest</term>. I did not feel love; only bitterness, shame, and sorrow.

## THIRD STEP

### SATISFACTION

I persevered in the penance <term>penance: an act of self-mortification or devotion performed to show sorrow for sins</term> that was imposed upon me; I tried to satisfy justice <note>the theological concept of making restitution to God for the "debt" of sin</note>, void of consolation, full of sorrow.

## FOURTH STEP

### CONTEMPLATION OF MERCY

I cast my first gaze upon divine mercy; I became acquainted with that mercy which had rescued me from hell, and which had granted me the grace I am now recounting. I received its first illumination <term>illumination: in mystical theology, a moment of sudden spiritual clarity or insight granted by God</term>; my pain and tears redoubled. Thus enlightened, I perceived in myself nothing but faults; I saw with absolute certainty that I had deserved hell. I groaned in bitterness, and I pronounced my own condemnation.

## SIXTH STEP

### SHE RECOGNIZES HER GUILT TOWARD ALL CREATURES

An illumination <term>illumination: a flash of spiritual clarity or divine insight</term> gave me a sight of my sins in their true depth. Here I understood that by offending the Creator, I had offended all creatures, for they were all made for me <note>In the medieval worldview, the physical world was often seen as created for the use and spiritual benefit of humanity; thus, sinning was viewed as a betrayal of the purpose of all creation.</note>. All my sins came back vividly to my memory, and in the confession I made to God, I weighed them most profoundly. Through the Blessed Virgin and all the saints I invoked the mercy of God, and feeling myself dead <note>In mystical writing, "feeling dead" refers to the spiritual state of being severed from God's grace by sin.</note>, I begged on my knees for life. And I implored all the creatures whom I felt I had offended not to speak up to accuse me before God. Suddenly, I seemed to feel the pity of all creatures and the pity of all the saints upon me. And then I received a gift: it was a great fire of love, and the power to pray as I had never prayed before.

# THE SEVENTH STEP

## THE SIGHT OF THE CROSS

At this point, I received the special grace of gazing upon the cross, where I contemplated—with the eyes of both the heart and the body—Jesus Christ dead for us. But this vision was tasteless <note>In mystical theology, "tasteless" or "insipid" refers to a state of spiritual aridity where one perceives a divine truth but does not yet feel the "sweetness" or emotional consolation of God's presence.</note>, although it was very painful.

# THE EIGHTH STEP

## THE KNOWLEDGE OF JESUS CHRIST

Along with the gaze upon the cross, I received a deeper knowledge of the way in which Jesus Christ had died for our sins. I felt a very harsh awareness of my own sins, and I realized that I myself was the cause <note>original: "l’auteur"; literally "the author" or "the one responsible."</note> of the crucifixion. However, I did not yet suspect the immensity of the blessing of the cross. I did not yet penetrate the *how* of these things: my salvation, my conversion, His death. That depth of understanding <term>understanding: here referring to "intellectus," a gift of the Spirit providing direct insight into divine mysteries</term> was given to me only later. ## THE NINTH STEP

I was still with my husband; therefore every insult that was said or done to me had a bitter taste. However, I bore it as best I could. It was then that God willed to take from me my mother, who was a great hindrance to me in going to Him. My husband and my sons also died in a short time. And because, having entered upon the path, I had prayed to God that He would rid me of them all, their death was a great consolation to me <note>To a modern reader, this sounds harsh; however, in the tradition of extreme asceticism, Angela views the loss of her family not with malice, but as a divine removal of worldly attachments that "hindered" her total focus on God.</note>. It was not that I was devoid of compassion; but I thought that after this grace, my heart and my will would always be in the heart of God, and the heart and will of God would always be in my heart.

---

times in sleep, or while awake, crucified. “Look,” he said, “look toward my wounds.” And in an astounding manner he showed me how he had suffered everything for me. This was repeated several times. The visions of the day were more peaceful than those of the night; yet all had the aspect of the most horrific pain. Then, all my sins being presented to my memory, I understood that the author of the scourging <note>Angela uses "scourging" (flagellation) to describe the biblical account of Jesus being whipped by Roman soldiers; she internalizes this by concluding that her own sins were the true cause of his pain.</note> was I. I understood what my sorrow ought to be. I felt a pain such as I had never felt before. He continued still, laying out his <term>Passion</term> <note>Passion: Referring to the short final period in the life of Jesus, including his trial, suffering, and death on the cross.</note> before me, and saying: “What can you do that may repay me?” I wept, I wept, I wept, I sobbed to such a point that I saw my tears burn my flesh; when I saw that I was burning, I went to look for cold water.

# ELEVENTH STEP

## PENANCE

I turned toward a penance too harsh for me to describe; and I strove to practice it. But as it was incompatible with the things of the world <note>Original French: "choses du siècle." In a religious context, "the world" refers to secular life, social status, and material concerns outside of a spiritual calling.</note>, I resolved to leave everything to follow the divine inspiration that pushed me toward the cross. This plan was an astonishing grace, and here is how it was given to me. The desire for poverty came to me, and I feared dying before having been poor: on the other hand, I was besieged by a thousand temptations; I was young, and begging <note>Original French: "mendicité." Seeking alms was a central practice for the early Franciscans, symbolizing total dependence on God.</note> was surrounded by perils and shames. I will have to, I said to myself, die of hunger, die of cold, and die naked: no one in the world will approve of me. At last, God had mercy, and light dawned in my heart; and the illumination was so powerful that it will never be extinguished; I resolved to persevere in my design, even if I should die of hunger, cold, and shame. I resolved to go forward, even if I were certain of all possible evils. I felt that in the midst of them I would die for God, and I made my decision resolutely.

<summary>Angela describes the "Eleventh Step" of her spiritual journey, focusing on her resolve to embrace extreme poverty and penance despite her fears of social shame and physical suffering. This internal conflict is resolved through a powerful divine "illumination" that confirms her path.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Eleventh Step, penance, poverty, divine illumination, asceticism, mendicancy</keywords>

I prayed to the mother of Christ and his evangelist Saint John, by the sorrow they endured <note>This refers to the "Stabat Mater" tradition—the grief shared by Mary and John at the foot of the Cross during the Crucifixion.</note>, to obtain for me a sign that would engrave for eternity in my memory the Passion of Jesus Christ.

# THIRTEENTH STEP

## THE HEART

In the midst of this desire, I was seized by a dream where the Heart of Christ was shown to me, and I heard these words: "Behold the place without lies, the place where all is truth." It seemed to me that this referred to the words of a certain preacher whom I had mocked a great deal <note>Angela often reflected on her past life as a "woman of the world" who had been skeptical or dismissive of religious figures before her conversion.</note>.

He called me and told me to place my lips upon the wound in his side. For the first time, I felt a great consolation, mixed with a great sorrow, for I had the Passion <note>The "Passion" refers to the physical and spiritual suffering of Jesus Christ in the final hours of his life, from the Agony in the Garden to the Crucifixion.</note> before my very eyes. And I prayed to the Lord that I might shed my blood for him just as he had shed his for me. I desired for each of my limbs a suffering and a death more terrible and more shameful than his own. I meditated, searching for someone who would be willing to kill me; I wanted only to die for the faith, for his love, and since he had died upon a cross, I asked to die elsewhere, and by a viler instrument <note>Angela expresses a common theme in medieval mysticism: the "holy envy" of the saints, where she feels so unworthy that she desires a death even more degrading than that of the martyrs to prove her love.</note>. I felt unworthy of the death of the martyrs; I wanted one that was viler and more cruel. But I could not imagine one shameful enough to satisfy me, nor different enough from the death of the saints, whom I felt myself unworthy to resemble.

## THE FIFTEENTH STEP

### MARY AND JOHN

I fixed my desire upon the Virgin and Saint John; they dwelt in my memory, and I begged them—by the sorrow they received on the day of the Passion <note>The "Passion" refers to the suffering and crucifixion of Jesus. In medieval devotion, the shared grief of Mary and John is considered a "compassionate" martyrdom of the heart.</note>—to obtain for me the pains of Jesus Christ, or at least those pains which were given to them. They acquired and obtained this favor for me, and Saint John overwhelmed me with it so much one day, that this day counts among the most terrible of my life. I glimpsed, in a moment of light, that the compassion of Saint John in the presence of Jesus and Mary made of him more than a martyr. From this came a new desire to strip myself of everything with a full will. The demon opposed this; men did as well, all those from whom I took counsel, without excepting the Friars Minor <term>Friars Minor: Literally "Lesser Brothers," the religious order founded by St. Francis of Assisi. Angela was a member of the Third Order, which was for laypeople living in the world.</term>; but neither all the riches nor all the evils of the world combined could have prevented me from giving my fortune to the poor, or at least from leaving it there, if the means of ridding myself of it otherwise had been taken from me. I felt that I could reserve nothing without offending Him from whom the illumination <note>A sudden, divine clarity or spiritual insight granted by God.</note> came. However, I still remained in bitterness, not knowing if God

## THE SIXTEENTH STEP

### THE LORD’S PRAYER <note>The original French title is *L’Oraison Dominicale*, referring to the "Prayer of the Lord."</note>

Entering a church, I asked God for some grace. I prayed: I said the *Lord’s Prayer* <note>Original: *Pater*. This is the "Our Father," the central prayer of Christian liturgy.</note>; suddenly God wrote the *Lord’s Prayer* with his hand in my heart with such an emphasis on his goodness and my unworthiness, that words fail me to say even a single word about it. Each of the words of the *Lord’s Prayer* expanded in my heart; I said them one after another with great slowness and profound contrition <term>contrition: Sincere and complete remorse for one's sins, motivated by a love of God rather than fear of punishment.</term>, and despite the tears drawn from me by a more vivid knowledge of my faults and my unworthiness, I began to taste something of divine sweetness. Divine goodness made itself felt to me in the *Lord’s Prayer* better than anywhere else, and this impression lasts at the moment I speak. However, as the *Lord’s Prayer* revealed to me—

# EIGHTEENTH AND FINAL STEP

## THE FEELING OF GOD

Here I began to feel God, and seized in prayer by an immense <term>delight</term> <note>delight (French: délectation): In mystical theology, this refers to a profound spiritual joy or pleasure that originates from a sense of the divine presence.</note>, I no longer remembered food, and I would have wished to eat no more so that I might always be standing in prayer. The temptation to no longer eat was mixed with my new state—to eat no more, or to eat too little; but I understood that this was an illusion. Such was the fire in my heart that no genuflection or any <term>penance</term> <note>penance: Voluntary acts of devotion or self-mortification performed to express sorrow for sin.</note> tired me. And yet I was led toward a greater fire and a more burning ardor. Then I could no longer hear mention of God without responding with a cry, and even if I had seen a raised axe above my head, I would not have been able to hold back that cry. This happened to me for the first time on the day I sold my castle to give the proceeds to the poor. It was the best of my properties.

From 그 moment on, when people spoke of God, my cry escaped me, even in the presence of all kinds of people. They believed I was possessed. I do not say the contrary; it is an infirmity <note>infirmity: Angela often uses this term to describe her ecstatic states, suggesting that her spiritual experiences were so powerful they overwhelmed her physical health and social decorum.</note>.

<summary>Angela describes the final step of her conversion, where her internal spiritual fire becomes so intense that she renounces her wealth and begins to cry out uncontrollably at the mention of God.</summary>

# NINETEENTH CHAPTER
## TEMPTATIONS AND SORROWS

---

Lest the greatness and the multitude of revelations and visions should puff me up, lest their delight should exalt me, there was given to me a tempter <note>tempter: Referring to a "thorn in the flesh" or a "messenger of Satan," a common motif in mystical literature where divine favor is balanced by intense trials to ensure humility.</note> of a thousand forms who multiplies around me temptations and pains: pains of the body and pains of the soul. Innumerable torments tear at my body; they come from the demons, who stir them up in a thousand ways. I do not believe that the pains of my body can be expressed. There does not remain a single limb that does not suffer horribly. I am never without pain and without languor <note>languor (French: langueur): a state of physical or mental weariness, often associated with spiritual longing or physical exhaustion in mystical texts.</note>, always feeble and fragile, to the point of remaining bedridden, full of suffering. I do not have a single limb that is not struck, twisted, and afflicted by the demons. I am weak, swollen, and filled in all my limbs with a painful sensitivity. I move only with the greatest difficulty; I am weary of the bed, and I cannot eat enough.

<summary>Angela explains that her divine visions are balanced by extreme physical and spiritual torments, which she believes are inflicted by demons to prevent her from becoming prideful.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, humility, temptation, demonic torment, physical illness, mysticism, spiritual trials</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 55, Angela shifts from describing the physical pains inflicted by demons to the even more agonizing "torments of the soul," using the vivid and terrifying metaphor of a blindfolded person hanging from a gallows to illustrate her spiritual isolation.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>
->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-

As for the torments of the soul, which are incomparably more numerous and more terrible, the demons inflict them upon me almost without ceasing. I can compare myself to nothing better than a man hanging by the neck who—with hands tied behind his back and eyes covered with a veil—would remain attached to the gallows by a rope, and would live there, without help, without remedy, without support. I even believe that what I suffer from the demons is more cruel and more hopeless. The demons have hung my soul; and just as the hanged man has no support, my soul hangs without support, and my faculties <note>faculties (original French: *puissances*): In medieval psychology, these are the "powers" of the soul—typically memory, understanding, and will. Angela feels these internal strengths are being incapacitated.</note> are overturned, in the full sight and knowledge of my spirit. 

When my soul sees this overturning and this abandonment of my faculties without being able to oppose it, such suffering arises that I can hardly weep, due to the excess of pain, rage, and despair; sometimes also I weep without remedy. Sometimes my fury is such that it is a great effort for me not to tear myself to pieces. Sometimes I cannot stop myself from striking myself horribly, to the point that my head and my limbs are swollen. When my soul witnesses the departure and the fall of its faculties, mourning takes hold within it, and I cry out to God, and I scream without ceasing: "My God, my God, do not abandon me!" <note>original French: "Mon Dieu, mon Dieu, ne m’abandonnez pas !" This cry mirrors the words of Jesus on the cross, signaling a state of spiritual "dereliction" where the mystic feels completely forgotten by the Divine.</note>

<summary>Angela describes her intense spiritual suffering using the metaphor of a blindfolded man on a gallows, illustrating a state of total despair where her mental and spiritual faculties fail her, leading to physical outbursts and desperate prayers.</summary>
