I suffer another torment: it is the return, at least seemingly, of former vices <note>vices: In the medieval context, these are moral failings or habits of sin, such as pride, greed, or lust.</note>. It is not that they truly subject my soul to their rule, but they torture me cruelly. Even the vices I never had enter into me, ignite, and tear me apart. But they do not live forever, and their death gives me great joy. I am delivered to numerous demons who resurrect within me the vices I once had, and produce others that I never possessed. But when I remember that God was afflicted, despised, and poor, I would wish to see all my evils redoubled.

Sometimes, there occurs a frightful and infernal darkness where all hope disappears, and this night is horrible. And the vices that I feel are dead in my soul are resurrected in my body; but the demons awaken them outside of the soul, and excite others which were never there. I then suffer particularly in three places of the body: the fire of concupiscence <note>concupiscence (original French: *concupiscence*): A technical theological term for a strong, often irrational desire for sensory or worldly pleasures, particularly sexual ones, which is seen as a source of temptation.</note> is such in those moments that, before having received a prohibition against it, I would burn myself with physical fire in the hope of extinguishing the other. Ah! I would much rather be burned alive! I cry out, I call for death—death of any kind—and I say to God: "If I am damned, well then! Let it be immediately:

And then, I understand that these vices <note>vices: In Angela’s writing, these are not just bad habits, but intense spiritual and physical temptations that feel like a return to a sinful life.</note> are not in the soul, because the soul never consents to them, and it is the body that suffers this violence. Weariness <note>weariness (original French: *l'ennui*): A profound, agonizing spiritual listlessness or "heaviness of heart" often described by mystics during times of trial.</note> joins with pain, and if it were to last, the body could not endure it. The soul sees itself stripped of its powers, and though it does not consent to the vices, it sees itself without strength against them: it sees between God and itself a terrifying contradiction; it sees its fall and feels its martyrdom.

A vice that I never had comes into me by a special permission <note>permission: A theological concept where God "allows" a soul to be tempted by demons or their own nature to test their virtue and humility.</note>: I clearly feel and know that it arrives by permission. It surpasses, I believe, all others; the virtue through which I fight it is a manifest gift from the God who delivers, and if I were to doubt God, amidst the ruin of all my beliefs, this felt gift would restore my faith. There is in this an assured, tranquil hope, and doubt is impossible; strength prevails; the vice is overcome; this strength holds me suspended above the abyss. Such is this strength and such is the power communicated by it, that all men, all demons, and all the tricks of earth and hell cannot obtain from me the slightest movement <note>movement: Referring to a movement of the will, meaning the temptation cannot make her choose to sin.</note>, and it is this strength that preserves my faith. And yet, this vice that I do not dare

<summary>Angela reflects on how the soul remains pure even when the body is racked by unwanted temptations, viewing her ability to resist as a divine gift. She describes a state where, despite feeling abandoned by God, she is supernaturally held above the "abyss" of despair.</summary>

<keywords>mysticism, soul and body, temptation, divine grace, spiritual warfare, faith, Angela of Foligno</keywords>

<page-num>59</page-num>

->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-

...naming [this vice] <note>original: "nommer." Angela often avoids specifically naming the temptations she faces—likely lust or pride—to prevent the reader from being scandalized or to avoid dwelling on the sin itself.</note> affects me so cruelly that if divine power hides for a moment and threatens to leave me, no power, no shame, and no punishment would stop me from rushing toward it. But divine power arrives and delivers me: all the goods and all the evils of this world can no longer do anything against it. And I have suffered in this way for more than two years!

Within my soul, a certain humility and a certain pride fight painfully against each other, and I feel a loathing for all these things. This kind of humility—which shows me stripped of every good, cast out from every virtue and every grace, which shows me the multitude of vices and emptiness within myself—robs me of all hope and hides all mercy from me. I then see myself as the house of the devil, his dupe, his daughter, and his agent, cast out from all rectitude <note>rectitude: A state of moral uprightness or being "straight" with God’s law.</note> and all truth, worthy of the lowest depths of the nethermost hell. This miserable humility is not like the other, the true one, which crushes the soul under the weight of felt divine goodness. False humility brings all evils in its wake. Swallowed up by it, I see myself surrounded by demons; in my soul and in my body I see only defects: God is closed off to me; power and grace, everything is hidden. Even the memory of the Lord is forbidden to me; seeing myself damned, I worry only about my crimes, which I...

I contemplate the superabundance of my iniquities within the abyss; I seek in vain a way to uncover them and reveal them to the world: I would like to go naked through the cities and the squares, with meats and fish hanging from my neck <note>This striking image represents a desire for public penance. In the Middle Ages, carrying the evidence of one's gluttony—such as meat or fish during a fast—was a way to expose one's secret sins to public shame.</note>, and cry out: "Behold this vile creature, full of malice and lies! Behold the seed of vice, behold the seed of evil!" I performed good deeds in the eyes of men; I caused them to say: "She eats neither fish nor meat." Listen to me: I was a glutton and a drunkard; I pretended to want only the bare necessities; I played at outward poverty. But I made myself a bed with rugs and blankets that I would take away in the morning to hide them from visitors. See the demon of my soul and the malice of my heart! Listen closely: I am hypocrisy, daughter of the devil: I call myself "she who lies"; I call myself "the abomination of God!" I called myself a daughter of prayer <term>prayer: The original French uses "oraison," referring to deep, contemplative mental prayer rather than just reciting standard petitions.</term>, but I was a daughter of wrath, and of hell, and of pride.

<summary>Angela expresses a profound sense of self-loathing and hypocrisy, describing her desire to publicly confess her secret gluttony and the comforts she hid from others while appearing holy.</summary>

Murderer <note>original: "Homicide"</note>, that is my name!

Murderer of souls, murderer of my soul!

Lying in the abyss, I rolled myself at the feet of my brothers—those who are called my sons—and I said to them: “Do not believe me any longer; do not believe me any longer. Do you not see that I am possessed? You who call yourselves my sons, pray to the justice of God so that the demons that have come out of my soul may manifest my acts in all their horror, and that God may no longer be dishonored by me. Do you not see that everything I have told you is a lie? Do you not see that if suddenly the world became empty of malice, I alone would fill it by the superabundance of my own? Do not believe me any longer. Do not worship this idol where the devil is hidden; everything I have told you is a lie, and a diabolical lie. Beseech the justice of God so that the idol may fall and be shattered, so that its diabolical works may be made manifest; for I...

The hidden malice of her whole life has just been revealed by her own self!”

But all of this is a small thing, and nothing is enough. Here is a new despair, an unknown despair. I have absolutely despaired of God and of all his gifts. It is finished; it is settled, settled between him and me. I have the certainty that in the entire world, hell has no prey as perfect as I am; all the graces of God, all his favors—all of these serve only to exacerbate my despair and my hell! Oh! This is what I am forced to say in the abyss of debasement. And then pride arrives!

And I am made of nothing but anger, nothing but pride, nothing but sadness, bitterness, and inflation <term>inflation: "enflure" in the original French/Latin; a spiritual state where the ego feels bloated or puffed up with self-importance even amidst suffering</term>. The good things God has done for me turn into infinite bitterness in my soul. They are of no use to me! They provide no remedy! They only provoke a painful wonder that feels like an insult to my despair! Why is there always this void of virtue within me? Why has God permitted this? And then I doubt and I say to myself: Could he have deceived me? This temptation closes off and hides every good thing. Anger, pride, sadness, bitterness, inflation, and pain—speech can express nothing of all this. Even if all the wise men of the world and all the saints of paradise were to overwhelm me with their consolations and their promises, and even God himself—

Ah! If I could trade these tortures for all the evils of the world, and take upon myself all the infirmities and all the pains that are in the bodies of all men, I would believe all of those to be lighter and lesser. I have said it often: let my torments be traded for martyrdom, no matter what kind!

My torments began some time before the pontificate of Pope Celestine <note>Pope Celestine V, a hermit elected to the papacy who famously abdicated in 1294.</note> (1294); they lasted more than two years, and their attacks were frequent. I am not yet perfectly healed, though their reach is now light, and only external. The situation having changed, I understand that the soul, crushed between bad humility <term>bad humility: A state of destructive self-loathing or despairing shame, which Angela distinguishes from "true" humility.</term> and pride, undergoes an immense purgation <term>purgation: The spiritual process of cleansing the soul of its attachments and ego through suffering.</term>, through which I have acquired the true humility without which there is no salvation. And the greater the humility, the greater the purgation of the soul. Between humility and pride, my soul passes through martyrdom and passes through fire. Through the knowledge of its voids and its faults...

# TWENTIETH CHAPTER

## PILGRIMAGE

---

Blessed be God, even the Father of our Lord Jesus, who consoles us in all tribulation <note>original: "Béni soit Dieu et le Père de Notre-Seigneur Jésus..." This is a paraphrase of 2 Corinthians 1:3-4, a common opening for spiritual testimonies.</note>.

Yes, he deigned to console the sinner in all her tribulation. After the eighteenth step <note>Eighteenth step: Angela’s spiritual autobiography is structured around thirty "steps" or stages of her conversion and mystical ascent.</note>, where the name of God made me cry out, and after the illumination brought to me by the **Lord’s Prayer** <note>original: "Pater." Angela describes a specific moment in her journey where the words of the "Our Father" took on a profound, overwhelming meaning.</note>, I felt the sweetness of God, and this is how it happened. I considered the union of humanity and divinity in Jesus Christ. Absorbed in this vision, drinking in contemplation and delight, I obeyed inspirations in my soul prompted by a divine attraction. It was, at that time, the greatest joy of my life. For most of the day, I remained standing in my cell, lost <note>original: "abîmée." This word suggests being "engulfed" or "plunged into an abyss," echoing the theme of the "abyss" from the previous page, but here it is an abyss of light rather than nothingness.</note> in prayer, shut away, alone, and struck with wonder. And my heart received the blow of joy so strongly that I fell to the ground, unable to speak. My companion <note>Companion: Referring to Masazuola, a woman who lived with Angela and assisted her during her life as a penitent.</note> ran to me, became distressed, and thought I was dead; but she only annoyed me and was an obstacle to me.

<vocab>Angela of Foligno, pilgrimage, Lord's Prayer, Jesus Christ, contemplation, delight</vocab>

<summary>Angela describes a moment of intense mystical ecstasy following her meditation on the "Our Father," where she became so overwhelmed by the joy of God's presence that she collapsed, physically unable to speak.</summary>
<keywords>mystical ecstasy, eighteenth step, Lord's Prayer, Jesus Christ, divine union, contemplation, Masazuola</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 66, Angela describes the moments leading up to her famous pilgrimage to Assisi. She moves from internal meditation on the Lord’s Prayer to a direct dialogue with the divine, centered on the total renunciation of her worldly goods.</meta>

<page-num>67</page-num>

->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-

One day, amid persistent prayer, before I had given everything away—though very little remained—during an evening prayer devoid of divine feeling, I lamented and cried out to God: “All that I do, I do to find you. Will I find you when I have finished?” The answer came. “What do you want?” it said. “Neither gold, nor silver, nor the whole world; only you.” — “Act then and hurry; when you have finished, the whole Trinity <note>Trinity: The Christian doctrine of God as three persons in one substance (Father, Son, and Holy Spirit). For Angela, the "indwelling of the Trinity" represents the highest state of mystical union.</note> will come into you.” I received many other promises; I was torn away from all sorrow, and I was dismissed with divine sweetness. Then I waited for the fulfillment. When I told the event to my companion <note>original: "compagne." Likely referring to Masazuola, her friend and witness.</note>, I expressed some doubt because of the greatness of the promises; however, the sweetness of the farewell sustained my hope.

It was then that I made the pilgrimage of Saint Francis <note>Saint Francis: Francis of Assisi (d. 1226), the founder of the Franciscan Order. Angela was a member of the Third Order of St. Francis (lay penitents).</note> to Assisi, and it was during the journey that the promise was fulfilled. Yet I had not given everything to the poor. Very little remained, in truth; but the death of a holy man, who had taken charge of my affairs, had delayed the final phase. This man, converted by me, also wished to give everything away; while he was coming and going on this business, he died on the road. His burial is honored and illustrated by miracles.

Let us return to myself. I was thus making my pilgrimage: I prayed on the way, asking, among other things, of the blessed Francis for the faithful observance of his rule <note>Rule: The Rule of Saint Francis, a set of guidelines for living in poverty, chastity, and obedience. Angela belonged to the "Third Order," intended for laypeople living in the world.</note>, to which I had just bound myself; I asked to live and die in poverty.

I had already gone to Rome to ask the blessed Saint Peter for the grace and freedom necessary to be truly poor <note>Angela’s trip to Rome was a quest for the "privilege of poverty," a formal recognition that allowed Franciscans to live without any property or possessions.</note>. Through the merits of Saint Peter and Saint Francis, I received, with a palpable certainty, the gift of true poverty. I had arrived at that cave beyond which one climbs to Assisi by a narrow path. I was there when I heard a voice that said:

> “You have prayed to my servant Francis; but I wanted to send you another missionary, the Holy Spirit. I am the Holy Spirit, it is I who come, and I bring you unknown joy. I am going to enter into the depths of you, and lead you near my servant.
>
> “I will speak to you throughout the whole journey; my word will be uninterrupted and I defy you to listen to another, for I have bound you, and I will not let you go until you have returned here a second time, and I will only let you go then in relation to this joy of today; but as for the rest, never, never, if you love me.”

And he provoked me to love, and he said:

...can love; God asks nothing of the soul but love; *for he himself loves without falsehood, and he himself is the love of the soul.*" Weigh these last words; weigh them. They are profound.

That God is the love of the soul, he made me feel through a vivid representation of his <term>Passion</term> <note>The physical and spiritual suffering of Jesus leading up to and during his crucifixion.</note>, and of his cross that he carried for us; He, the immense; He, the glorious, he explained his Passion to me and everything he did for us, and he added: "Look closely; do you find in me anything that is not love?" And my soul understood with clarity that there is nothing in Him that is not love. He complained of finding in these times few people in whom he could deposit his grace, and he promised to give to his new friends, if he could find any, even greater graces than those given to the people of old. And he continued: "O my dear daughter, love me; for I love you much more than you love me. Love me, my beloved; I love with an immense love the soul who loves me without guile <note>original: "sans malice." In this context, it refers to a soul that is simple, sincere, and free from deceitful intentions.</note>." And he wanted the soul, according to its power and capacity, to love him with the same love, with the love that he has for it, promising to give himself, if only the soul desires it. And he said always: "O my beloved, O my spouse, love me! Eat, drink, sleep; your whole life will please me, as long as you love me!" He added: "I will do..."

Doubt took hold of me, and my soul said to the One who was speaking: "If you were the Holy Spirit, you would not say these improper <note>original: "inconvenantes." I made every effort to conceive a feeling of pride; but all my sins returning to my memory, I felt such a humility as never before in all my life. I tried to find distractions; I looked curiously at the vineyards <note>Angela is traveling through the Italian countryside toward Assisi; the "vineyards along the road" are a literal detail of her physical journey occurring simultaneously with her internal vision.</note> along the road. I tried to escape the discourse being held with me; but wherever my eye wandered, the voice always said: "Look, contemplate; this is my creature <note>A term used to describe any living or created thing—plants, animals, or people—emphasizing that they owe their existence to the Creator.</note>." And I felt a sweetness, an ineffable <note>Too great or extreme to be expressed or described in words; a common term in mystical writing to describe the encounter with the Divine.</note> sweetness.

I was so loved, the voice said, that the Son of God and of the Virgin Mary had bent down toward me to speak to me. And Jesus Christ me...

> "I, Jesus Christ, who speak to you and who have spoken to you, I give you this sign that it is truly I; I give you the cross and the love of God; I give them to you for eternity."

I felt the cross of love in my soul, and it overflowed onto my body; I felt the cross physically, and my soul was liquefied <note>In mystical theology, "liquefaction" refers to the soul melting or becoming fluid so that it can be poured into and united with God.</note>. I fell to the ground languishing, and I remained there for eight days.

## TWENTY-FIRST CHAPTER

### BEAUTY

---

One day when I was in prayer <term>prayer: the original term "oraison" refers to a deep, contemplative state of mental prayer often characterized by silence rather than recited petitions</term>, raised in spirit, God spoke to me in peace and in love. I looked and I saw him.

You will ask me what I saw? It was he himself, and I can say nothing else. It was a fullness, it was an interior and filling light for which neither word nor comparison is worth anything. I saw nothing that had a body. He was on that day on earth as in heaven: the beauty that closes the lips <note>This refers to the "ineffability" of God—a common theme in mysticism where the experience is so powerful that it renders the visionary speechless.</note>, the sovereign beauty containing the sovereign good <term>sovereign good: a translation of the theological concept "Summum Bonum," identifying God as the ultimate and highest good from which all other goodness originates</term>. The assembly of the saints stood, singing praises before the supremely beautiful majesty. All this appeared to me in a second. And God said to me: “O my dear daughter, most loving and most beloved, all the saints have a special love for you, all the saints and my Mother, and it is I who will associate you with them.”

Despite the importance of these words, they seemed small to me. What he told me of his Mother and of his saints touched me little. The immensity <note>The text breaks off here at "immensity," suggesting the overwhelming nature of the divine presence which dwarfs even the most holy figures of the Church hierarchy in Angela's immediate experience.</note>

<summary>Angela describes a mystical vision of God as a non-corporeal, overwhelming "sovereign beauty" and "fullness." The experience is so profound that even God’s promise to unite her with the Virgin Mary and the saints feels insignificant compared to the sheer immensity of the divine presence.</summary>

<keywords>Angela of Foligno, sovereign beauty, sovereign good, mystical vision, ineffability, contemplative prayer, assembly of saints, divine light</keywords>

## TWENTY-SECOND CHAPTER

### THE POWER

---

One day I heard a divine voice saying to me: “I who speak to you, I am the divine power, bringing you a divine grace. This grace is as follows: I desire that the mere sight of you shall be beneficial to those who see you. Ah! That is not all! I desire that the thought of you, the memory of you, and your name shall bring help and favor to whoever makes use of them. No one shall think of you in vain. Every soul that remembers you shall receive a grace proportioned to the divine union <term>union divine: the mystical state where the soul feels joined or merged with God</term> that it already possesses.”

I refused, despite my joy, fearing vain glory <note>original: "vaine gloire." In medieval spirituality, vainglory was the dangerous pride one might feel in their own holiness or spiritual gifts.</note>.

But He added:

“You have nothing to gain from this, nothing, in terms of vanity. This glory is not yours; it is a burden that you will carry, and it is nothing else. Keep it; carry it; and restore the glory to its owner <note>The "owner" refers to God; Angela is taught that she is merely a vessel for His work.</note>.”

I understood that I was safe. “And yet—

<summary>In this passage, a divine voice grants Angela the miraculous gift of being a source of grace for others through her presence and name. To prevent the sin of pride, God explains that this spiritual power is a "burden" she must carry as a steward, rather than a personal achievement.</summary>
<keywords>divine power, grace, vainglory, vanity, divine union, spiritual burden, intercession</keywords>

The eyes of my spirit were opened within me; I saw a divine fullness <term>plénitude divine: the absolute totality of God's presence filling all of existence</term> where I embraced the entire universe—on this side and beyond the seas, and the Ocean, and the abyss, and all things—and I saw nothing anywhere but divine power; the mode of this vision was absolutely inexpressible <note>original: "inénarrable." This is a common term in mystical literature to describe an experience that so far transcends human language that it cannot be put into words.</note>. In a transport of wonder, I cried out: “But it is full of God, it is full of God, this universe!” Immediately the universe seemed small to me. I saw the power of God which did not only fill it, but which overflowed on all sides.

“I have shown you,” he said, “something of my power.”

And I understood that, later, I might perhaps receive an even higher understanding of it.

“I have shown you,” he said, “something of my power; now look at my humility.”

I felt in the depths of my soul the inexpressible sweetness of a joy so immense that it will fill my life before it is ever exhausted.

# CHAPTER TWENTY-THREE

## WISDOM

---

One day, a person asked me to pray to God to obtain certain knowledge that they wanted to have. I hesitated; their request seemed to me full of folly and pride.

While I was occupied with this thought, I was caught up in spirit <note>original: "ravie en esprit." This describes a state of mystical ecstasy or "rapture," where the soul is believed to be momentarily detached from the physical body to receive divine revelation.</note>. During this rapture, I was placed near a table without beginning or end; I did not see the table itself, but I saw what was placed upon it. It was a divine fullness, an inexpressible fullness which has no relation to any form of expression; it was the fullness, the Divine Wisdom, and the sovereign good <term>Sovereign Good: Translated from the Latin "Summum Bonum," referring to God as the ultimate, highest good from which all other goodness flows.</term>.

And in the vision of Divine Wisdom, I saw that it is not permitted to question it about certain future and secret paths that it will choose in the time to come; for there is a lack of respect in wanting to walk before it. When I see men devoted to these investigations, their error is visible to me. The mys-

<meta>The text ends mid-word at the bottom of the page: "Le mys-" (likely "Le mystère" or "The mystery").</meta>

<summary>Angela describes a mystical vision of Divine Wisdom as an infinite table of "plenitude," learning that human attempts to pry into God's secret future plans are a mark of pride and disrespect.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Divine Wisdom, mystical vision, spiritual pride, rapture, sovereign good, divine secrets</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from page 83, completing the word "mystery" and concluding Angela's account of her vision of the Divine Table. It explores the permanent change in her perception and the limits of human language in describing mystical experience.</meta>

<page-num>84</page-num>
->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-

-tery that I perceived, under the likeness of an object spread out on a table, has left me with a profound intelligence <note>In this context, "intelligence" refers to a supernatural gift of understanding or spiritual discernment (the "gift of counsel") rather than mere intellectual ability.</note> which discerns, at the first word I hear, people and spiritual things. I no longer judge as I once did with my old judgment, which was error and sin. I judge with a true judgment, which allows me to glimpse the flaw of my old judgment. I cannot recount this vision; for *the table* is the only sensible object <note>A "sensible object" is something that can be perceived by the physical senses (sight, touch, etc.), as opposed to the purely abstract or spiritual realities that made up the rest of the vision.</note> whose idea or name was presented to my mind. As for the mystery of the vision itself, it escapes speech.

# TWENTY-FOURTH CHAPTER

## JUSTICE

---

One day, I was in prayer <note>The original French 'oraison' refers to a deep, contemplative form of prayer common in the mystical tradition.</note>; I asked questions, not to resolve a doubt, but because I was burning to know more about God, and I said to him:
> "Why did You create man? Why did You permit his fall? Why the Passion <note>The 'Passion' refers to the physical and spiritual suffering of Jesus Christ leading up to and including his crucifixion.</note> of Your Son, when You had so many other instruments in Your hands to redeem <note>To 'redeem' here means to save or 'buy back' humanity from the state of sin.</note> us?"

I felt with absolute certainty that God could have indeed given us life and saved us in another way. I felt driven and compelled to ask these questions. In that moment, I would have wanted to settle into pure and simple prayer <note>This refers to a state of quietude or wordless contemplation, as opposed to the active, intellectual questioning she describes here.</note>; but God forced me to question Him. I remained in this state for several days, always questioning, and yet the questions did not arise from doubt. I understood that God had chosen the path most suited to His goodness and to our needs; but this was not enough, for I saw clearly that He could have acted in an entirely different way.

<vocab>Angela of Foligno, prayer, Justice, God, Passion, redeem, fall, goodness</vocab>

<summary>Angela describes a period of contemplative questioning where she asks God why He chose the suffering of the Passion for humanity's redemption when other means were possible. She emphasizes that her inquiry stems from a desire for deeper knowledge rather than a lack of faith.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Divine Justice, Redemption, the Fall, the Passion of Christ, contemplative prayer, theological inquiry</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the 24th chapter, "Justice," following Angela's intense questioning of God regarding the necessity of Christ's suffering. Having asked why God chose the path of the Passion for human salvation, she now receives her answer through a profound mystical experience that transcends intellectual reasoning.</meta>

->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-
<page-num>86</page-num>

There came a moment when I was caught up in spirit; I saw then that the mystery of His ways is a mystery without beginning or end. Caught up in the immense darkness <note>The "immense darkness" (original French: *immense ténèbre*) refers to the "Divine Dark" or *caligo*, a concept in mystical theology where God’s light is so blindingly bright that it appears as darkness to the limited human intellect.</note>, my soul wished to move back toward itself. Impossible! It wished to go further forward. Impossible! Then, lifted higher, it perceived the <term>unutterable power</term> <note>original: "puissance inénarrable." This technical term refers to the *ineffability* of God, a core belief that the divine essence is beyond the descriptive power of human language.</note>, then the justice of God, His will, His goodness, and I discovered at the heart of them the things I had sought. 

Suddenly my soul was snatched from the immense darkness. While it had been plunged there, my body lay stretched out on the ground; but when the light came, I rose up quickly, standing on the very tips of my toes. The agility of my body was unheard of, and I believed I felt that I was created for the second time. I plunged my gaze with immense joy into the will of God, into His power, His justice, and beyond my hopes, I drank with transport the understanding of the mysteries; but their manifestation is forbidden to words, because they exceed nature.

I knew well that God could have saved us otherwise; but I had never understood how the mode of redemption He chose constitutes the highest manifestation of His goodness toward us, and the most intimate union—that which is made through the mouth, the <term>Eucharistic union</term> <note>Angela describes the Eucharist (the sacrament of Communion) as the ultimate physical and spiritual bridge between the human and the divine.</note>.

<vocab>Angela of Foligno, mystic, rapture, darkness, redemption, Eucharistic union, unutterable</vocab>

<summary>Angela experiences an ecstatic vision of the "immense darkness" where she perceives God's justice and power, physically rising from the ground in a state of supernatural agility. She concludes that while God could have saved humanity in many ways, He chose the Passion and the Eucharist to achieve the most intimate physical and spiritual union possible with mankind.</summary>

On that day, I attained such a knowledge of the justice of God and the rectitude of His judgments, such satisfaction, and such tranquility, that under no circumstances would I experience pain, negligence, or any slackening in prayer. This vision left in my soul a peace, a rest, and a tranquility without parallel—an eternal tranquility. But I have not yet said everything.

After having contemplated the will of God, His power, and His justice, I was carried away to a still greater height where I no longer saw any of those things, and the mode of vision was changed. I saw an eternal, inexpressible unity, about which I can say nothing, except that it is the <term>absolute good</term> <note>original: "le tout bien." In mystical theology, this describes God not merely as a "good thing" among others, but as the very essence of Goodness itself.</note>. And my soul, in the delirium of its joy, no longer distinguished love and instead contemplated the <term>unutterable</term> <note>original: "l’inénarrable." This is a key term in mysticism referring to the *ineffable*, or experiences that transcend the limits of human language.</note>. I had emerged from my first vision and entered into the unutterable: whether with my body or without my body, I am entirely unaware <note>Angela is here using the language of St. Paul from the New Testament (2 Corinthians 12:2) to describe a state of mystical rapture where the physical senses are suspended.</note>. All the states I had known previously were lesser than this one. This vision left within me the death of vices and the security of virtues. I love all good things and all evils, benefits and misdeeds. Nothing breaks the harmony for me. I am in a state of great peace, in great veneration of the divine judgments. Morning and evening, in my prayers, I say:

<summary>Angela describes her transition from a rational understanding of God's justice to a supreme mystical state of "unutterable unity." This experience results in a state of "holy indifference," where she finds divine harmony in both good and evil circumstances.</summary>

By your justice, deliver me, Lord; by your judgments, deliver me, Lord; I have the same confidence and the same delight as when I say: By your advent, deliver me, Lord; by your Nativity, deliver me, Lord; by your Passion, deliver me, Lord <note>Angela is here parodying or expanding upon the "Litany of the Saints," a traditional Catholic prayer. She provocatively suggests that God's "judgments"—which usually imply punishment—are just as much a source of joy and salvation as His birth or His suffering on the cross.</note>. I do not see the goodness of God any more clearly in a saint or in all the saints, than in a damned soul or in all the damned <note>This is one of Angela's most radical mystical insights: that God is so perfectly "Good" that His presence and essence are equally manifest in His justice toward the lost as they are in His mercy toward the holy.</note>. But this <term>abyss</term> <note>original: "abîme." In mystical writing, the "abyss" refers to the unfathomable, bottomless nature of God's being which swallows up all human understanding.</note> was shown to me only once; the memory and the joy it has left me are eternal. If, by some impossible misfortune, all the truths of the faith were to abandon me, there would remain for me, in my shipwreck, a certainty of God, and of His judgments, and of the justice of His judgments.

But, O depth! O depth! O depth! O depth! <note>original: "ô profondeur!" A likely reference to Romans 11:33: "O the depth of the riches both of the wisdom and knowledge of God!"</note> every creature serves the salvation of the <term>predestined</term> <note>predestined: those who, in theological terms, are chosen by God for eternal life. Angela suggests that the entire universe, including the actions of others, is orchestrated for the spiritual benefit of these souls.</note>: This is why the soul, which, having descended into the abyss, has caught a glimpse of the justice of God, will henceforth look upon all creatures as the servants of its glory.

<summary>Angela describes a radical vision where God's justice and judgments are seen as equally beautiful and divine as His mercy, even in the case of the damned. She expresses an unshakable certainty in God's goodness that would survive even the loss of religious dogma.</summary>

# TWENTY-FIFTH CHAPTER

## LOVE

---

It was during <term>Lent</term> <note>The forty-day period of fasting and penitence preceding Easter in the Christian calendar.</note>; I was dry and without love <note>Angela refers here to "spiritual aridity," a state frequent in mystical life where the soul feels empty, cold, and unable to find joy or connection in prayer.</note>. I was praying to God to give me something of Himself; for, as for me, I had nothing. My inner eyes were opened, and I saw love coming toward me. I saw its beginning, but not its end. What I saw had a continuation, without having a limit. Colors would provide me with no term of comparison. When love reached me, I saw it with the eyes of the soul much more clearly than I have ever seen anything with the eyes of the body.

I will say, if you like, that love took on the resemblance of a **scythe** <note>original: "faux." A striking and unusual image for love. While a scythe is usually a tool for harvesting or a symbol of death, Angela uses it to describe the "cutting" and "sweeping" sensation of a force that touches the soul and then recedes.</note> as it touched me. I beg you not to believe that it was a measurable resemblance. But it seemed to me that a sharp instrument touched me, then it withdrew, not penetrating as deeply as it allowed itself to be glimpsed. I was filled with love; I was satiated with an inestimable fullness. But

<summary>Angela describes a mystical vision during Lent where she transitions from spiritual "dryness" to a profound encounter with infinite Divine Love, which she strikingly compares to the touch of a scythe.</summary>
<keywords>Lent, spiritual aridity, mystical vision, Divine Love, inner eyes, fullness, Angela of Foligno</keywords>

->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-

listen to the secret: this satiety engendered an inexpressible hunger, and my limbs were breaking and shattering with desire, and I languished, I languished, I languished toward that which is beyond. Neither seeing, nor hearing, nor feeling any creature <note>In mystical writing, "creature" refers to any created being or thing—anything that is not God.</note>. Oh! Silence! Silence!

But there was a cry within. Oh! Do not make me languish any longer! Oh! Death! Death! For life to me is a death. Death! Blessed Virgin! Take the apostles with you! Go together, together, together before the Most High; and then on your knees, all at once on your knees, so that He may no longer wish, so that He may no longer allow me to suffer. Everyone on your knees, so that I may reach Him whom I feel! Saint Francis, on your knees! On your knees, Evangelist <note>Likely referring to Saint John the Evangelist, often associated with divine love and mystical visions.</note>! I cried out, I implored: He approaches, I thought, He approaches. See, I am becoming all love! There are many who believe themselves to be in love, and who are in hate; others, who believe themselves to be in hate, and who are in love. I desired to see this with a clear view; God gave me the evidence, and I remained satisfied. I was filled with a love to which I do not fear to promise eternity; and if a creature predicted the death of my love, I would say to them: "You lie"; and if it were an angel, I would say to him: "I know you; it is you who fell from heaven <note>A reference to Lucifer or the fallen angels; Angela asserts her spiritual certainty is so great that even an angelic visitation could not shake her unless it were a deceiving demon.</note>."

<summary>Angela describes the agonizing intensity of mystical desire, where the "satiety" of God’s presence creates a deeper hunger that makes earthly life feel like death. She concludes with a profound certainty in the eternal nature of the love she has received, claiming it is beyond even the deception of fallen angels.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Angela’s description of her internal mystical state, picking up from the intense longing and "hunger" described on the previous page. I saw two parts in myself, as if a tear had cut me in two. Here, that which is of God—love and the sovereign good <note>The "Sovereign Good" (Latin: summum bonum) is a philosophical and theological term for the ultimate, highest good, which is God.</note>; and there, my part—dryness and void, absolute emptiness.

And, in this light, I saw that it was not I who loved. I saw myself, however, within love; but it was by virtue of a gift.

Love drew near; it created in me a more ardent burning; and then, here is desire—the desire to go where He is. I do not know if above this love there is another, unless one reaches mortal love <term>mortal love: A mystical state where the intensity of divine love is so great that it feels as though it might physically kill the body or cause the "death" of the ego.</term>; for there is a love that brings death. Between generous love and mortal love, there is an intermediate love which closes the lips, because its joy and its abyss are beyond words. Someone would have done me a horrible wrong if they had recounted the Passion <note>The "Passion" refers to the suffering and crucifixion of Jesus Christ.</note> to me, or if they had named God before me, because at that name, I am delighted with such an infinite joy that I am crucified with languor and love.

And yet, everything that is less great than this Name becomes another torment for me.

Ah! Let no one speak to me anymore of the Gospel, nor of the life of Jesus Christ, nor of any divine word! All of that would no longer seem like anything to me. I see in God greater grandeurs!

<note>original vocabulary: Angela of Foligno, mysticism, sovereign good, mortal love, Passion, Jesus Christ, Gospel</note>

# CHAPTER TWENTY-SIX

## THE GREAT DARKNESS

---

One day my soul was rapt <note>In a mystical context, "rapt" refers to being seized or carried away into a state of spiritual ecstasy.</note> and I saw God in a clarity superior to any known clarity, and in a fullness superior to any fullness. In the place where I was, I searched for love, and found it no longer. I even lost Him whom I had dragged along until that moment <note>original: "Celui que j’avais traîné." Angela likely refers to her previous reliance on a humanized, tangible image of Christ or her own emotional efforts to reach Him.</note>, and I was made into "non-love" ¹.

1. This sublime word is commented upon by the entire treatise of Saint Dionysius the Areopagite <note>A 5th-6th century theologian and philosopher whose "apophatic" or "negative" theology emphasizes that God is best known by realizing His nature is beyond all human concepts and descriptions.</note> on the *Divine Names*. The great doctor, after having exhausted every affirmation—finding them all inferior to Him who designated Himself in human language by the *Tetragrammaton* <term>Tetragrammaton</term> <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God (YHWH), held to be so sacred it is inexpressible.</note>, three times mysterious, the terrible and ineffable name—the great doctor adds:

> "Although one attributes to the Divinity, who surpasses all things, the names of Unity and Trinity, nevertheless, this Trinity and this Unity can be known neither by us nor by any being; but, in order to holily glorify this indivisible and fruitful essence, we designate by the divine names of Trinity and Unity that which is more sublime than any name, more sublime than any substance; for it is neither unity nor trinity; it is neither number, nor singularity, nor fecundity; it is no existence, nor any known thing that can unveil the divine essence so excellently raised above all things, to unveil a mystery superior to... <note>The text here follows the mystical tradition of "Negative Theology," where the soul realizes God is so great that even words like "one" or "three" are merely human approximations.</note>

<summary>Angela describes a supreme mystical state of "non-love" where she encounters God in a fullness that surpasses all human emotion and previous conceptions of Christ. The text links this experience to the negative theology of Saint Dionysius, who argued that the divine essence is ultimately beyond all names and numbers.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, the Great Darkness, mystical ecstasy, non-love, Saint Dionysius the Areopagite, Divine Names, Tetragrammaton, negative theology, divine essence, apophaticism</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues Angela’s description of her mystical ascent into the "Great Darkness," a state where human understanding fails. It transitions from her first-person account to a scholarly commentary linking her experiences to the tradition of "negative theology" established by Saint Dionysius the Areopagite.</meta>

<page-num>95</page-num>

Then I saw God in a darkness, and necessarily in a darkness, because he is situated too high above the spirit, and everything that can become the object of a thought is out of proportion with him.

A perfect confidence was then given to me, a certain hope, a security without shadow and without obscuration, continual and guaranteed.

In the infinite good which appeared to me in the darkness, I withdrew entirely into myself, and at the bottom I found peace, the certainty of God with me; I found the Emmanuel <note>Hebrew: "God with us." In this context, it signifies the profound personal presence of the Divine within the soul.</note>.

Often I see God in this way, following the ineffable <note>Too great or extreme to be expressed or described in words.</note> mode and without the absolute fullness, which can be neither expressed by the mouth nor conceived by the heart. In the certain and secret good that I perceive within an immense darkness, [the divine essence] is en- <note>A line of text appears to be missing here in the original print between 'en-' and 'toute', likely: "tirely above" (French: *entièrement au-dessus de*).</note> tirely above all reason, all intelligence. "And God is not named, and is not explained; his majesty is absolutely inaccessible... From this it comes that theologians have preferred to rise to God by the way of negative locutions <term>negative locutions</term> <note>Also known as "apophatic theology," this is the practice of describing God only by what He is NOT (e.g., infinite, invisible), because His true nature exceeds human language.</note>." (Saint Dionysius, *The Divine Names*, ch. XIII, translation by Bishop Darboy <note>Georges Darboy (1813–1871) was the Archbishop of Paris and a noted translator of Dionysius.</note>.)

Perhaps Angela of Foligno reached the practical application of the Areopagite’s <note>Referring to Dionysius the Areopagite.</note> theories; perhaps she arrived at a mystical state that corresponded to the metaphysical greatnesses glimpsed by the disciple of Saint Paul. Realizing the "dark night" upon which Saint Dionysius fixed his eagle eye, Angela saw God in the immense darkness, and was made into "non-love" <note>This refers back to the previous page's concept of a state so far beyond human emotional love that it can only be described in the negative.</note>.

->(Translator's note.)<-

<summary>Angela describes her encounter with God within a "divine darkness" that transcends all human thought, providing her with absolute spiritual security. The text then provides a scholarly commentary linking her experience to the apophatic theology of Saint Dionysius the Areopagite.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Divine Darkness, Emmanuel, Saint Dionysius, Areopagite, negative theology, via negativa, Bishop Darboy, non-love, mysticism</keywords>

...my hope is buried; in Him I know and possess everything I wish to see and possess; in Him is the total good. I can fear neither His departure, nor mine, nor any separation. It is an ineffable <note>original: "ineffable" — meaning too great or extreme to be expressed or described in words.</note> delight in the good that contains everything, and nothing there can become the object of either a word or a conception. I see nothing, I see everything: certainty is drawn from the darkness. The deeper the darkness, the more the good exceeds everything; it is the reserved mystery.

Then I see within the darkness that He who is there, above all, surpasses even absolute good. And everything else is darkness, and everything one can think is very small beside it.

Pay attention. The divine power, wisdom, and will, which I have seen marvelously elsewhere, appear lesser than this.

This one is a whole; the others, one would say, are parts; the others, though unspeakable <term>inénarrables</term> <note>From the Latin *inenarrabilis*, referring to something that cannot be narrated or recounted. In mystical writing, this suggests an experience that transcends the narrative structure of human memory.</note>, give a joy that overflows into the body.

But when God appears in the darkness, there is neither laughter, nor fervor, nor devotion, nor love, nothing on the face, nothing in the heart, not a tremble, not a movement. The body sees nothing; the eyes of the soul are open. The body rests and sleeps, the tongue cut off and motionless: all the friendships that God has shown me, numerous and unspeakable, and His sweetnesses and His gifts, and His...

...words and His actions, all of this is small beside Him whom I see in the immense darkness; and even if everything were to deceive me, there would remain for me supreme peace, because of the immense darkness where the all-good <note>original: "tout bien" — a reference to the absolute goodness of God as a singular entity.</note> rests.

To the ineffable <note>original: "ineffable" — that which cannot be expressed in words.</note> altitude of seeing God in the immense darkness, my soul was raptured <note>original: "ravie" — referring to a state of mystical ecstasy where the soul is "carried away" by God.</note> three times. I have seen Him a thousand times with darkness, but only three times in the supreme obscurity. My body is labored by infirmities; the world pursues me with its trials and bitterness; the demons afflict and persecute me almost continually; they have power over me. God has permitted them to afflict my soul and my body, and I see almost materially the assaults they deliver upon me.

On the other side, God draws me to Him, through the supreme good that I see in the black night. In the immense darkness, I see the Holy Trinity, and within the Trinity, perceived in the night, I see myself, standing, at the center.

Here is the supreme attraction, beside which everything is nothing; here is the incomparable.

My words have the effect of nothingness on me; what am I saying? My words fill me with horror, O supreme obscurity! My words are curses, my words are blasphemies. Silence! Silence! Silence! Silence! When I dwell

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues Angela’s account of the "Seventh Step" of her mystical journey. Having described the "immense darkness" of the divine essence on the previous page, she now explains the transition between that formless void and her intimate visions of the incarnate Christ.</meta>

In the black shadow, I no longer remember the humanity of Jesus Christ, nor the God-man <note>original: "Dieu-homme" — a term used to emphasize the dual nature of Christ as both divine and human.</note>, nor anything else that has a form. I see everything and I see nothing.

Emerging from the darkness, I begin once more to see the God-Man; he draws my soul with sweetness, and he sometimes says: *You are me, and I am you.*

I see his eyes; I see his merciful face; he embraces my soul, he presses it against himself, he holds it in an immensely tight embrace. That which proceeds from his eyes and his face is the "good" that one sees in the black night. It is the thing that emerges from the depths, and inexpressible <note>original: "inénarrable" — a common mystical term for an experience that transcends the power of human speech.</note> delight comes with it. Within the God-Man my soul draws its life; it remains in him longer than it does in the dark vision <note>original: "vision obscure" — refers to the "Cloud of Unknowing" or the experience of God beyond all mental images.</note>. But the attraction of the immense darkness is incomparably superior—at least for me—to the attraction of the God-Man. From now on, I dwell in the God-Man almost continually. One day I received from him this assurance: that between him and me, there is nothing that acts as an intermediary. Since that moment, joy flows from his humanity onto me night and day.

Praise sings within me, and I say: "Glory to you, Lord! your cross is my bed; I have poverty for my pillow; I stretch out and rest..."

<summary>Angela describes the alternating states of her ecstasy, moving from the formless "black shadow" of God's essence to an intimate, direct union with the person of Jesus Christ. She expresses a preference for the "immense darkness" while finding constant joy in the humanity of the God-Man.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, God-Man, mystical union, divine darkness, Jesus Christ, poverty, ecstasy</keywords>

...my limbs in pain and contempt <note>original: "mépris" — also translated as "disdain" or "scorn." God loved the company of pain and contempt so much that he chose it for his Son; and the Son laid down in this bed, and he was in agreement with the Father in this love. It is in this bed that I have rested and that I rest; I hope to die there and be saved by him. O Jesus! The joy that I expect from these feet and these hands is an inexpressible joy <note>original: "inénarrable" — a term used by mystics to describe experiences that transcend the capacity of human language.</note>! When I see him, instead of returning to myself, I wish to approach always, always—and my life is a death. At the memory of him, I become mute; my tongue is cut off <note>A common mystical metaphor for "aphasia," or the inability to speak when confronted by the divine.</note>. When I leave him, the world and everything I encounter increases my hunger and my thirst. The length of the waiting makes my desire a mortal pain. In these visions and consolations, very often my soul is ravished and enchanted by the most sweet God, to whom be honor and glory forever and ever <note>original: "dans les siècles des siècles" — literally "in the ages of ages," a standard liturgical conclusion meaning "eternity."</note>. Amen. »

# CHAPTER TWENTY-SEVEN

## THE INEFFABLE

---

I was ravished in spirit and I found myself in God according to an unknown mode. I felt myself in the midst of the <term>Trinity</term> <note>The Christian doctrine that God exists as three persons—Father, Son, and Holy Spirit—in one essence.</note> by a mode of presence greater and more elevated. I was receiving blessings more enormous than usual, and from these blessings flowed joys, delights, and unspeakable delectations, far beyond my usual state and superior to my experience.

The divine operations that took place in my soul were too <term>ineffable</term> <note>From the Latin *ineffabilis*, meaning "unutterable." In mystical theology, this refers to experiences that completely transcend the capacity of human language.</note> to be recounted by any saint or any angel. The divinity of these operations and the depth of their abyss crushes the capacity and the intelligence of every soul and every creature. If I speak of them, my words feel to me like a blasphemy <note>Angela frequently expresses the fear that by trying to describe God in human words, she is "lying" or "blaspheming" because the reality is so much greater than the description.</note>. I am torn from my old habits. Farewell, hidden life of Christ that I loved so much before; farewell, profound contemplation of the depth—of the beloved depth of the Father, who from all eternity predestined the abyss to...

<summary>Angela describes a transcendent mystical experience where she is pulled into the presence of the Trinity, finding the experience so profound that human language feels like a "blasphemy" against its truth.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Trinity, ineffable, divine operations, mysticism, ecstasy, spiritual abyss</keywords>

<page-num>101</page-num>

...His Son, to keep him company; farewell, poverty, sufferings, abjection, which were the life of the Son of God, and which were also my rest on earth. Farewell, sacred darkness where I saw the face of the Lord; farewell, my ancient joy.

Now, my old life has been torn away from me with such <term>unction</term> <note>From the Latin *unctio*, meaning anointing. In this context, it refers to a smoothing, spiritual grace that makes the transition into a higher state feel divinely ordained and gentle.</note>, and amidst the forgetfulness of such a deep sleep, that I do not know how it happened; I remember only one thing: that I once had these things, and I have them no longer.

In the ineffable blessings and the new <term>operations</term> <note>A technical term in mystical theology referring to the direct actions of God upon the soul, where the human will becomes a passive recipient of divine influence.</note> that my soul undergoes, God first performs His work, then He manifests Himself; and at the moment He reveals Himself to my soul, He overwhelms it beneath more enormous gifts, accompanied by a higher, more ineffable light.

Now, He presents Himself in two ways.
Here is the first mode of manifestation. He manifests Himself in the innermost part of the soul: I then understand His presence in all nature, in every creature that has received the gift of being—in the demon, in the angel, in paradise, in adultery, in homicide <note>This is one of Angela's most radical insights: the concept of divine immanence. She suggests that because God is "Being" itself, nothing can exist—even a sinful act—without His underlying power of existence, though He does not cause the sin itself.</note>, in every good action, in everything that has received, to any degree, the gift of existing, in every...

<summary>Angela describes the transition from her former spiritual practices to a state where God acts directly upon her soul, revealing His presence as the underlying existence of all things, even in the midst of darkness or sin.</summary>

This presence is an <term>illustration</term> <note>In mystical theology, an "illustration" refers to a sudden, divine enlightenment of the intellect that surpasses human reason.</note> full of grace and truth, and the soul that possesses it is inaccessible to the shock of things! It brings divine joys; the deep feeling of the God who is there breathes humility and confusion; one remembers that one is a sinner. With consolation and divine joy, the soul receives wisdom and gravity.

As for the second mode of presence, it is completely different, and the joy it brings is not the same joy.

This unknown presence deeply gathers the soul into itself, and there, in the depths, it accomplishes the <term>divine operation</term> <note>As noted previously, this refers to the direct action of God upon the soul, where the human will is a passive recipient of grace.</note>, with an incomparable and more grandiose grace. Such is the abyss where it is fulfilled, the unutterable abyss of divine delights and illustrations, that this manifestation of God—with no other good but Himself—is the sovereign good, that which the saints possess for eternity. ...that instead of speaking, I blaspheme; and that instead of cultivating, I devastate <note>Angela often expresses the "mystic's paradox": the more she tries to describe God, the more she feels she is actually insulting His greatness because human words are so inadequate.</note>. If something must be said, I will say that the gifts the saints receive in eternal life are delights of the soul through which God increases its capacity to grasp Him and to hold Him. Oh! when God presents Himself to the soul, when the Lord reveals His face, He expands the soul and pours unknown joys and riches into this suddenly enlarged capacity; and this takes place in an <term>abyss</term> <note>In mystical theology, the "abyss" (French: *abîme*) refers to the unfathomable, bottomless depth of the Divine essence which "swallows up" the soul's limited understanding.</note> of which I have not yet spoken; this one is deeper. 

The soul is torn away from all darkness: the knowledge of God exceeds the possibilities foreseen by the intellect; and such is this light, and such is this joy, and such is this clarity <note>original: "évidence"</note>, and such is this new abyss that it is inaccessible to any created heart. After the abyss, my heart is ignorant; incapable of understanding anything, of thinking anything about the things of the abyss, it knows nothing, unless perhaps the natural impossibility it faced in going there. Of the things of the abyss, it is impossible to say anything; not a word exists whose sound gives an idea of the thing; not a thought, not an intellect can venture there. They remain in their own realms, in the lower realms. There is not one word, not one idea, that resembles the God of the abyss.

<summary>Angela describes how God expands the soul's capacity to experience Him, eventually leading the mystic into a "deeper abyss" that completely transcends human language, thought, and intellect. This state represents the ultimate ineffability of the divine, where no human word or concept can truly reflect the reality of God.</summary>

Holy Scripture is so profound that the wisest man in the whole world, being too weak to understand it, is surpassed by its depth; human intelligence is too short.

But when it comes to the absolutely ineffable <term>operations</term> <note>In mystical theology, "operations" refer to the active movements or "workings" of God within the human soul.</note> that exist and take place within the soul, in the supreme moment, in the dazzling light <note>original: "éblouissement"</note> of God, one cannot even stammer. My soul is often ravished by divine secrets. I understand then why Scripture is both easy and difficult; why it seems to contradict itself; how man misses the salvation that comes from it; how it condemns, and how it saves. I know these things, and I stand upon them, full of knowledge; and when I return from these divine secrets, I can speak a few small words with confidence. 

But when it comes to those ineffable operations, when it comes to the dazzling light of glory, do not approach, human speech; for what I am articulating at this moment feels to me like a ruin, and I feel the terror one has when one blasphemes <note>Angela frequently uses the word "blasphemy" to describe the act of using limited human language to describe the infinite God, viewing it as a form of unintentional desecration.</note>. If all the spiritual consolations, if all the heavenly joys, if all the divine delights that have been felt since the beginning of the world—let us go further, let us say something else; what should I say? If all the saints who have ever lived had spoken of God without ceasing, and if all the delig—

And I embrace the whole world, and it no longer seems to me that I am on earth, but in heaven, and in God. The heights of my past life are very low compared to these. O fullness, fullness! O filling light, certainty, majesty, and expansion, nothing approaches your glory! Now, this blinding radiance of God—I have experienced it more than a thousand times, and it was never twice the same, being eternally varied and forever new.

O altitude! O majesty! Neither on earth nor in heaven could I have believed, suspected, or invented such glory. And my soul, too narrow for itself, could neither encompass nor understand itself. If the created and finite soul cannot understand itself, how much further will its powerlessness grow in the face of the immense and the infinite, in the face of the <term>uncircumscribed</term> <note>original: "incirconscrit." A theological term meaning that which has no boundaries and cannot be contained or limited by space.</note>?

My soul presented itself before the face of God with an immense security, without shadow and without cloud; it presented itself with an unknown joy, with a youthful, superior rapture, above all excellence: the novelty and the splendor of the miracle that I was surpassed my own intelligence.

In the encounter I had with the Lord, I felt the <term>ineffable</term> <note>That which is beyond the power of words to describe.</note>, that thing of which I have spoken, the dazzling light of God; then words were spoken to me, words coming from the lips of the <term>Most High</term> <note>A traditional title for God.</note>. But I do not wish for them to be written down.

When, after this, the soul returns to itself, it finds therein a readiness to take joy in every pain and every insult borne for God: it feels the impossibility of a separation. Also...

# TWENTY-EIGHTH CHAPTER

## CERTAINTY

---

Some time after my conversion, on a feast day of the Virgin <note>Likely one of the major liturgical celebrations dedicated to Mary, such as the Assumption or the Purification.</note>, I begged her to obtain for me this immense grace: the certainty of not being deceived by the voices that spoke to me. I received a response that was a promise, and the voice that spoke added:

> "God has manifested himself to you; he has spoken to you; he has given you a sense of Him such as he has of himself. Avoid then speaking, seeing, and hearing, except according to Him."

I felt in the one who spoke an inexpressible <term>discretion</term> <note>original: "discrétion." In this context, it refers to the spiritual gift of "discernment of spirits," the ability to distinguish divine revelations from human or demonic deceptions.</note> and maturity. I remained in joy and in hope, with the feeling of a prayer answered. It was told to me at that same instant that I would no longer act except by the guidance of God. To see, to speak, to hear according to Him! I began to do these three things; suddenly my heart was lifted up from the earth and placed in God, and when it

<summary>Angela prays to the Virgin Mary for the gift of certainty to ensure her mystical revelations are authentic. She receives a divine promise that her senses will henceforth be guided entirely by God's own perspective.</summary>

<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Virgin Mary, certainty, discernment of spirits, divine guidance, mystical prayer, conversion</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Angela’s profound mystical state where her heart is "placed in God." It concludes her request for certainty regarding the authenticity of her visions, resolving the spiritual tension introduced on the previous page.</meta>

->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-

<page-num>111</page-num>

was necessary to descend to the things of life, such as speaking or eating, nothing disturbed my heart from its position; I could neither think, nor see, nor feel anything but God. When, at the end of the <term>prayer</term> <note>original: "oraison." This refers to "mental prayer" or interior contemplation, a silent form of communication with God distinct from recited vocal prayers.</note>, I went to take nourishment, I asked permission: "Go," said the voice, "eat with the blessing of the Father, and the Son, and the Holy Spirit." Sometimes the permission was slow in coming, sometimes not. This lasted for three days and three nights.

Finally, <term>rapt in spirit</term> <note>A state of ecstasy or "ravishment" where the mystic feels their soul is pulled out of their body or normal consciousness to be with God.</note>, during the Mass, I saw God at the moment of the <term>elevation</term> <note>original: "élévation." The moment in the Catholic Mass when the priest raises the consecrated bread (the Host) for the congregation to adore, believed to be the real presence of Christ.</note>. After this vision, there remained in me an inexpressible sweetness and an immense joy that will last my whole life. It was in this vision that I received the assurance I had asked for, and doubt took flight. I received full satisfaction; I had the certainty that it was God who had spoken to me.

# TWENTY-NINTH CHAPTER

## THE <term>ANOINTING</term> <note>original: "onction." In mystical theology, an "anointing" refers to a spiritual grace that "softens" or "perfumes" the soul, often accompanied by a physical sensation of warmth or sweetness.</note>

---

Another time, I was in <term>prayer</term> <note>original: "oraison." This refers to "mental prayer" or interior contemplation, a silent form of communication with God distinct from recited vocal prayers.</note>. I heard words of peace: “O my dear daughter,” said the voice, “I love you much more than you love me; o my chosen temple, the heart of the Almighty God is pressed against your heart.”

An unknown and inexpressibly delicious feeling flowed through all my limbs, and I fell to the ground, and I remained stretched out. The voice spoke again:

“Almighty God has chosen you above all the women of this city, and has placed his love in you. He takes his delights in you, in you and in your companion <note>likely referring to Angela's friend and follower, Masazuola, who shared in her spiritual life.</note>. Let your life therefore be light and mercy for whoever shall look upon it; let it be justice and judgment for whoever shall not look upon it.”

# THIRTIETH CHAPTER

## JESUS CHRIST

---

I was meditating one day on the Passion <note>The <term>Passion</term> refers to the short final period in the life of Jesus, encompassing his suffering and death on the cross.</note> of the Son of God and on his poverty. Now, Christ gave me the *vision* of his poverty. He showed it to me as immense within my heart. His will was urgent; he commanded me to see it and to consider it well. And I saw those for whom he made himself poor. I felt such a sense of reproach and sorrow that my heart failed me.

Then he increased in me the light which shone upon his Passion. I saw him poor in friends, poor in relatives; finally, I saw him poor in himself, and, in relation to his humanity, incapable of helping himself <note>Angela is describing the concept of <term>kenosis</term>, or the "self-emptying" of Christ, where he chose to experience the full vulnerability of being human.</note>. It is sometimes said that his divine power was hidden because of his humanity; it was not hidden, of this I have received God's assurance; but when I saw to what state Jesus was reduced in terms of his humanity, I began to glimpse for the first time the dimensions

<summary>Angela of Foligno describes a divine vision of Christ’s extreme poverty and vulnerability during his Passion, emphasizing his total lack of support and his choice to experience human limitation.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Passion of Christ, spiritual poverty, humanity of Jesus, mystical vision, kenosis, Franciscan spirituality</keywords>

of my pride: I felt a pain that I did not know, greater than ever, and so deep that I believe myself henceforth incapable of joy. I understood with what eyes he saw all those hearts of executioners <note>original: *bourreaux*. This refers to the Roman soldiers and others who actively participated in the crucifixion.</note> obstinate against him. He saw all their limbs conspiring together in the sole concern of abolishing his name and his memory. He saw their anger gathering their memories and marshalling their forces to destroy the Savior; he saw their subtleties, their ruses, their machinations; he saw all their counsel and the multitude of their calumnies, and their rage, and their atrocious anger; he counted their preparations one by one; he was present in their thoughts, in the internal and external searchings that their cruelty made to prepare unknown refinements for his torture. Their ferocity had innumerable inventions. He saw the tortures being prepared for him, and the insults, and the ignominies.

In this light my soul saw, of the Passion of Christ, more things than I am able and even than I wish to declare. I have made certain discoveries for which I ask permission to remain silent <note>This "holy silence" is a common trope in <term>mysticism</term>, where the experience of God is considered "ineffable," or beyond the power of human words to describe.</note>.

## THIRTY-FIRST CHAPTER

### CALVARY <note>Also known as Golgotha, this refers to the site outside Jerusalem where the crucifixion of Jesus took place. In mystical literature, "Calvary" often represents the highest point of spiritual suffering and union with God.</note>

---

Once again, the suffering of Jesus Christ was placed before my eyes. Neither the tongue is sufficient to tell what I saw, nor the heart to feel it. Every other feeling became impossible for me, except for a feeling of sorrow without precedent in my life. And I was transformed into sorrow itself. <note>In mystical theology, "transformation" refers to a state where the visionary so closely identifies with Christ's suffering that they feel they have become the suffering itself.</note>

And my soul saw within the soul of Christ some of his sufferings along with their causes.

This soul was without stain, absolutely holy, and, for its own part, should never have known punishment.

He suffered, therefore, only for us—for us who are so ungrateful, so unworthy, and who mocked him at the very moment he was <term>redeeming</term> <note>The act of "buying back" humanity from the debt of sin through sacrifice.</note> us. Since the sin of his executioners was beyond all measure, Jesus—who hated sin with an infinite hatred—did not merely feel his <term>Passion</term> <note>From the Latin *passio* (suffering); specifically the period of spiritual and physical agony leading up to and including the death of Jesus.</note> as a physical ordeal; he felt it as—

# THIRTY-SECOND CHAPTER

## THE NAILS

---

Another time I was reflecting on the immeasurable pain of Jesus Christ on the cross, and I thought of those nails which, according to a certain saying <note>Angela likely refers here to a specific devotional tradition or a phrase she had heard in a sermon regarding the physical brutality of the Crucifixion.</note>, had driven the flesh of his hands and feet into the wood, and I desired to see at least that small part of Christ's flesh that those nails had driven into the wood. This suffering of Christ gave me such pain that I was no longer able to stand. I lowered my head and I fell. Then I saw Jesus Christ lean his head upon my arms, which were stretched out on the ground; he showed me his own arms, and at the same time his neck. Immediately my pain was changed into such joy that I lost all feeling and sight of everything that was not him. The neck was of a beauty that would make human words die <note>original: "faire mourir la parole humaine". I understood that this unheard-of beauty was the radiance <note>original: "rejaillissement". Literally a "springing back" or "overflowing." Angela suggests the physical form of Christ was glowing with the light of his divine nature.</note> of the divinity, and yet my eyes

# CHAPTER THIRTY-THREE

## TRUE LOVE AND FALSE LOVE

---

Another time, it was the fourth day of Holy Week <note>Holy Wednesday, the Wednesday before Easter, traditionally associated with the betrayal of Christ by Judas</note>, and I was immersed in a meditation on the death of the Son of God. I meditated with great sorrow, striving to empty my soul of all other things so that I might seize it and hold it entirely gathered within the Passion <note>term: Passion - From the Latin 'passio' (suffering); refers to the physical and spiritual suffering of Jesus from the Agony in the Garden to his death on the Cross</note> and the death of the Son of God. I was completely lost in the desire to find the strength to achieve this emptiness of spirit and to meditate more effectively.

Then, these words were spoken into my soul: "It was not in jest <note>original: "pour rire." This is one of the most famous revelations in Angela's writings, emphasizing that Christ’s love was a literal, physical, and agonizing reality, not a mere gesture or metaphor</note> that I have loved you."

These words dealt a mortal blow to my soul, and I do not know how I survived it; for my eyes were opened, and I saw in the light of truth just how true those words were. I saw the actions, the real effects of that love, and to what lengths it had truly led the Son of God. I saw what he endured in his life and in his death for the love of me, by the power

# THIRTY-FOURTH CHAPTER

## THE CROSS AND THE BLESSING

---

One day I was at Mass in the church of Saint Francis. The moment of the elevation <note>The point in the Mass when the priest raises the consecrated Host and Chalice for the congregation to adore.</note> was approaching, and the choir of Angels resounded: *Holy, Holy, Holy* <note>original: "Sanctus, Sanctus, Sanctus" — the hymn of praise from the liturgy of the Mass.</note>, etc.; my soul was carried away and enraptured in the <term>uncreated light</term> <note>In mystical theology, this refers to the light of God’s own essence, which is eternal and not part of the created world.</note>; it was drawn in, it was absorbed, and behold—a fullness ineffable <note>Too great or extreme to be expressed or described in words.</note>, ineffable, in truth.

Regard as nothing, as absolutely nothing, everything that can be expressed in human language.

O unspeakable creation of the uncreated and almighty God, the praises that one can sing are but dust compared to you. Sacred absorption of the abyss where the hand of the ravishing <note>Here used in the sense of "seizing" or "carrying away" the soul in divine ecstasy.</note> God plunges me; after your transport, but still under the influence that had preceded it, there appeared to me the image of the crucified God, as if just a moment after the descent from the cross; the blood was fresh and red and still flowing from the wounds
