# THIRTY-FIFTH CHAPTER

## THE WAYS OF DELIVERANCE

---

One day I was in prayer. I was meditating on the <term>Passion</term> <note>The "Passion" refers to the period of intense spiritual and physical suffering of Jesus Christ leading up to and including his crucifixion.</note> with a deep, entirely internal sorrow. I sought to measure, to weigh my crimes, since their redemption cost the Son of God not just prayers or tears, but death—and such a death! I was trying to calculate what the weight of damnation might be, since, to lift this weight, it required neither the death of an angel, nor that of an archangel, but the death of the true God! And I plunged myself into thoughts of hell and its immense torments, its infinite misery, and its countless tortures! Then I tried to weigh my own ingratitude. For this nameless and immeasurable benefit, what do I bring in return? Sin. Daily sin, forgetting the resurrection, and the refusal to cooperate. On one side, God’s mercy contemplated in an abyss; in the other, my injustice and

<summary>Angela meditates on the "weight" of sin and the infinite cost of redemption, noting that only the death of God himself—not an angel—could outweigh the burden of human damnation. She contrasts the abyss of divine mercy with the abyss of her own daily ingratitude and injustice.</summary>
<keywords>Passion of Christ, redemption, damnation, divine mercy, sin, Angela of Foligno, meditation, weight of sin</keywords>

my madness, all this led me to a kind of wisdom. In this state, I had the revelation of sins of every kind, and of the tortures and the punishments from which the <term>Passion of Jesus</term> has saved us. I was in the crowd; but such was the light of this terrifying vision that I could scarcely prevent myself from roaring <note>original: "rugir." Angela of Foligno was famous for her "shouts" or "roars" of spiritual ecstasy/agony that occurred in public spaces, often to her own embarrassment.</note> in the midst of men.

I had an apparition of the <term>Crucified Christ</term>. He showed me how he had been suspended on the cross, and how the man who loses himself is forever without excuse. For <term>salvation</term> requires of man what the doctor requires of the sick; one must confess their illness and carry out the prescription. There is no expense to be made for the treatment. One only has to show oneself to the doctor, do the things prescribed, and keep away from forbidden things.

My soul then had an understanding of the <term>antidote</term> <note>The metaphor of Christ as the "Divine Physician" and his blood as the "medicine" or "antidote" for the "poison" of sin was a common theme in medieval theology.</note> residing in the blood of Christ. The antidote is distributed for free, and requires only a disposition. Then all my sins were laid out before my soul, and I recognized in each of my members a <term>spiritual infirmity</term>.

Then, in accordance with what I had just learned, I strove to lay out before God all the miseries of my soul and my body, and I cried: “O Lord, my God, who holdest...”

# THIRTY-SIXTH CHAPTER

->JOY<-

***

One day, I was looking at the cross, and on it the Crucified One; I saw him with the eyes of the body. <note>Angela often distinguishes between seeing with "the eyes of the body" (physical sight) and "the eyes of the mind" (spiritual vision). Here, the experience begins with physical contemplation.</note> Suddenly my soul was set ablaze with such ardor, that joy and pleasure penetrated all my limbs intimately. I saw and I felt Christ embrace my soul with that arm which was crucified, and my joy astonished me; for it was outside my usual habits, and, at the degree it reached, I did not yet know it. Since that moment, there remains in me a joy and a sublime light in which my soul sees the secret of our flesh in <term>communion: a close spiritual relationship or sharing of life</term> with God. This <term>delight: a profound spiritual sweetness or satisfaction</term> of the soul is <term>indescribable: beyond the power of words to express</term> <note>original: "inénarrable"</note>; this joy is continual; this <term>illumination: a spiritual enlightenment or divine "lighting up" of the mind</term> <note>original: "illustration"</note> is dazzling beyond all my previous dazzlements. Since that moment, there has remained in me such certainty, such security regarding the <term>divine operations: the active workings or influence of God within a person</term> that take place in me, that I am surprised to have ever known doubt, and

if all the created worlds took up a voice to try and recreate it, they would speak in vain; for I see, in the transports of a pleasure that cannot be recounted <note>original: "qui ne se raconte pas"</note>, I see that hand which he showed me with the mark of the nails, and which he will show on the day when he says:
"Behold what I have suffered for you."

Even now, when I am in this vision and in this embrace, such joy is communicated to my soul that I would try in vain to suffer the sufferings of Jesus; yet I see his hand and the wound in his hand. All my joy is henceforth in this crucified God. Sometimes the embrace is so tight that it seems to my soul that it enters into the wound in his side. There, the soul is <term>illuminated: spiritually enlightened or filled with divine light</term> <note>original: "illustrée"</note> by joys which human speech has no right to approach. Striking <note>original: "Foudroyante," implying a sudden, lightning-like force</note> joy, which takes from my legs the strength to carry me, which throws me to the ground, which knocks me over, which stretches me out there, lying down and speechless! This happened to me once in the Piazza of Saint Mary. They were performing the <term>Passion: a dramatic retelling or play of the trial, suffering, and death of Jesus</term>! One might have thought I was going to weep. I was touched and flooded with a joy that was not natural; the joy grew, it grew; I lost the power of speech, and I fell to the ground, struck down: I had just experienced the <term>indescribable: that which is beyond the power of words</term> <note>original: "inénarrable"</note>, the dazzle of glory.

# THIRTY-SEVENTH CHAPTER

## THE THRONES

---

It was during Mass; I was trying to immerse myself in the abysses into which the humility and goodness of God cast me, when He deigns to approach us in the **Blessed Sacrament** <term>Blessed Sacrament: the bread and wine consecrated during the Mass, which Catholics believe becomes the real presence of Jesus Christ</term> of the altar.

I was caught up in spirit <note>original: "ravie en esprit," referring to a state of mystical ecstasy or trance</note>, and for the first time, I had an **intellectual vision** <term>intellectual vision: a high form of mystical experience where the soul understands a truth directly in the mind, without the use of physical eyes or mental images</term> regarding the Blessed Sacrament. It was first said to me that the body of Christ can be simultaneously on all the altars of the world by the power of the **Omnipotence** <note>original: "Toute-Puissance," referring to the all-powerful nature of God</note>, which cannot fit within the narrow measure of the thoughts of a person living on this earth.

"Scripture," said the voice, "speaks much of this power; but those who read understand little. Those to whom I grant a certain feeling of myself understand more, but even they understand very little. But a moment will come when you will see the light."

<summary>Angela experiences an intellectual vision during Mass where she is taught about the divine power that allows Christ to be present on every altar simultaneously, despite human intellectual limitations.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Blessed Sacrament, Mass, intellectual vision, Omnipotence, Holy Scripture, mystical understanding</keywords>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>This page continues Angela's Eucharistic vision from the previous page. While page 148 dealt with the "intellectual vision" of Christ's omnipresence on all altars, this page transitions into a visual, sensory experience of the heavenly court accompanying the host during the Mass.</meta>

->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-
149

Then, I saw in a flash how God comes into the **Blessed Sacrament** <term>Blessed Sacrament: the bread and wine consecrated during the Mass, which Catholics believe becomes the actual body and blood of Christ</term>. Neither before nor since have I experienced anything like it.

Then I saw how Jesus Christ comes with an army of angels, and the magnificence of His escort was savored by my soul with immense delight. I wondered for a moment that I could take pleasure in looking at angels. For usually all my joy is condensed in Jesus Christ alone. But soon I perceived in my soul two perfectly distinct joys: one coming from God, the other from the angels, and they did not resemble each other. I admired the magnificence with which the Lord was surrounded. I asked the name of those I saw. “These are the <term>Thrones</term> <term>Thrones: in the traditional hierarchy of angels, the Thrones are the third highest rank, representing divine justice and serving as the "seat" of God's power</term>,” said the voice. Their multitude was dazzling and so perfectly innumerable that, if number and measure were not the laws of creation, I would have believed the sublime crowd I saw was without number and without measure. I could see no end to this multitude, neither in width nor in length; I saw crowds beyond what our numbers can count.

<summary>Angela experiences a vivid vision of the Blessed Sacrament, witnessing Jesus Christ accompanied by an immeasurable host of angels known as the Thrones. She notes a distinct spiritual difference between the joy she feels from God and the joy she feels from the beauty of His angelic court.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Blessed Sacrament, Eucharist, angelic hierarchy, Thrones, mystical vision, divine joy, celestial escort</keywords>

## THIRTY-EIGHTH CHAPTER

### THE ANGELS

---

It was in September, on the feast of the holy angels. I was at the church in <term>Foligno</term> <term>Foligno: a city in the Umbria region of Italy where Angela lived as a Franciscan tertiary and experienced her famous mystical revelations</term> and I wished to receive communion. I was praying to the angels, especially <term>Saint Michael</term> <term>Saint Michael: the archangel and leader of the heavenly host, celebrated on September 29th (Michaelmas)</term> and the <term>seraphim</term> <term>seraphim: the highest order of angels in the celestial hierarchy, often depicted with six wings and associated with the fire of divine love</term>, and I said:

> "O ministering angels, who have received from God the office and power to communicate Him through knowledge and love, I beseech you to present Him to me as the Father of mercies gave Him to men—as He Himself wishes to be received and adored: poor, suffering, despised, wounded, bloodied, crucified, and dead."

The angels replied to me with an unspeakable sweetness and kindness:

> "Since you have found grace before the Lord, here He is; you possess Him. We present Him to you; and beyond what you have asked, we give you the power to present Him and communicate Him to others."

Indeed, I saw, in the <term>Blessed Sacrament</term> <term>Blessed Sacrament: the bread (Host) consecrated during the Mass, which Catholics believe becomes the literal body of Christ</term>, with

the eyes of the spirit, the <term>real presence</term><term>real presence: the theological doctrine that Jesus Christ is truly and substantially present in the bread and wine of the Eucharist</term>; I saw Him whom I had wanted to see, just as I had wanted to see Him: suffering, bloodied, crucified, and dead. ## THIRTY-NINTH CHAPTER

### MARY

---

One day I was hearing <term>Mass</term><term>Mass: the central act of Catholic worship, centered on the liturgy of the Eucharist</term>; and at the moment of the <term>elevation</term><term>elevation: the ritual lifting of the consecrated host and chalice by the priest for the adoration of the faithful</term>, at the very instant when the congregants were kneeling, I was caught up in spirit: the Virgin appeared to me and said:

> “My daughter, beloved of God, and my beloved, my Son has already come to you, and you have received his blessing.”

She made me understand that her Son was upon the altar after the <term>consecration</term><term>consecration: the moment in the Mass when the bread and wine are believed to be transformed into the Body and Blood of Christ</term> of the <term>host</term><term>host: the wafer of unleavened bread used in the sacrament of the Eucharist</term>. I heard what I had never heard before; I heard that it was a matter of an absolutely new joy. Indeed, the joy that resulted from the words I heard was such that if someone were to ask me: “Is there a creature who could express this by any word whatsoever?” I would answer: “I do not know, and I do not believe so.” The Virgin spoke with great humility, and deposited within my soul a new feeling of an unknown sweetness. One thing astonished me: it was that I had been able to remain standing. I did not fall to the ground, and I do not understand it at all <note>Mystical ecstasies often resulted in physical collapse or "falling in the spirit"; Angela is surprised that the intensity of this joy did not overcome her physical strength</note>.

She added:

I experienced a new joy, which was surpassed by no joy I had ever known, but it was soon surpassed by itself; for it increased at the moment of the <term>elevation</term><term>elevation: the ritual lifting of the consecrated host by the priest for the adoration of the faithful</term>. I did not see the body of Jesus Christ upon the altar; I see it often, but I did not see it that day. But I felt the presence of Jesus Christ in my soul; I felt it in truth.

I learned then that, to set a soul ablaze, there is no fire like the presence of Christ; it was not the fire that usually burns within me; this one was extraordinarily sweet.

When this flame is in the soul, I can vouch for the presence of God; He alone can light it.

In moments like that, my limbs believe they are going to separate. I even hear the noise they make; it sounds like a dislocation <note>original: "déboîtement." Angela frequently describes intense physical manifestations of her spiritual ecstasies, including sensations of her body being stretched or her joints popping as if on a rack.</note>. I especially experience this impression at the moment of the elevation. My fingers separate and my hands open.

# CHAPTER FORTY

## FULLNESS

---

One day I was approaching the holy table <note>The "holy table" refers to the altar where the Eucharist is distributed to the faithful.</note>, and I heard the voice, and it said to me:

> “Beloved, all good is in you, and you are going to receive all good.”

I said to myself internally: “If the good is already in you, why are you going to receive it?”
And the voice replied:

> “The one does not exclude the other.”

The moment of <term>communion: the ritual of receiving the consecrated bread, which Catholics believe to be the actual Body of Christ</term> was approaching, and I heard:

> “The Son of God is now on the altar, both in his <term>humanity: the nature of Christ as a human being</term> and in his <term>divinity: the nature of Christ as God</term>. The multitude of angels is united with him.”

I desired to see, and I saw. I did not see Jesus in any physical form; but I saw a <term>fullness: a mystical perception of God’s absolute and all-encompassing presence</term> and a beauty; I saw the <term>Sovereign Good: the "Summum Bonum," or the highest possible good which is God himself</term>.

> “O beloved,” said the voice, “you shall be thus before him for all eternity.”

<vocab>Angela of Foligno, holy table, communion, Son of God, humanity, divinity, angels, fullness, Sovereign Good, eternity</vocab>

<summary>Angela describes a mystical dialogue and vision occurring as she prepares to receive the Eucharist. She experiences God not as a physical shape, but as a "Sovereign Good" and an all-encompassing fullness that exists both within her soul and in the sacrament.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Eucharist, mysticism, Sovereign Good, Summum Bonum, divinity of Christ, angelic presence, spiritual fullness</keywords>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues Angela's narrative of her Eucharistic experience. Having previously described the spiritual vision of God's "fullness" and "Sovereign Good" at the altar, she now focuses on the physical and sensory experience of consuming the Host and the specific sensations triggered by the Sign of the Cross.</meta>

->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-

I renounce once again trying to describe my joy.

Recently, when I receive <term>communion: the ritual of consuming the consecrated bread (the Host)</term>, the host expands in my mouth; it has neither the flavor of bread, nor that of any known flesh; but a certain flavor of unknown flesh, a very distinct and delicious flavor, which can be compared to absolutely nothing. The host is not hard as it was before, and it does not go down in fragments, according to my old habit. Instead, it remains whole, and its <term>sweetness: in mystical theology, "suavitas" or sweetness refers to a spiritual delight that overflows into the physical senses</term> is so divine that, if I had not been instructed to swallow it without too much delay, I would keep it in my mouth for a long time. And it goes down entirely whole, and it has the unknown flavor of which I spoke, without saying anything further about it. When it descends, it gives me an inexpressible pleasure, which manifests even on the outside. My body trembles, and it is extremely difficult for me to remain still.

Now, when I make the **sign of the cross**, when I bring my hand to my forehead, saying: *In the name of the Father* <note>original: "Au nom du Père"</note>, I feel nothing new. But when I bring my hand to my chest, saying: *And of the Son* <note>original: "Et du Fils"</note>, I experience such love and such joy that it reveals itself and I feel it right there.

Without an order <note>Angela frequently mentions that she only shares these intimate experiences because her confessor, Brother Arnaldo, commanded her to do so under the vow of obedience.</note>, I would have neither said, nor permitted the writing of all the rest, nor of this.

# CHAPTER FORTY-ONE

## THE ALTAR OF THE ANGELS

It was the Feast of the Angels <note>Likely Michaelmas, the feast of St. Michael and all the angels, celebrated on September 29th.</note>. I was sick, and I wanted to receive <term>communion: the ritual of consuming the consecrated bread, believed by Catholics to be the Body of Christ</term>. There was no one available to bring the communion to me. My sadness was immense. Suddenly, at the very depth of my pain and my desire, I was carried in spirit to contemplate the eternal praise of the angels, and their <term>sublime office: the specific duties and liturgical roles assigned to the various ranks of angels</term>, and their assistance and their ministry. And behold, I was caught up in ecstasy, and an immense multitude of angels appeared to me, and they led me near an altar, and they said to me: "Behold the altar of the Angels." And upon the altar they showed me the praise of the Angels—that is to say, Him who is their praise, and the universal praise, and praise itself <note>Angela suggests that Christ is not just the recipient of praise, but the very essence of the praise the angels offer.</note>. And the angels said to my soul: "In Him who is upon the altar is the perfection and the fulfillment of the sacrifice you seek. Prepare yourself, therefore, to receive Him. You already have the ring of His love upon your finger; already you are His bride <note>This refers to the "Mystical Marriage," a common spiritual milestone for medieval female mystics, symbolizing a permanent union with God.</note>. But the union...

<summary>Angela, unable to receive communion due to illness, experiences a mystical vision of the heavenly liturgy where angels present Christ upon an altar as the ultimate sacrifice.</summary>

# FORTY-SECOND CHAPTER

## TWELVE YEARS

---

One day I saw Jesus Christ in the consecrated Host <term>Host: The bread used in the Christian service of the Eucharist, which believers hold becomes the body of Christ.</term>; I saw him in the form of a child. But this immense child, Lord above all lords, seemed to me to hold in his hand the scepter and the sign of dominion <note>This "sign of dominion" likely refers to the *globus cruciger*, an orb topped with a cross representing Christ's sovereignty over the world.</note>. What then was he holding in his hand? The priest raised the Host; everyone fell to their knees except me. I remained standing; the excess of my joy kept my eyes fixed upon him. But the priest placed the Host back on the altar too quickly for my liking. I had a cruel moment of sadness and weariness <note>original: "ennui." In this context, it refers to a painful spiritual longing or a sense of deprivation rather than simple boredom.</note>. If I tried to describe the beauty and splendor of Him whom I saw, I would need a language that I do not know. Based on his size, I would have certainly judged him to be twelve years old. The joy of this vision was so immense that I...

# FORTY-THIRD CHAPTER

## SPLENDOR

---

Another day, during the Mass, I was caught up in spirit <note>To be "caught up in spirit" (Latin: *in spiritu*) refers to a state of mystical ecstasy where the soul's perceptions are detached from the physical world and directed toward divine realities.</note>, and I spoke to the Lord, and I asked Him: “You are in the Blessed Sacrament <note>The Blessed Sacrament refers to the bread and wine consecrated during the Mass, which Catholics believe becomes the real body and blood of Christ.</note>; but, Lord, where are your faithful?” But He, opening my understanding, answered and said to me: “Where I am, there they are with me.” I opened the eyes of my soul, and I saw that this was so; and among the faithful, I clearly distinguished myself; but this being that we had there was not inside the Divinity <note>The "Divinity" (or Godhead) refers to the essence of God. Angela is distinguishing between being "with" God and being "of" the same essence as God.</note>, it was outside. He is alone in Himself wherever He is; only He encompasses all things. I have seen the body of Jesus Christ in the Blessed Sacrament, often and in various ways. Sometimes I saw the neck of Jesus Christ, but with such splendor and such magnificence that, compared to Him, the sun had very little. It is this beauty that revealed God to me. How pale the sun is beside Him! I saw at the han- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "hands."</note>

But I feel a profound sorrow: I cannot make understood what I have seen. It has also happened to me to see two dazzling eyes, then the mouth, and I saw nothing but that. These visions resemble new creations; it is joy that brings them about. These immense and varied joys cannot be compared to one another; but each of them, by virtue of being so immense, seems as though it must be eternal.

# CHAPTER FORTY-FOUR

## THE PRAYER TO THE BLESSED VIRGIN

---

That day I was not in prayer; I had just eaten and was resting. At the moment I was thinking of it least, I was caught up in spirit, and I saw the Virgin in her glory. Could a woman, then, be placed upon such a throne and in such majesty? This feeling flooded me with an ineffable <note>ineffable: something too great or extreme to be expressed or described in words.</note> joy. This glory was possible for a woman: it is so, and I have seen it. She was standing, praying for the human race; the capacity that comes from goodness and that which comes from strength gave her prayer unspeakable virtues. I was transported with happiness at the sight of this prayer; and while I was looking at the Virgin, suddenly Jesus Christ appeared near her, clothed in his glorified humanity. I had the sense of the sorrows that this flesh had suffered, the reproaches <term>reproaches (opprobres): harsh criticism, shame, or public disgrace</term> it had endured, the cross it had carried; the tortures and the ignominies of the Passion <note>The "Passion" refers to the short final period in the life of Jesus, including his visit to Jerusalem and his crucifixion.</note> were placed within my

<summary>Angela describes a sudden vision of the Virgin Mary in a state of immense majesty and glory, followed by the appearance of Jesus in His glorified humanity, which reminds Angela of His physical suffering during the Passion.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Virgin Mary, Jesus Christ, glorified humanity, Passion, mystical vision, intercession, divine majesty</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the account from the previous page where Angela, after seeing the Virgin Mary, witnessed Jesus Christ in His glorified humanity. The vision of His past suffering and the "ignominies of the Passion" was placed within her spirit, leading to the transformative experience described here.</meta>

->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO 163<-

...spirit. But here is the marvelous part: the feeling of the unheard-of <term>unheard-of (inouïs): something so extreme or unusual as to be previously unknown</term> torments of which I had knowledge, and which Jesus suffered for us; this feeling, instead of breaking me with grief, broke me with joy. Transported by an unspeakable <note>original: "inénarrable" — a common term in mystical writing for an experience that transcends human language</note> happiness, I lost the power of speech and I waited for death. And I experienced a pain above all pain: for I waited in vain. Death did not come, and I did not immediately reach—since death refused to break my earthly bonds—the unspeakable reality that was before my eyes.

This vision lasted three days without interruption. I ate, though very little, but, languishing with desire, I could not speak; I was cast down, prostrate, overcome.

If I had something to do, I did it; but one must not name God in my presence, for my joy then became absolutely unbearable.

# CHAPTER FORTY-FIVE

## FEBRUARY 2ND

---

It was the day of the <term>Purification of the Virgin</term> <note>Also known as Candlemas, this feast commemorates the ritual purification of Mary and the presentation of the infant Jesus in the Temple forty days after his birth.</note>. I was in Foligno, in the church of the <term>Friars Minor</term> <note>original: "Frères Mineurs" — the Franciscans, the religious order Angela was affiliated with as a member of the Third Order.</note>. And the voice spoke; it said to me: “Behold the hour when Mary, Virgin and Queen, came to the temple with her Son.”

My soul listened with a great love, and, having listened, it was ravished <note>original: "ravie" — a term for mystical ecstasy where the soul feels "carried away" by God.</note>; and in its rapture it saw the Queen enter, and it went toward her, trembling with respect. I hesitated, however; I was afraid to approach. She reassured me, and reached out Jesus toward me, and said to me: “O you who love my Son, receive the one whom you love.” She laid him in my arms; he was wrapped in swaddling clothes; his eyes were closed as if in sleep.

The Queen sat down, like a tired woman. Her gestures were so beautiful, her attitude so marvelous, her person so noble, her sight so sublime, that my eyes could not fix themselves on Jesus alone, and were forced to look at his mother.

<summary>Angela experiences a vision on the Feast of the Purification where she visits the temple in Foligno and is invited by the Virgin Mary to hold the infant Jesus. She is struck by the humanity and weary grace of Mary, whose presence is so powerful it rivals the sight of the Christ child.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Virgin Mary, Infant Jesus, Candlemas, Friars Minor, mystical vision, Foligno, spiritual rapture</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page concludes the vision of the Presentation in the Temple, transitioning from the humanity of the infant Jesus to a revelation of his divine majesty and Angela's total surrender to God.</meta>

->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-
->165<-

Suddenly the child woke in my arms: his swaddling clothes had fallen away; he opened and raised his eyes. Jesus looked at me; with that one look he overcame me, he conquered me completely. Splendor flowed from his eyes, and his joy shone like a blinding flame.

Then he appeared in his immense, ineffable <note>original: "ineffable" — something that is too great or extreme to be expressed or described in words.</note> majesty, and he said to me:

“He who has not seen me small will not see me great.” He added: “I have come to you, and I offer myself to you so that you may offer yourself to me.”

Then my soul offered itself to him by an astonishing mode of <term>oblation</term> <note>From the Latin *oblatio*, an "oblation" is a formal religious offering or gift to God.</note>, beyond any connection to words: I offered myself entirely: I offered my sons <note>Angela’s mother, husband, and children all died shortly after her conversion. Here, she describes the spiritual act of releasing her worldly attachments and family to God's care.</note> with me in a whole and perfect <term>oblation</term>, keeping nothing for myself—nothing of their persons, and nothing of their things.

My soul understood that its <term>oblation</term> was well-received, and the joy of God, in accepting it, did not remain unknown to me. As for my own joy, I will not try to say a single word of it. When I felt my <term>oblation</term> was accepted, the inner delight I experienced was too great, too immense, and too sweet for speech to approach it. Another time I saw the Virgin; she exhorted me to know her more deeply; she blessed me, and she showed me the sorrow she suffered during the <term>Passion</term> <note>The "Passion" refers to the period of suffering, physical and spiritual, endured by Jesus leading up to and during his crucifixion.</note>.

<summary>Angela describes a transformative moment where the infant Jesus wakes and reveals his divine majesty, prompting her to offer herself and her deceased family entirely to God. The vision concludes with a separate encounter with the Virgin Mary, who shares the mystery of her suffering during the Passion.</summary>

<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Jesus Christ, Virgin Mary, oblation, divine majesty, mysticism, the Passion, spiritual surrender, motherhood</keywords>

# FORTY-SIXTH CHAPTER

## THE EMBRACE

---

One day I was caught up in spirit <note>original: "ravie en esprit" — a state of mystical ecstasy where the soul feels transported beyond the physical body to witness divine truths.</note>; drawn, elevated, and absorbed into the light without beginning or end, I saw things that cannot be told. During this influence, the image of the <term>God-Man</term> <note>original: "l'Homme-Dieu" — a theological term referring to Jesus Christ as being simultaneously fully human and fully divine.</note> appeared to me again, at the moment of the descent from the cross. The blood was recent, fresh, and red; it flowed from open wounds; it had just come out of the body. Then, in the joints, I saw such tearing, I saw the nerves so stretched, and the bones so dislocated by the effort of the executioners, that a sword pierced through me, and my inner self was struck <note>original: "mes entrailles furent percées" — literally "my entrails were pierced," an expression of deep, visceral emotional and physical pain.</note>; and, when I remember the pains I have suffered in my life, I find none that are equal to this one.
     I was there, absorbed in my grief; around the <term>Crucified One</term>, I perceived a devoted crowd, who preached in words and in deeds the poverty, the <term>opprobrium</term> <note>original: "l'opprobre" — public disgrace or shameful treatment. In Angela's Franciscan context, embracing social shame was a key part of following Christ.</note>, and the pain of the Crucified. This crowd was my spiritual sons <note>Having lost her biological family, Angela became a "spiritual mother" to a community of followers, particularly within the Third Order of St. Francis.</note>. Jesus called them, the...

<summary>Angela experiences a vivid, painful vision of the recently crucified Christ being taken down from the cross, which she feels physically within her own body. She then sees her "spiritual sons" following Christ’s example of poverty and suffering.</summary>

[He] drew them to himself, embraced them one by one with an immense love; then he took their heads in his hands, and gave them the sacred wound of his Heart to kiss. The way Jesus placed my children upon his Heart was not the same for all of them. For some of them he repeated it; for some it was more complete, and for others, less complete. ## FORTY-SEVENTH CHAPTER

### THE DEGREES

---

Another day, while I was attending a procession, I felt the attraction of the abyss <note>original: "l’abîme" — in mystical theology, this refers to the unfathomable, bottomless depth of God’s nature.</note>. The uncreated God <note>original: "Dieu incréé" — a theological term for God as the eternal source of all, who was not Himself created.</note> called me in that inexpressible manner of which I have spoken before.

I saw the God who is one in three persons <note>A vision of the Holy Trinity.</note>, and his majesty dwelt within the souls of my sons <note>These "sons" are the spiritual followers and disciples who gathered around Angela.</note>, transforming them into itself according to the degrees whose laws I have already observed. This sight was for me like a paradisiacal immensity. The very heart of God <note>original: "Les entrailles de Dieu" — literally "the entrails of God," a common medieval expression for the most visceral seat of divine mercy and compassion.</note> poured out upon my children, and I could not satisfy myself with seeing it. And the depth of the blessing falling upon their heads is a mystery beyond words <gloss>1</gloss>. Then I heard God [tell] them—

---

<note>
1. I, who write under her dictation <note>This refers to Brother Arnaldo, Angela's scribe and confessor.</note>, secretly contemplated her face; it was no longer a human face, it was something angelic, it was glorified joy. The sweetness and immensity of the blessing she had seen falling from heaven is too inexpressible to be honored by anything other than silence.

*(Note from the brother who wrote under Angela’s dictation.)*
</note>

to ask for something: it was the unreserved sacrifice, the entire, perfect holocaust <term>holocaust</term> <note>original: "holocauste" — in a medieval mystical context, this refers to a sacrifice that is completely consumed by fire, symbolizing a soul totally surrendered to God.</note> of their bodies and their souls.

Weigh this, my brothers, weigh it. How must one love, how must one serve this jealous God who wants to possess, this God who gives Himself, this God who asks? <note>Angela refers to the "jealousy" of God in the Biblical sense (Exodus 20:5), describing a divine love that demands total, exclusive devotion.</note>

I again had before my eyes the representation of the crucified God, with the tension of the joints that I had already seen. <note>Angela often describes the physical reality of the Crucifixion with intense, anatomical detail, emphasizing the suffering of the "God-Man."</note> He was carried through the air, and flew to wherever the procession walked; and this image followed us, without any human hand being there to support it. I saw my sons gathered together again, and their lips were pressed to the wound in His side; <note>The "wound in the side" (from the lance of Longinus) was a central focus of medieval devotion, seen as the entrance to Christ's heart and a source of life-giving blood.</note> and Jesus said to them:

This blood is the price of your redemption. This heart is the place of your residence. Do not fear, my children, to reveal through your words and actions this truth of my way and my life, which the wicked fight against; for I am always with you to help and succor you.”

On that day, and several other days, I saw the purification of my sons and the three degrees <note>In Angela's theology, these "degrees" represent stages of spiritual advancement or different ranks of intimacy with the divine.</note> that it comprises.

<summary>Angela describes a miraculous vision of a levitating crucifix following a procession and records Christ’s promise of redemption and purification to her spiritual followers.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the revelation given to Angela concerning the spiritual transformation of her followers, specifically detailing the three stages or "degrees" of purification that lead the soul toward total union with the divine.</meta>

->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-

The first purification is a great grace of strength which makes the absence of evil easy.

The second is a great grace of joy in the fulfillment of good.

The third is the fullness of perfection, and the transformation of the soul into God.

In all these graces of renewal, the soul receives an admirable beauty. The splendor of the second degree is immense and joyful. As for the third, it belongs to the realm of those excesses <note>original: "excès" — in mystical theology, this refers to *excessus mentis* or ecstasy, a state where the soul is "carried out" of itself beyond the reach of human language or reason.</note> that reduce me to silence. I can say nothing more of it.

The elect of the third degree appeared to me transformed into God, so that in them I see only Jesus, sometimes suffering, sometimes glorified; it seems to me that He has <term>transubstantiated</term> <note>original: "transsubstantiés" — Angela borrows this heavy theological term from the Mass (where bread and wine become the body and blood of Christ) to describe how the very "substance" of the person is replaced by the presence of God.</note> them and swallowed them up in His abyss.

<summary>Angela describes the three stages of spiritual purification, culminating in a state of "transubstantiation" where the soul is entirely transformed into and consumed by Christ.</summary>

# FORTY-EIGHTH CHAPTER

## THE LIGHT

In this same procession, we were approaching a church dedicated to the Blessed Virgin. Here is the Queen of grace and mercy who leaned down over her sons and daughters; at first, she was at an immense height. She leaned down and blessed them with an unknown blessing, and drawing them to her heart, she embraced them in varying degrees. One might have called them the outstretched arms of love. She was entirely luminous, and seemed to absorb them into herself within an immense light. Do not imagine that I saw arms of flesh: all of this was light, and a most marvelous light; the Virgin, pressing the children to her heart by the power of love, which issued from the depths of her being <note>original: "entrailles" — literally "entrails" or "womb." In medieval mystical tradition, this refers to the deepest, most visceral seat of compassion and maternal origin.</note>, absorbed them into herself.

<summary>Angela recounts a vision of the Virgin Mary appearing during a procession, where she transforms into a being of pure light and mercifully absorbs her followers into her own divine presence.</summary>

<keywords>Virgin Mary, Queen of Grace, divine light, mystical embrace, spiritual absorption, maternal love, procession, Foligno</keywords>

# FORTY-NINTH CHAPTER

## THE DEAD <note>While the title refers to "The Dead," the chapter specifically concerns those who have passed into the "glory" of heaven, focusing here on Saint Francis of Assisi.</note>

---

On another day, amidst a multitude of visions, Saint Francis appeared to me in glory. He greeted me with his usual salutation, which is this: "May the peace of the Most High be with you." <note>original: "Pax Altissimi sit tecum." This is a variation of the famous Franciscan greeting "Pax et Bonum" (Peace and All Good).</note> The voice of Saint Francis is always most pious, most humble, most gracious, and most tender.

Regarding those of my sons <note>Angela refers to the Franciscan friars of her time, whom she considered her spiritual children.</note> who observe the law of poverty with a fiery ardor, he greatly praised their intention and asked for its practical expansion. He added:

"May the eternal, perfect, and abundant blessing, received by me from God who is without beginning or end, fall upon the heads of these cherished children, your sons and mine: tell them to live according to the voice of Christ, and to manifest it in both words and actions. Let them not fear; for I am with them, and the eternal God is their support."

<summary>Angela recounts a mystical encounter with Saint Francis of Assisi, who appears in glory to bless his followers and exhort them to remain steadfast in their commitment to absolute poverty.</summary>

They ask you, my children, to be the luminous examples of their luminous life, and to follow—through poverty, contempt, and suffering—the path they followed. In all your acts let your intention be in the heavens, unchanging with Jesus and Jesus crucified. Whether you act, speak, or eat, be always

## FIFTIETH CHAPTER

### THE INVITATION

---

One day, I was praying to God that he would give me something of himself. And I made the sign of the cross upon myself. And I was also praying to him to show me who his children are. Among other answers, this parable <note>original: "exemple." In medieval spiritual literature, an "exemplum" or example was a moralizing story used to illustrate a point of doctrine.</note> was given to me:

“A man who has many friends prepares a banquet <note>original: "festin." In the mystical tradition, the banquet often symbolizes the Eucharist or the joys of Heaven.</note> with immense care and invites them; but many of them do not come. What will be the sorrow of the one who has prepared such an abundant banquet, and who has spent so immensely? But with what joy he receives those who do appear! He receives them all with rapture. But there are reserved places, places near to him, for his intimate friends: these ones eat with him, and drink from his cup.

— Lord,” I said with joy, “what is this banquet? When did you invite everyone? Oh! Tell me, tell me!” He replied: “I have invited all people to eternal life: that <note>The text breaks off here at the end of the page.</note>

“And these called ones,” I said then, “by what way did they come?”

“By the way of tribulation,” <note>term: tribulation. Derived from the Latin 'tribulum' (a threshing sledge), it refers to the purifying process of suffering and trial used to separate the "wheat" of the soul from the "chaff."</note> it was answered to me. “Virginity and chastity have their trials.” And he called by name the poverties and the sorrows of those whom he was showing me. I saw how virginity, how poverty acted upon the children of the Lord. I saw how suffering was converted into thanksgiving. <note>original: "action de grâces."</note> One does not understand at first, but afterwards one gives thanks. I saw the common path of the chosen for eternal life, and there is no other way.

# FIFTY-FIRST CHAPTER

## THE THREAT

---

One day I was in prayer <term>oraison</term> <note>oraison: A form of deep, contemplative mental prayer, distinct from reciting set vocal prayers.</note> in my cell, and I heard these words:

> "Those who have the Lord God as their enlightener <term>illuminator</term> <note>illuminator: A theological term for God when He provides direct spiritual light or understanding to a person’s soul.</note> see their specific path in the inner and spiritual light. But some of them close their ears for fear of hearing, and their eyes for fear of seeing. Not wanting to listen to the word of Him who speaks within the soul, even though they feel the divine sweetness from that direction, they turn away, despite the inner voice, and follow the common way <term>voie commune</term> <note>common way: This refers to a conventional or mediocre religious life, choosing to follow general rules rather than the specific, more demanding path God reveals to an individual soul.</note>. These shall be cursed by the Almighty God."

I heard this word, not just once but a thousand times. But, seized by a violent temptation <note>In this context, a "temptation" often refers to a spiritual trial or a doubt planted in the mind to lead the mystic away from the truth.</note>, I took this teaching for an illusion. "How," I said, "can there be a soul whom God enlightens with his light, whom he fills with his gifts, and yet, because she follows an ordinary path, he curses her?" This word seemed too terrible to me.

<vocab>Angela of Foligno, prayer, enlightener, inner light, soul, temptation, common way</vocab>

<summary>Angela receives a recurring divine warning that those who ignore their unique spiritual calling to follow a conventional life will be cursed, a message she initially rejects as an illusion due to its severity.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, illumination, divine threat, inner voice, spiritual discernment, temptation, common way</keywords>

I refused with horror to even listen to the voice that was speaking.

Then, out of compassion for my weakness, a <term>rough example</term> <note>original: "exemple grossier." Here is this parable.

> “A father wanted to make his son a scholar. The father spares nothing; he incurs enormous expenses. He magnificently provides the son of his love with everything necessary for the great figure he must cut in the world. When certain studies are completed under the direction of a first master, the father has his beloved moved to another residence, where another higher master gives him more sublime teachings. But if the ungrateful disciple, neglecting the high science, goes off to work in the workshop of an artisan, and forgets at a <term>laborer’s</term> <note>original: "mercenaire." This refers to someone who works only for worldly wages, representing the soul abandoning spiritual wisdom for mundane, material concerns.</note> place what he held from the wisdom of his master and the magnificence of his father, the father will be overwhelmed by a grief and an indignation proportioned to the greatness and depth of his betrayed love.”

The son is the soul which, enlightened first by preaching and by Scripture, is admitted into the sanctuary where the word of God resounds; it sees in the spiritual light how it must...

He gives <term>high science</term> <note>original: "la haute science." But if this beloved one neglects God’s gift, if he <term>hardens himself</term> <note>original: "s'encroûte," literally "becomes encrusted." This describes a soul becoming opaque and unresponsive to divine grace.</note>, if he grows heavy, if he repels this light which is his own, and the knowledge of God and His inspiration, God withdraws the light from him and gives him His curse.

I received the order to write these words and to show them to the brother who was hearing my confession, because they concern him personally. <note>This "brother" is Arnaldo, Angela's relative and scribe, who recorded her visions. God often used Angela to give Arnaldo direct instructions or warnings.</note>

On another day, God spoke to me and said: “There is a class of men who know the Lord only by the goods they receive from Him. These know Him little. Another class of men, who also possess this knowledge, possess another more intimate one. These feel the essential goodness of the Lord in their depths.”

In another conversation, I received a light, and I heard a voice crying out, and in the cries I distinguished these words:

->“Oh! how great they are! how great they are!”<-

This word was spoken to me often. It appeared to me in its truth and was shown to me in an immense light.

# FIFTY-SECOND CHAPTER

## THE SIGNS

---

It is important to know by what signs one can recognize the presence of God in the soul, and recognize it with certainty.

Sometimes he arrives without being called or asked, and brings with him a fire, a love, and a <term>sweetness</term> <note>original: "suavité." In mystical writing, this refers to a gentle, pleasant spiritual sensation that brings peace and comfort to the soul.</note> previously unknown. In this fire, the soul gathers joy and believes it recognizes the presence and the work of God; but certainty is still lacking. The soul sees that God is within it, even though it does not see him there, when it feels his grace and the joy of his grace. But none of this is certainty. The soul feels the arrival of God when it hears sweet words carrying their own delight with them, when it feels the <term>Divinity</term> through a delicious <term>touch</term> <note>original: "attouchement." This is a technical term in mysticism describing a direct, non-physical contact between God and the soul, often felt as an interior sensation of God’s substance.</note>; yet a doubt may still remain, a slight doubt. The soul does not yet know perfectly and absolutely if God is within it; for another spirit can bring these feelings with it. This doubt comes either from the de-

I understood the lesson, and returned to silence. <note>This reflects a common experience for mystics; when their private revelations seemed too strange or potentially heretical, their confessors directed them back to the safety of established Bible teachings.</note>

# FIFTY-THIRD CHAPTER

## HOSPITALITY

---

We have just said how the soul recognizes within itself the presence of God. But we have said nothing of the welcome it gives Him, and all that precedes is a small thing compared to the moment when the soul recognizes God as its guest.

When the soul has given hospitality to the stranger who comes within it, it enters into such a profound knowledge of the infinite goodness of the Lord that, often recollected in my innermost depths, I have known with certainty that the more one has the feeling of God, the less one can speak of Him. The more one has the feeling of the infinite and the <term>ineffable</term> <note>original: "l'indicible." In mystical tradition, this refers to an experience so profound that it transcends the boundaries of human language.</note>, the more one lacks words; for compared to what one wishes to convey, words are pitiful.

If a preacher were introduced there, if he felt what I have sometimes felt, his lips would close; he would no longer dare to speak, he would keep silent, he would become mute. God is too far above the intellect and everything else; He is too

As for me, I feel and I affirm that all the words uttered by the mouths of men since the beginning of the ages, and even the words of <term>Holy Scripture</term>, have not touched the marrow of divine goodness, and are not, before this goodness, even what a grain of millet is compared to the vastness of the universe.

# FIFTY-FOURTH CHAPTER

## THE ILLUSIONS

---

But those who lead a spiritual life can sometimes fall into illusion. One of the causes of error, and the greatest, is an impure love, mixed with <term>self-love</term> <note>original: "amour-propre." In spiritual literature, this refers to a self-centered or ego-driven attachment that masks itself as virtue.</note> and self-will; this love has, to a certain extent, the spirit of the world.

Thus, the world approves and encourages it. This approval is a trap; this encouragement is a lie. In this state, the person whom the world sees and approves seems to burn with love; they have certain tears, certain sweetnesses, certain tremblings, and certain cries which bear the hallmarks of spiritual impurity. But these tears and these sweetnesses, instead of coming from the depths of the soul, are phenomena that occur in the body; this love does not penetrate the heart; this sweetness vanishes quickly, is easily forgotten, and produces bitterness. I have had these experiences; I lacked <term>discernment</term> <note>original: "discernement." The ability to judge whether a spiritual impulse or feeling comes from God, the human ego, or a deceptive influence.</note> then. I had not yet reached the certain possession of the truth.

When love is perfect, the soul, after having

...felt God, she feels her own portion, which is <term>nothingness</term> <note>original: "le néant." She nevertheless calls the saints to her aid; for from the depths of her abyss she does not dare speak to God: she invokes the Virgin and the saints. If in this state one addresses a praise to you, the thing has the effect of a bad joke. This upright and unmixed love enlightens the soul regarding its faults at the same time as regarding the goodness of God. The tears and the sweetnesses that occur then, instead of breeding bitterness, breed joy and security. This love introduces Jesus Christ into the soul, and the absence of all illusion becomes for her then a matter of experience.

Here is another illusion into which God sometimes allows <term>interior souls</term> <note>original: "âmes intérieures." Those who prioritize the life of the spirit and deep contemplative prayer over external religious rituals.</note> to fall. When a person devoted to the Spirit feels God's love for them, experiences, performs, and recounts the works of the Spirit, if they exceed the measure of prudence, if this soul loses fear, God allows them to fall into some illusion, in order to know who they are, and who He is.

Here is yet another cause of error.

A soul is on the path of pure love; she feels God; her hands are pure, her heart is pure; she renounces the esteem of the <term>world</term> <note>original: "siècle." In mystical writing, this often refers to secular or worldly life as opposed to the spiritual life.</note>; she renounces being seen as a saint; she wishes to please only Christ entirely; she places herself entirely within Christ, she dwells in Him; she experiences unutterable joy, she feels the fire of God.

Oh! let her then render unto herself what is her own, and unto God what is God’s! Otherwise God allows her to be mistaken; He allows it to protect her, He allows it so that she does not slip away from Him; for He loves her with a jealous love. He plunges her into an abyss where she finds <term>two sciences</term> <note>original: "deux sciences." Here "science" refers to a profound, structured knowledge or spiritual understanding.</note>: the knowledge of herself and the knowledge of God. Here, there is no longer any room for error; the soul sees pure truth. In this contemplation, she experiences such fullness that she does not see herself capable of a more immense <term>rapture</term> <note>original: "ravissement." A state of being seized by divine ecstasy.</note>. Absorbed first in the sight of herself, she closes herself to every other thought, every other memory.

Suddenly, divine goodness appears to her. Then she sees both abysses simultaneously, and the manner of her vision is a secret between her and God.

But that is not all. God, who is jealous, further allows her <term>tribulations</term> <note>original: "tribulations." These are the trials or sufferings that a soul must endure to remain humble and purified.</note>.

<summary>Angela explains how God, out of a "jealous love," allows a soul to experience trials and a deep awareness of its own nature to prevent the error of pride. This process leads to the "two sciences"—the simultaneous profound knowledge of one's self and of God.</summary>

# FIFTY-FIFTH CHAPTER

## POVERTY OF SPIRIT

There is a safeguard that removes all room for <term>illusion</term> <note>original: "illusion." In this context, it refers to spiritual self-deception or "false lights" where a soul believes it is following God but is actually following its own ego.</note>. This safeguard is poverty of spirit. One day, I heard a divine word that recommended poverty of spirit to me as a light, and as a happiness that surpasses all conceptions of human understanding.

Here is what the Lord says:

> “I, if poverty had not been so happy, I would not have loved it; and if it had been less glorious, I would not have taken it. For pride can only find a place in those who possess or believe they possess. Man and angel fell, and fell through pride; for they believed they possessed. Neither man nor angel possesses anything. Everything belongs to God. <term>Humility</term> <note>original: "humilité." Angela treats humility not just as a feeling, but as a realistic recognition of one's total lack of independent existence or "possession."</note> dwells only in those who see themselves bereft of everything. Poverty of spirit is the supreme good.”

...such poverty that there never has been and never will be a poor man equal to Him. And yet, <term>Being</term> <note>original: "l'Etre." He possesses substance, and it belongs to Him so completely that this belonging is beyond human speech. And yet God made Him poor, as if substance had not belonged to Him.

This is madness in the eyes of sinners and the blind. The wise call the same thing by another name. This truth is so profound—poverty is so truly the root and mother of all humility and all happiness—that the abyss where I see this cannot be described. The poor man can neither fall nor perish through <term>illusion</term> <note>original: "illusion." Angela uses this to mean spiritual self-deception or the prideful belief that one’s spiritual gifts originate from oneself rather than God.</note>. If a person could see the good of poverty, the love of God would fall upon them; if you considered the immense value of this treasure, and how it attracted the heart of God, you could no longer keep anything perishable nor have anything as your own—nothing.

Such is the teaching of divine Wisdom which shows man his emptiness, his poverty, and presents him to himself in a mirror that does not lie, destitute of all merit and all good; then Wisdom gives him the gift of light, and with that light, the love of poverty. Then the soul sees the divine goodness, and finding nothing to love in itself, it turns entirely...

<note>Vocabulary from the original text: poverty, Being, substance, God, sinners, the wise, humility, happiness, divine Wisdom, light, divine goodness, soul</note>

<summary>Angela explains that Christ is the ultimate example of poverty because He renounced all despite owning "Being" itself, and she argues that recognizing one's own total emptiness is the only way to attract God's love.</summary>
<keywords>poverty, Being, Christ, divine Wisdom, humility, spiritual emptiness, divine goodness, spiritual illusion</keywords>

...entirely to loving the Almighty God; she acts according to her love, having lost all confidence in herself and placed all her confidence in God. In this confidence she finds illumination, through which doubt is cast out. Anyone who possessed this truth would be inaccessible to every diabolical or human <term>illusion</term> <note>In Angela’s writings, "illusion" refers to spiritual self-deception or the tricks of the devil that lead a person to believe they are holy when they are actually full of pride.</note>; for the spirit of poverty illuminates the soul with an immense light, and by this light all of life appears to her, with all its inner workings, and illusion becomes impossible.

I have seen this light; I have seen that poverty, the mother of virtues, is the first thing to come from the lips of divine Wisdom. Divine Wisdom told us through the incarnation of the <term>Word</term> <note>original: "le Verbe." This refers to Christ, the "Logos" or eternal Word of God, becoming human.</note>: "You are mortal"; through poverty of spirit she tells us: "You are blessed."

That is why all human wisdom that does not enter into this truth is a nothingness that leads to hell. And all the wise men of the world, if they do not enter into this truth, are nothingnesses going to hell. And when the soul sees this truth, it acts without <term>vainglory</term> <note>original: "vaine gloire." This is the prideful desire for the esteem of others; Angela argues that true poverty of spirit makes such pride impossible because the soul realizes it has nothing of its own to brag about.</note>, and without reflecting back upon itself.

---

<summary>Angela describes how poverty of spirit grants a divine light that protects the soul from deception and exposes the emptiness of worldly wisdom. She concludes that poverty is the essential truth of the Incarnation, leading the soul to act for God without pride.</summary>
<keywords>poverty of spirit, illumination, divine Wisdom, Incarnation, humility, vainglory, spiritual truth</keywords>

# FIFTY-SIXTH CHAPTER

## THE ECSTASY

---

Everything that the soul conceives or grasps while it is enclosed within its narrow limits is nothing compared to ecstasy <note>original: "ravissement." In mystical theology, this refers to a state of being "carried away" or "seized" by God, where the physical senses are often suspended.</note>. But when the soul is raised above itself, illuminated <note>original: "illustrée." In this context, it means the soul is filled with spiritual light or "enlightened" by a divine presence.</note> by the presence of God, when God and the soul have entered into one another’s depths, she conceives, she enjoys, and she rests in those divine joys that she cannot describe. They overwhelm all words and all concepts. It is there that the soul swims in joy and in knowledge; enlightened at the source of light, she penetrates the obscure and difficult words of <term>Jesus Christ</term> <note>Jesus Christ: Angela’s mysticism is deeply "Christocentric," meaning it focuses on the person of Jesus, particularly his suffering on the Cross, as the ultimate key to understanding God.</note>. She also understands why and in what manner a sorrow without alleviation <note>original: "adoucissement." This refers to a lack of comfort or relief; Angela emphasizes that Christ’s suffering was pure and unmitigated by any human or divine consolation.</note> inhabited the soul of Christ.

My soul, thus illuminated and transformed into the suffering <term>Jesus Christ</term>, sought to see if there was any alleviation there, and found that there was none at all. When my soul gathers herself in the

# FIFTY-SEVENTH CHAPTER

## KNOWLEDGE OF GOD AND OF ONESELF

***

I am a blind woman, I walk in the darkness. The truth is not in me. Be suspicious, O my children, of the words of this sinner, and follow them only when they resemble the footprints of <term>Jesus Christ</term> <note>Angela frequently uses the imagery of "footprints" or "vestiges" to describe following the humble and suffering path of Christ's life.</note> and place your feet in the place where he placed his feet.

My children, I am no longer inclined to write, but to weep. When will I finally weep for my sins and their terrible redemption? When will I weep for the <term>Passion</term> <note>Passion: Referring to the physical and spiritual suffering of Jesus leading up to and during his crucifixion.</note> of the Son of God, of the Just One, the <term>Passion</term> of the <term>Immaculate</term> <note>Immaculate: Meaning "without stain"; referring to the sinless purity of Christ.</note>? But you write to me! I am obliged to write in order to answer you. What I say to you is the most recent impression of my heart. Know that nothing is necessary for you—nothing, except God. To find God, to gather your powers within Him, that is the one thing necessary. For this recollection it is necessary to cut away every superfluous habit, every superfluous familiarity with creatures—whoever they may be—every superfluous knowledge,

<vocab>Angela of Foligno, knowledge of God, sinner, Jesus Christ, Passion, recollection, one thing necessary</vocab>

<summary>Angela of Foligno humbles herself as a "blind sinner," urging her followers to ignore her own merits and instead focus entirely on the suffering of Christ and the "one thing necessary"—finding God by detaching from all worldly distractions.</summary>

<keywords>humility, penitence, Passion of Christ, spiritual recollection, detachment, Angela of Foligno, asceticism</keywords>

...all superfluous curiosity, all superfluous operation and occupation. In a word, man must separate himself from everything that divides. He must, by trying to penetrate the abyss of his miseries, <term>recollect</term> <note>Recollection (*recueillement*) is a core mystical practice involving the withdrawal of the senses and mind from external distractions to focus entirely on the internal presence of God.</note> himself in his past, in his present, and in the probabilities of his eternal future. 

Let this be done every day, or at least every night. Since man turns and returns his heart, let him strive to penetrate into the knowledge of the God of mercies, in the <term>dispensation</term> <note>Dispensation: In this context, the divine arrangement or the "handing out" of God's grace and mercy to humanity.</note> of His supreme pity, realized by **Jesus Christ** in the face of all our miseries; let him watch over his memory, so that it preserves the remembrance of the infinite benefit.

To know oneself! To know God! This is the perfection of man, and I have no desire to say or write anything outside of these two phrases: **To know oneself! To know God!** Contemplate your prison—a prison with no exit—and if a man does not find happiness in this prison, let him appeal to another <note>Angela implies that if one finds no peace within their own limited human nature (the "prison"), they must look outward and upward to God for rescue.</note> and not rest upon his <term>pallet</term> <note>Original: "grabat." O my dear children, visions, revelations, contemplations—all are nothing without the true knowledge of God and of self: I tell you in truth, without this, nothing is of any value. Thus, I ask myself why you desire my letters, since my letters can do nothing for your...

O perfection of knowledge!

O God! When the soul plunges into the bottomless abyss of the divine height—which I blaspheme <note>Original: "que je blasphème." But the King of kings—although an immense and continuous sorrow had already stretched Him upon the cross since His conception <note>Angela reflects a common medieval mystical theme: that Christ, in His divine foreknowledge, experienced the pain of the Crucifixion throughout His entire earthly life.</note>—instead of a purple bed and a golden carpet, when the hour of His death arrived, He found Himself before this cross, so vile and so abominable that He could only be supported and attached to it by means of the nails that pierced Him; the nails in His feet and the nails in His hands were necessary to hold Him, otherwise He would have fallen. Instead of eager servants, He had the henchmen of the devil, striving to make the torment more cruel, and helping the torture to penetrate more deeply into His innermost being; and they refused Him the single drop of water that He asked for, and that He asked for with a cry.

Oh! my God, when the soul sees these things, when she sinks into the contemplation of her misery, when she knows herself as she is—she who has cast herself into infinite misery, who has deserved eternal punishments, who has become the laughingstock of God, of the angels, and of the demons...

As for divine grace, everyone can have and find it; and man is without excuse; for God, in His munificence, gives it generously to those who want it and seek it.

I desire, my son, that your heart be empty of everything that is not the eternal God, His knowledge and His love, and that your spirit does not—

If this matter is too high for you, at least possess and keep the knowledge of the Crucified God; if this second vision is withdrawn from you like the first, refuse rest, my son, until you have found and reconquered one or the other of these two satiations <note>original: "rassasiements" — refers to the spiritual state of being "filled" or "fed" by a divine vision until one is satisfied</note>. Listen again, my son, and believe firmly what I am about to tell you.

He who seeks the path and the way to approach God, he who wishes to enjoy God in this world and the next, let him know God in truth, not from the outside and superficially; let him not stop at words spoken or written, or at analogies drawn from created things. This way of knowing, which relates to human speech, is a knowledge without depth. One must know God in truth through a profound intelligence of His absolute value, His absolute beauty, His absolute height and sweetness, and His virtue, goodness, liberality <term>liberality: a traditional virtue referring to God's generous and free giving of grace</term>, mercy, and tenderness; one must know Him as being the Sovereign Good <term>Sovereign Good: a translation of the Latin "Summum Bonum," the highest, most supreme good from which all other goods flow</term>, in the absolute. The wise man and the common <note>original: "vulgaire" — used here in the Latin sense of "vulgus," referring to the ordinary person who lacks deep spiritual discernment, rather than someone "crude"</note> man both know, but very differently. He who possesses wisdom knows the thing in its depth and in its reality; the other, in its appearance. The common man, who finds...

<summary>Angela distinguishes between superficial knowledge of God gained through words and creatures versus a profound, essential knowledge of God as the Sovereign Good. She exhorts her disciple to persist in seeking spiritual "satiation" through the contemplation of the Crucified God.</summary>

...a precious stone, appreciates and desires it for its brilliance and for its beauty, without looking any further; the wise man loves and desires it because, beyond its brilliance and its beauty, he sees its true value and its hidden virtue <term>virtue: in the Middle Ages, "virtue" often referred to the inherent power or medicinal property believed to exist within a stone or object</term>. Thus, the soul that possesses wisdom does not concern herself with knowing God through the superficial consideration of appearances. She wants to know Him in truth; she wants to experience <note>original: "expérimenter" — in a mystical context, this implies a direct, lived encounter rather than just an experiment</note> what He is worth, and to taste the flavor of His goodness; to her, He is not merely a "good," but the Sovereign Good <term>Sovereign Good: "Summum Bonum," the ultimate and highest good from which all other goodness flows</term>. And the Sovereign Good gives Himself to her, and the soul feels it: she tastes His sweetness and enjoys His delight; and the soul participates in the Sovereign Good. The soul, set ablaze by the power of love, is transformed into God, her love. ...stance; thus the soul, united to God by the perfect grace of love, is transformed into God without changing her own substance <term>substance: in philosophy and theology, this refers to the essential nature of what a thing is</term>, but by the virtue of the movement which carries her divinized life into God. Knowledge of God! Oh joy of joys, Lord! It is this knowledge that comes first, and love follows after—transforming love! Whoever knows in truth, that person loves in the fire <note>original: "aime dans le feu" — a common mystical image for the intense, purifying heat of divine love</note>.

Now, this profound knowledge the soul cannot have by herself, nor through the Holy Scriptures, nor through science <term>science: here meaning human learning or academic study</term>, nor through any created thing; these external things can prepare the soul for knowledge, but divine light and the grace of God alone can lead her into it. To obtain this knowledge from God—the Sovereign Good, Sovereign Light, and Sovereign Love—I know no surer or shorter way than a prayer that is pure, continual, humble, and violent <note>"Violent" in this context refers to a prayer of intense spiritual force, urgency, and passionate persistence, rather than physical harm</note>; a prayer that comes not only from the lips, but from the spirit and the heart, and from all the powers of the soul, and all the senses of the body; a prayer full of immense desires, which pleads and flings itself upon its object.

Let the soul who wishes to discover the Precious Stone and to know in truth and see the Light, pray, meditate, and continually read the book of life <term>book of life: Angela defines this not as a literal book, but as the lived example and "mortal life" of Jesus Christ</term>, which is the mortal life of Jesus Christ.

Our Father, the God most high, teaches and shows the soul the form, the method, and the way of knowledge—this way which is love; and this exemplar, this model, this archetype, it is in the Son that the Father reveals it.

In truth, there is no other way open to the children of God: there is no other means to find Him and keep Him than the life and death of Jesus Christ crucified: this is what I call the **book of life** <term>book of life: a metaphor common in mystical literature where Christ's suffering and actions serve as a "text" for the believer to study and imitate</term>. When she runs in this way, the soul feels not only delivered from the weight of the world and its crushing cares, but lifted toward divine delight and sweetness. Consumed and burned by the fire that God ignites, she is changed into Him: constant prayer finds everything in the sight of the cross.

# FIFTY-EIGHTH CHAPTER

## THE BOOK OF LIFE

***

Know that this book of life is none other than Jesus Christ, Son of God, the Word <term>Word: Originally "Verbe" (from the Latin *Verbum*), referring to Christ as the divine expression and reason of the Father</term> and wisdom of the Father, who appeared to instruct us by his life, his death, and his word. His life—what was it? It is the model offered to whoever desires salvation; now, his life was a bitter penance <note>original: "pénitence" — in the medieval context of Angela's writing, this refers not just to a sacrament but to a constant state of ascetic living and atonement</note>. Penance was his companion from the hour when, in the womb of the most pure Virgin, the soul created for Jesus entered into his body, until the final hour when his soul departed from that body by the most cruel death. Penance and Jesus never left one another.

Now, here is the company that the most high God, in his wisdom, gave to his beloved Son in this world: first, perfect, continual, and absolute poverty; next, perfect, continual, and absolute reproach <term>reproach: "opprobre" in the French, meaning extreme shame, disgrace, or public scorn</term>; finally, perfect, continual, and absolute suffering.

Such was the company that Christ chose upon

# FIFTY-NINTH CHAPTER

## FIRST COMPANION OF JESUS CHRIST

### POVERTY

The first companion of Jesus was a poverty that was continuous, perfect, and immense. It has three forms: the first is great; the second is greater and unites with the first; and the third, which, when joined to the first and the second, was perfect. 

Here is the first degree. Jesus was deprived <note>original: "destitué" — meaning to be completely stripped of or lacking</note> of all the goods of this world. He had neither land, nor vineyard, nor garden, nor property, nor gold, nor silver; he received no human assistance except in the measure strictly necessary for the relief of extreme indigence. He suffered hunger and thirst; he was wretched; he was cold and he was hot; he worked. Everything for him was austere and hard; he desired none of the refinements of life. He made use of the common and coarse things found in that province, where, without hearth or home <note>original: "sans feu ni lieu" — a French idiom for being homeless and having no fire for warmth or cooking</note>, he lived as a beggar. The second poverty, superior to the first, was the poverty of relatives and friends, and the distance from the great <note>referring to those of high social or political rank</note>,

<summary>Angela of Foligno describes the first of Christ's "companions," Poverty, detailing how Jesus lived in total destitution of worldly goods and comforts.</summary>

of the powerful, or of natural friendships: he had no one, neither on the side of his mother, nor on the side of Joseph, nor on the side of his disciples, who could spare him a slap, a hammer blow, a lash of the whip, or an insult. He willed to be born of a poor and humble mother; to be subject to a putative father <note>original: "père putatif" — a legal or commonly accepted father, referring here to St. Joseph</note>, a poor carpenter. He stripped himself of the love and familiarity of kings, high priests, scribes, friends, and relatives; he sacrificed nothing for the sake of human affection that would please, or could please, God.

But here is the supreme, sublime, and absolute poverty. Jesus Christ stripped himself of himself, and the Almighty showed himself poor. He showed himself as poor in power; he acted as if he were incapable. He clothed himself in misery and childhood; except for sin, he took on every sorrow. The journeys <note>original: "les courses" — referring to his travels and constant labors during his ministry</note>, the preaching, the healings, the visits, the reproaches—everything overwhelmed him, and he became well-acquainted with fatigue.

Not only did he give sinners power over him, but even the inanimate things and the elements which he had created with his own hand received the power to afflict him. He played the part of helplessness; he did not resist; he endured for our sake. He gave the thorns the power to penetrate and cruelly pierce that divine and thrice-formidable head.
