God, who is invincible by nature, the First Act, the Pure Act <note>The terms "First Act" and "Pure Act" (originally *Actus Purus*) are technical philosophical descriptions of God used in the Middle Ages. They suggest that God is complete and perfect existence, with no "potential" to be anything other than what He is—unlike humans, who are always changing or "becoming."</note>, made this universal submission to every creature and every pain. He did this to put to shame the fastidiousness of miserable man, who not only refuses penance and voluntary suffering, but pushes away with all his might the pain imposed upon him, and murmurs against God.

Jesus Christ imposed upon himself another kind of poverty. He stripped himself of his wisdom—the wisdom that belongs to him. He might have seemed like someone common, the most ignorant, the crudest of men. He did not take the stance of a philosopher or a scholar, of a great speaker, a writer, a man of science, or a famous sage; instead, he mingled with men in all simplicity and gentleness, showing them at the same time the way of truth through <term>thaumaturgic</term> virtue <note>original: "vertu thaumaturgique." This refers to the ability to work miracles or "wonders," from the Greek words *thauma* (wonder) and *ergon* (work).</note>. He, the wisdom of the Father and the God of sciences, master of the prophetic spirit—breathing it wherever he wills—could have burst forth with scientific and philosophical genius, displaying and glorifying himself. But he spoke the truth so simply that he was seen not only as a common man, but as a madman and a blasphemer. Will it be necessary to...

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Christ’s voluntary "stripping away" or poverty. On the previous page, Angela described how Christ set aside his intellectual majesty and divine wisdom to appear as a simple man. Here, she explains how he went further, abandoning even his reputation for holiness to be seen as a criminal.</meta>

->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-
<page-num>228</page-num>

...consequently inflate ourselves with our knowledge, seek to pass for masters, or beg from men a hollow name and an empty glory?

He stripped himself of his very self, even abdicated the glory of being holy, just, and innocent. This is the mystery of mysteries <note>The "mystery of mysteries" refers to the theological concept of *kenosis*, or the "self-emptying" of Christ, where God takes on the lowliest human form.</note>. He followed a mystical path so far removed from human expectation that instead of passing for the Saint of saints, he was held to be a sinner, a friend of sinners, a traitor, a deceiver, a conspirator, a public enemy, and a blasphemer, condemned and executed between two thieves. And yet, he alone could accomplish our salvation.

He could have inclined the world—He, the Saint of saints—before the glory of his holiness; He, the <term>Impeccable</term> <note>original: "l’Impeccable." In theology, "impeccability" is the absence of sin and the inability to sin. Angela highlights the paradox of the only sinless man being treated as the greatest sinner.</note>, who bore the sins of the people; He, the King of virtues and the God of saints. Instead of keeping the name of "Saint," he gave it to John the Baptist, his servant. But as much as he could without wounding truth and doctrine, he stripped himself of the appearance of holiness to put to shame the hypocrisy of us miserable creatures—we who seek appearances without having the reality, and who, by a thousand roundabout ways, falsifying facts and turning them to our advantage, run headlong after a glory that does not belong to us.

->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-

He further stripped himself of his very self by divesting himself of the dominion that belongs to him. He, the King of kings, the Lord of lords, whose reign shall have no end, lived among men as a slave. And indeed, he was sold; buyers were found. He was offered empire. He refused. He obeyed even unto death bad kings, paying the tribute, submitting to unjust judgments. And not only did kings find him defenseless, but their most base ministers and subjects were able to overwhelm him with blows and lay him upon the cross; and until the age of thirty, it was his mother and his <term>putative father</term> <note>original: "père putatif." This refers to Saint Joseph, who was the recognized earthly father of Jesus, though not his biological father according to Christian doctrine. Among his disciples, whom he chose few and poor, instead of conducting himself as a master, he declared that he had not come to be served, but to serve; finally, he gave his life for them, for sinners. In the midst of these poor disciples, if he was king and master, it was in the matter of misery, in hunger, in thirst, in pain; he was "jealous" and surpassed the others <note>In this context, Angela suggests Christ was "jealous" or "zealous" to be the most humble, competing with his followers only to see who could occupy the lowest position.</note>; ambitious for the lowest place, he served them at table and washed their feet. Oh, the immensity of our folly! After having seen this God made into a servant, we aspire, without order and without love, to vain greatness and empty positions of authority!

God, to whom everything belongs, in order to give us the love of poverty, was therefore absolutely poor—poor in fact, in spirit, and in truth, crushing the thoughts of earthly creatures through his poverty; and his poverty came from his love: this is why he was a beggar. Poor in money, poor in friends, poor in power, in wisdom, in reputation, and in dignity—poor in all things—he preached poverty, and he announced that it would judge the world. # CHAPTER SIXTY

## THE SECOND COMPANION OF JESUS CHRIST
### SELF-DENIAL

---

The second companion that Jesus Christ never left during his earthly life was shame; he continually bore the weight of voluntary and perfect reproach <term>reproach: derived from the French "opprobre," meaning public disgrace, infamy, or the state of being looked down upon by society</term>. He lived as a slave sold and never redeemed—not merely as an ordinary slave, but as one perceived to be wicked and depraved. He was burdened with reproaches, contempt, chains, blows, slaps, and bruises, without a trial and without a defender, like a wretch who is not even worth the trouble of being judged, whom they send, surrounded by thieves, to the most shameful and cruelest execution <note>original: "supplice," referring here to the physical agony of the cross and the public nature of the execution.</note>. If any mortal thought to honor him, he always escaped it, either by a word or by a deed, and instead took up the burden of shame, which he chose always, without ever deserving it. Without cause, without pretext, without occasion, men to whom he had done nothing but good,

Thus the Son of God made himself the form, the model, the master, and the teacher of this unknown science, which is the contempt for glory. When it is absent, let us not seek it. When it is present, let us not give ourselves to it; for he never sought his own glory, but the glory of his Father. He rejected and despised honors to such an extent that he cast himself down from the height of heaven to the feet of his disciples; he <term>emptied himself</term> <note>original French: "s’est anéanti." Oh, misery!

Who, then, today would choose the company he chose? Who would flee from honor and love contempt—the child of poverty—the humble state, the humble task, and all that is humble? Who would want nothingness and dishonor? Who does not desire esteem and praise for the good they possess or perform, in deed and word, or which they believe they possess and perform? In truth, everyone has gone astray, and no one is faithful—no one, not a single soul. If anyone were to stand firm, it would be because that person was a living member united to the <term>Head of the Body</term> <note>A common metaphor in mystical writing (derived from St. Paul) where the Church is a body, and Christ is its head. In the second case, if your heart is as innocent as your hands, bear the contempt with God's permission, and rejoice a thousand times more in this second case than in the first; all your shame, all your sorrow will become a weight of grace, and with grace, glory will increase. This acceptance of shame, endured though unmerited—this acceptance of poverty and suffering borne for the sake of God—enlarges holy souls. The example of Jesus Christ, fleeing what people seek and seeking what they flee, shows the path to greatness. His second companion was to him...

# SIXTY-FIRST CHAPTER

## THE THIRD COMPANION OF JESUS CHRIST

### SORROW

---

The third companion of Jesus Christ, more constant, more intimate than the other two <note>The "other two" companions previously discussed are Poverty and Contempt.</note>, was that sovereign sorrow which, from the hour when his soul was united to his body, never left the Son of God. At the first moment of the hypostatic union <note>The "hypostatic union" is a technical theological term referring to the union of Christ's divine and human natures in one single person.</note>, this soul was filled with supreme Wisdom. Being at once a wayfarer and a comprehender <note>In medieval theology, a "wayfarer" (viator) is a person still journeying through life toward God, while a "comprehender" (comprehensor) is one who has already attained the "beatific vision" or direct sight of God. Christ is uniquely described as being both at once.</note>, in the womb of his mother, Jesus began to feel sovereign sorrow: all the pains that his soul and his body were to bear for us, he knew them, he saw them, he weighed them, he grasped them in their entirety and in their detail. When death approached, he entered into agony. His certain knowledge of his upcoming death, envisioned in all its horrors, caused a nameless sadness to penetrate him: he sweated blood, and the earth drank this sweat. Thus the soul of Christ, foreseeing the Passion in the womb of his mother, already knew the an-

<summary>Angela introduces Sorrow as the third lifelong companion of Christ, arguing that because of his divine wisdom, he felt the full weight of his future suffering and the Passion from the moment of his conception.</summary>
<keywords>Sorrow, Hypostatic Union, Passion of Christ, Wayfarer and Comprehender, Agony in the Garden, Angela of Foligno, Incarnation</keywords>

<page-num>240</page-num>

immense anguish; however, the body was not yet associated with these tortures.

Jesus Christ saw in advance the movements of those infamous tongues, and every sound that each of them would produce, all his sufferings, his death, the shame and the pain, all the tortures for which he was born, for which he came among us; everything was present to him with a prophetic and unceasing presence, including every detail of the appointed time, the instrument used, and the prescribed measure. He saw himself sold, betrayed, captured, denied, abandoned, bound, struck, mocked, beaten, accused, blasphemed, cursed, scourged, judged, reproached, condemned, led to Golgotha <note>Golgotha is the hill outside Jerusalem where the crucifixion took place; the name is derived from the Aramaic word for "skull."</note>, like a thief, stripped, naked, crucified, dead, and pierced with a spear. Where did he dwell, if not in sorrow? He knew every hammer blow, every lash of the whip, every hole, every nail, every tear, and every drop of blood: he had counted in advance his sighs, his groans, his laments, and those of his mother. In this deep and continuous contemplation, how could the companion of his life—how could sorrow—have ever abandoned him?

Beyond the sorrows of the future, felt prophetically, those of the present were innumerable. At the hour of his birth, he was neither

As for the pains of the Passion, they are beyond human words and the suspicions <note>In this context, "suspicions" refers to the limited capacity of the human heart to even imagine or suspect the depth of such suffering.</note> of the heart. The pain of Jesus was multifaceted and ineffable <term>ineffable: that which is too great or sacred to be expressed in words</term>.

Let us speak first of his compassions. His compassion for the human race, whom he loved with an immense love, filled him with an acute and heartbreaking pain. It was not only a general compassion for the fallen and condemned human species; it was an immense compassion, specific to each individual. And he did not merely see the sins of each individual with a general glance; he measured exactly every sin and every punishment, in the past and in the future. Every person—past, present, or future—and every sin of each of those people, pierced with measureless pain Him who loved us with measureless mercy and compassion. It was this compassion—immense, appalling—which hurried Jesus toward the cross, toward death, toward the abyss of tortures. He wanted to redeem us! He wanted to relieve us!

One of the most forgotten sorrows of Jesus Christ was his compassion for himself. His innumerable tortures, and the ineffable <term>ineffable: that which is too great or sacred to be expressed in words</term> sorrow with which he saw himself threatened, meant that in looking at himself, his heart was torn apart. Seeing and considering that the mission he held from his Father was to bear the weight of all the sins and all the sorrows of the elect <term>the elect: those chosen by God for salvation, a central concept in medieval theology</term>, feeling that these terrible things were infallible, certain, inescapable, and that he was devoted body and soul to their embrace, he was seized, in looking at himself, with a heartbreaking pity.

Imagine the state of a man who could see with a prophetic and infallible view the most unheard of, the most ineffable sorrow approaching him, with the certainty of being struck, and who would have continually before his eyes the details of all his tortures: he would have pity on himself. But to what extent would this pity grow, if the foreseen and imminent pain were beyond all proportion,

<summary>This page describes Christ's "compassion for himself," a mystical concept where Jesus, foreseeing the full weight of the Passion and the sins he must carry, experiences a profound and heartbreaking self-pity for the agony he must endure for the sake of the elect.</summary>

The more admirable the divine dispensation was, the more piercing and heart-rending was the sorrow that resulted from it. It is their beginning and it is their end <note>original: "alpha... oméga". &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And if it is impossible for the intelligence to conceive the love by which he redeemed us, it is equally impossible to conceive the sorrow from which he suffered. This sorrow is a sanctuary that speech does not approach.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Among the supreme sorrows was the compassion of Jesus for his Mother, the most sweet Mary. He loved her above every other creature. Because of the crime without name, because of the deicide <term>Deicide: the act of killing a divinity; here, the execution of Christ</term>, perhaps God the Father would have damned the human race, if Jesus, as if he had for an instant forgotten all other pain, had not cried and wept in death, for us and toward God. <note>Angela suggests that Christ’s final intercession was so intense it momentarily eclipsed his own physical agony to stay God’s judgment on humanity.</note>

The sorrow of compassion for his apostles and disciples penetrated Jesus Christ. The apostles, the disciples, the women who had followed him, suffered horribly. Jesus, who loved them with an immense love, carried within himself the sorrow of the disciples, dispersed and persecuted.

Besides these sorrows, Christ bore a thousand others of a thousand natures. I could count four swords and four arrows upon his crucified body.

First, the villainous cruelty of those hardened hearts. They were there, all day long, obstinate, studious, and diligent, inventing and scheming: it was a competition to see who would find the darkest calumny <term>Calumny: the making of false and defamatory statements in order to damage someone's reputation</term> or the most atrocious torture to exterminate the Savior, his name, and his followers.

The malice and the abomination of this implacable anger that the executioners carried incessantly within them—each of their thoughts, their intentions, their inner iniquities—was a sharp dagger that pierced the soul of Jesus.

Then the wickedness and the duplicity of the tongues that shouted out. Each of the accusations, the calumnies <term>Calumny: the making of false and defamatory statements in order to damage someone's reputation</term>, the unjust resolutions, the curses; each of the blasphemies, each of the lies, each of the false testimonies, fell upon him, causing him a specific wound.

Finally, the barbaric work of his Passion, where they invented refinements of cruelty that terrify at first glance. How many tortures would the eye count that could count the violences he suffered—the brutalities, the blows to the face, the hair and the hairs of his beard pulled out, the spitting, the lashings! Above all, the nails. They were very large, square, and so poorly hammered <note>original: "mal battus"; this refers to the nails being roughly forged rather than smooth, leaving jagged edges</note>, that they presented on all their sides a thousand little splinters which pierced his feet and the hands that they tore, which tortured him with dreadful suffering. A pain above all pain resulted from the shape of these nails. Even if his feet and his hands had not been thus nailed to the wood, the Passion would still have been terrifying. But the nails themselves did not satisfy the executioners. They pulled his feet and his hands with such violence that they dislocated his body, broke his nerves <term>Nerves: in historical medical contexts, this often referred to tendons or sinews</term>, and counted his bones when they laid him on the hard wood and stretched him out horribly. # CHAPTER SIXTY-TWO

## PRAYER

---

The knowledge of the eternal God and of the crucified God-Man, which is absolutely necessary for the spiritual transformation of man, presupposes the diligent reading of the <term>book of life</term> <note>a mystical concept where the life and suffering of Christ are treated as a "text" to be studied and meditated upon</note>, the book where the life and death of <term>Jesus Christ</term> are written. Now, for this reading to be insightful <note>original: "intelligente," referring here to spiritual understanding rather than mere academic intellect</note>, it requires a <term>prayer</term> <note>the original French "oraison" refers to contemplative prayer or communion with God</note> that is devoted, pure, humble, fervent <note>original: "violente," suggesting an intense, forceful spiritual exertion or "holy violence" to reach God</note>, deep, and persistent. I am not speaking only of <term>vocal prayer</term> <note>prayers that are spoken aloud or recited</note>, I am speaking of <term>mental prayer</term>, that which springs from the heart and from all the united powers of the soul. Having spoken of the <term>book of life</term>, let us now speak of prayer.

Prayer is the force that draws God near, and the sanctuary where He is found. There are three kinds of prayers in the depths of which one meets the Lord: <term>bodily prayer</term>, <term>vocal prayer</term>, and <term>supernatural prayer</term>.

<term>Bodily prayer</term> involves the cooperation of the voice and the limbs; one speaks, one articulates, one...

<summary>In this chapter, Angela of Foligno explains that spiritual transformation requires "reading" the life of Christ through intense, multifaceted prayer. She introduces three specific categories of prayer: bodily, vocal, and supernatural.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Book of Life, mental prayer, vocal prayer, bodily prayer, supernatural prayer, spiritual transformation, mysticism</keywords>

...makes the sign of the cross; <term>genuflections</term> <note>the act of bending the knee in reverence</note> have their place in this prayer. This prayer, I never abandon. I once wanted to sacrifice it entirely to <term>mental prayer</term> <note>silent, inward prayer where the mind and heart focus on God without spoken words</note>. But sometimes sleep and laziness intervened, and I lost the spirit of prayer. This is why I no longer neglect <term>bodily prayer</term> <note>outward physical acts of devotion, such as kneeling or reciting set prayers</note>: it is the road that leads to the others.

But it must be performed with <term>recollection</term> <note>original: "recueillement," a state of focused inward stillness and spiritual concentration</note>. If you say: *Our Father*, consider what you say. Do not hurry to repeat the prayer a certain number of times. I only ask you not to imitate those poor little <term>simple women</term> <note>original: "bonnes femmes," often used to describe unlearned, pious women; here Angela critiques their focus on the quantity of prayers rather than the quality</note> who believe they have prayed well when they have prayed for a long time. It seems as if they have a certain task to perform, which will be paid according to the length and the quantity.

There is mental prayer when the thought of God so possesses the spirit that the person no longer remembers anything outside of their Lord. And if any thought that is not the thought of God enters the spirit, it is no longer mental prayer. This prayer stills the tongue, which can no longer move. The spirit is so full of God that there is no room in it for the thought of <term>creatures</term> <note>in mystical theology, "creatures" refers to anything that is not God—people, worldly concerns, or physical objects</note>.

Mental prayer leads to <term>supernatural prayer</term> <note>also called "infused contemplation," where the experience of God is given directly to the soul by grace, rather than through the person's own mental effort</note>. There is supernatural prayer when the soul,

...enraptured above itself by divine thought and fullness, the soul is transported higher than its own nature, entering into divine understanding more deeply than the nature of things allows, and finds light within this understanding. But the soul cannot explain the knowledge it draws from these sources, because everything it sees and feels is superior to its nature.

In these three types of <term>prayer</term> <note>original: "oraison," referring to the structured practice of communicating with the Divine</note>, the soul obtains a certain knowledge of itself and of God. It loves to the extent that it knows; it desires to the extent that it loves; and the sign of love is not a partial transformation, but an <term>absolute transformation</term> <note>the mystical process where the soul is completely changed and united with God</note>.

But this transformation is not continuous. Therefore, the soul applies itself entirely to seeking a new transformation and returning to <term>divine union</term> <note>the state of being one with God</note>.

Divine Wisdom loves order in all things, because it carries absolute order within itself. This <term>ineffable</term> <note>too great or extreme to be expressed or described in words</note> Wisdom has given <term>bodily prayer</term> <note>outward physical acts of devotion, such as kneeling or reciting set prayers</note> as a footstool for <term>mental prayer</term> <note>silent, inward prayer where the mind and heart focus on God</note>, and mental prayer as a footstool for <term>supernatural prayer</term> <note>a state of divine ecstasy where the soul is lifted beyond its natural capacity by God’s grace</note>. It willed that each thing be done in its own time, unless during mental or supernatural prayer an overwhelming joy occurs that absolutely seals the lips.

<summary>This page describes the pinnacle of spiritual life, where the soul is transformed by divine understanding that exceeds human nature. It establishes a hierarchy of prayer, viewing physical and mental devotion as necessary steps toward the silence of supernatural ecstasy.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, supernatural prayer, divine union, transformation, divine wisdom, hierarchy of prayer, ecstasy, mysticism</keywords>

--- Page 271 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions on prayer by Angela of Foligno, specifically addressing the necessity of total concentration and the progressive rewards of persistent contemplation.</meta>

<page-num>267</page-num>
->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-

Excepting, of course, the case of physical illness, one must give back to God what belongs to God, to the full extent of human strength, and watch over the soul’s rest so that no worldly worry approaches its divine peace.

The law of <term>prayer</term> <note>original: "oraison," referring to the deep, structured practice of communicating with God</note> is unity. It requires the whole of the man, and not just a part of him. Prayer demands the entire heart; and if one gives it only a part of the heart, one obtains nothing from it. The opposite happens in the acts of human life; if it is a matter of drinking or eating, or accomplishing anything at all, one must hold back one’s inner self. But, in prayer, one must give one’s whole heart if one wishes to taste the fruit of this tree; for temptation comes from a divided heart. Pray and pray assiduously. The more you pray, the more you will be enlightened; the deeper, more evident, and more sublime will be your contemplation of the <term>Sovereign Good</term> <note>original: "souverain bien," a philosophical and theological term for God as the ultimate and highest source of all goodness and desire</note>. The deeper and more sublime the contemplation, the more ardent will be the love; the more ardent the love, the more delicious will be the joy, and the more immense the understanding. Then you will feel the inner capacity to understand increase within you; next, you will reach the fullness of light, and you will receive knowledge of which your nature was not capable, the secrets that are above you.

<summary>This page emphasizes that true prayer requires a totally undivided heart, contrasting it with worldly activities where a person remains internally reserved. Angela describes a spiritual cycle where persistent prayer leads to enlightenment, love, and a divine understanding that exceeds natural human capacity.</summary>

--- Page 272 ---
<meta>This page continues the teachings of Angela of Foligno on the practice of prayer, transitioning from the necessity of an undivided heart to the specific example set by Jesus Christ as the master and model of "oraison."</meta>

<page-num>268</page-num>
->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-

Of this glorious <term>prayer</term> <note>original: "oraison," referring to deep, contemplative communication with God</note> we find the science, the model, and the form in Jesus Christ, true God and true man, who taught both by word and by deed. He taught us prayer when he said to the disciples:
> “Watch and pray, lest you enter into temptation.”

In a thousand places in the Gospel, he recommended prayer to all our respects. He showed that it was the nourishment of his heart. It is advised to us by Him who loves us without falsehood, and who wishes us every good. To remove every excuse from those who refuse grace, having placed upon our prayer the promise of omnipotence:
> “Ask, and you shall receive”;

he wished to pray himself to draw us to where he is, to align our love with his own.

The Evangelist tells us that at the height of a long prayer, a sweat of blood issued from his body and flowed upon the earth. Place this spectacle before your eyes: look upon the model of prayer, and remember that he prayed, not for himself, but for you:
> “Father, if it is possible, let this <term>cup</term> <note>original: "calice," the chalice of suffering in the Garden of Gethsemane</note> pass from me. Nevertheless, let your will be done, and not mine.”

See and imitate the submission of this prayer.

He prayed when he said:
> “Father, I commit my spirit into your hands.”

In a word, her <term>prayer</term> <note>original: "oraison," referring to deep, contemplative communication with God</note> lasted as long as her life, which was prayer, knowledge, and revelation.

Do you think that Christ prayed in vain? Why do you neglect the thing without which everything is impossible? Since Jesus Christ, true God and true man, prayed to give you an example, if you want anything from him: pray, pray, pray—otherwise, nothing. If the true God wished to receive only by asking humbly, will you—a miserable creature—receive without asking, and without asking on your knees? Therefore, pray.

You know, dear child, that without light and without grace <term>salvation</term> is not possible. Divine light is the principle, the middle, and the center of all perfection.

Do you want to begin the journey? Pray. Do you want to grow? Pray. Do you want the mountain <note>Likely referring to the mountain of spiritual ascent or perfection</note>? Pray. Perfection? Pray. <page-num>271</page-num>

[...] take care only to redouble [your prayer]! It is prayer that delivers, it is prayer that illuminates, it is prayer that purifies, it is prayer that unites one to God. <term>Mental prayer</term> <note>original: "oraison"; referring to silent, meditative communication with God rather than recited vocal prayer</note> is the manifestation of God and of man. This manifestation is perfect humility, which resides in the knowledge of God and of self. Deep humility is the source from which divine grace flows to pour itself into the soul where it wishes to enter and grow. Follow this chain of reasoning. The more grace digs the abyss of humility, the more grace itself grows, leaping up from the bottom of this abyss, becoming all the higher as the abyss is deeper: the more grace grows, the more the soul digs the abyss of humility, and it lies down there as if in a bed, and it sinks into prayer, and the divine light grows in the soul, and grace digs the abyss, and the height and the depth give birth to one another.

Such are the fruits of the <term>book of life</term> <note>A common medieval metaphor for Christ, whose life and suffering provide the "text" for the believer to study and emulate</note>.

To know the "everything" of God and the "nothingness" of man—that is perfection. I have just described the road that leads there. Therefore, dear son, reject all laziness and negligence.

I have one more piece of advice to give you. If the grace of <term>sensible fervor</term> <note>original: "ferveur sensible"; the emotional "sweetness" or tangible feelings of joy and warmth often felt during devotion</note> is taken away from you, be just as diligent in prayer and in action as you were in the days of great ardor. Your prayers, your cares, your labors, your works are very...

<summary>Angela describes a mystical "chain" where humility and grace deepen each other, defining perfection as the dual knowledge of God’s totality and man’s nothingness. She advises the reader to persevere in prayer even when they no longer feel emotional or "sensible" spiritual joy.</summary>

If divine grace pushes you to prayer and to action, follow it, so long as you have the fire. But if through your fault—for this is how the withdrawals of love most often happen—if, through your fault, or by some greater design of the eternal mercy which is preparing you for something sublime, the <term>sensible fervor</term> <note>original: "ardeur sensible"; the tangible emotional warmth or "sweetness" one feels during religious devotion</note> is for a moment withdrawn from you, persist in prayer, in watchfulness, persist in charity; and if tribulation, if temptation arise with their purifying force, continue, continue, do not slacken; resist, fight, triumph, by dint of persistence and violence: God will return to you the ardor of his flame; do your part, he will do his. 

The <term>violent prayer</term> <note>original: "prière violente"; a mystical concept referring to an intense, forceful effort of the will to reach God when one feels spiritually "dry" or abandoned</note> that one wrenches from their own depths by tearing them open, is very powerful before God. Persevere in prayer; and if you begin to feel God more fully than ever, because your mouth has just been prepared for a divine flavor, <term>empty yourself</term> <note>original: "faites le vide"; the practice of clearing the mind of all worldly thoughts and self-will to create a space for the divine presence</note>, empty yourself; leave all the room to him: for a great light is going to be given to you to see yourself and to see him.

<summary>Angela explains that prayer is most meritorious when performed during times of spiritual "dryness" or trial. She encourages the seeker to use "violent prayer" to persist until divine light and fervor return.</summary>

Do not surrender yourself to anyone before you have learned to separate yourself from everyone.

Watch over your ardors, test the spirit that gives them to you. Take care not to abandon yourself to the one who brings ruins. Examine where the fire begins, where it leads you, and where it will lead you. Compare your inspirations to the <term>book of life</term> <note>original: "livre de vie"; for Angela, this refers to the life, poverty, and suffering of Jesus Christ, which serves as the ultimate "textbook" for a Christian's conduct</note>; follow them as long as it authorizes them, but no further.

Distrust those with a devout appearance who have only honeyed words in their mouths. Quick to put forward the divine communications with which they are favored, they set a trap for you to draw you to them, and the spirit of malice is there.

Distrust, oh! distrust the appearances of holiness; distrust, distrust the displays of good works. Take care that you are not dragged into the unworthy path of appearances. Look, look again; test all things, compare them to the book of life, and walk only when it allows it.

Distrust those who claim to have the <term>spirit of liberty</term> <note>original: "l'esprit de liberté"; likely a reference to the "Heresy of the Free Spirit," a contemporary movement that claimed a person could reach a state of perfection where they were no longer bound by moral laws or the Church</note>, but whose life is the living contradiction of Christianity. Founder of the law, Jesus Christ submitted himself to it. Free, he made himself a servant: his disciples must not seek liberty in the license that breaks divine law.

This illusion is frequent. There are great Christians who draw a circle around themselves, and a sublime order is inscribed within this circle. This order comes from the Holy Spirit, who makes them live, who leads them by the hand. For them, it is not a matter of knowing whether a thing is permitted or forbidden. There are certain things permitted in themselves from which the Holy Spirit steers them away, because they are not included in the immense order inscribed within the circle.

# SIXTY-THIRD CHAPTER

## HUMILITY

---

Prayer is empty without humility; after prayer, humility is the first necessity of man. Blessed children of the Lord, look upon the crucified Christ as the model <note>original: "type"; in this context, it refers to an archetype or the perfect pattern to be imitated</note> of humility, and let the form of all perfection be engraved within you. See His path, see His doctrine; it is not supported by empty words, but founded upon works and confirmed by miracles. With all the strength of your soul, follow Him who, while being in the bosom of the Father, emptied Himself <note>original: "s’est anéanti"; a reference to the theological concept of *kenosis* from Philippians 2:7, describing Christ's voluntary renunciation of divine privilege to become human</note>, took the role of a servant, humbled Himself even unto death, and was obedient even to the cross.

In Himself He established the supreme model of humility; it is there that He placed His heart, and He asked us to fix our eyes upon Him when He said: "Learn from me, for I am meek and humble of heart." <note>A quotation of Matthew 11:29</note>

O my children, look, see the importance and the necessity of this thing; see its root,

<summary>Angela of Foligno presents humility as the essential foundation for prayer and the Christian life, pointing to the "self-emptying" and obedience of Jesus Christ as the ultimate pattern for the soul.</summary>
<keywords>humility, Jesus Christ, prayer, obedience, kenosis, Matthew 11:29, Angela of Foligno, spiritual perfection</keywords>

look upon its foundations. Through a deep and savory <note>original: "savoureuse"; in mystical theology, this refers to a "tasting" or experiential knowledge of divine truths, related to the Latin *sapientia* or wisdom</note> contemplation, descend into this abyss, and cast your gaze toward this sublimity. Listen well. He does not say: "Learn the humility of the apostles; learn it from the angels." No. He says: "Learn it from me. My majesty alone is high enough for my humility to be at the bottom of the abyss." <note>Angela frequently uses the "abyss" to describe the infinite distance between God's greatness and the soul's nothingness.</note>

He does not say: "Learn from me to fast," despite the example of the forty days and forty nights. He does not say: "Learn from me the contempt of the world; learn from me poverty," although He performed and advised these things. He does not say: "Learn from me how I created the heavens." He does not say: "Learn from me to perform miracles," although He performed them by His own power, and He commanded the disciples to do them in His name. He never says: "Learn this from me." He says it only on one occasion: "Learn humility." In other words: "If I am not in fact and in truth the model <note>original: "type"; as on the previous page, this refers to Christ as the archetype of the virtue</note> of humility, look upon me as a liar." And He returns to this subject in a stunning manner, to force our attention. After having washed with His hands—His own hands—the feet of His disciples: "Do you know," He says, "what I have just done? If I, Master and Lord, have washed your feet, [you] must..." <note>This refers to the scene in John 13:12–15 where Jesus washes the feet of the Apostles to demonstrate servanthood.</note>

Truly, truly, the Savior of the world has placed gentleness and humility at the base of the virtues. Abstinence, fasting, austerity, internal or external poverty, good works, miracles—all are nothing without humility of heart. The stalk and the branches proceed only from the root. Because its value is infinite, because it is the foundation upon which all spiritual perfection rises, the Lord wished to entrust to no one but Himself the task of telling us: "Be humble." And the Virgin Mary, because humility is the universal guardian—the Virgin Mary, as if she had forgotten all the other virtues of her soul and her body, admired only one thing in herself, and gave only one reason for the incarnation of the Son of God within her:

> "Because he has looked upon the humility of his servant." <note>original: "Quia respexit humilitatem ancillae suae"; a line from the *Magnificat* (Luke 1:48). While modern bibles often translate this as "lowly estate," Angela and her contemporaries interpreted it specifically as the virtue of humility.</note>

[The soul], having been transformed, loves every creature just as God loves every creature; for in every creature, it is God whom she sees, it is the name of God that she reads. If she is innocent, she knows that she is not so by her own power, but that she has been held by the hand, strengthened, and that temptation has been diminished; and, instead of puffing her up, the faults of other men help her to return to her own <term>abyss</term>, <note>original: "abîme." Calumny finds them ready to confess their ignorance and to withdraw rather than enter into discussion: they have no such indulgence for it.

The <term>spirit of childhood</term> knows nothing of questions of precedence; it knows nothing of the heaviness, the weight of the man who argues.

# SIXTY-FOURTH CHAPTER

## CHARITY

---

Love is the first of the virtues. Without it, prayer is worth nothing; without it, it is a pure vanity that God rejects, and every virtue is without fruit. Regarding the uselessness of prayer destitute of love, read the book of life <note>original: "livre de vie." While this term often refers to the names of the saved in Heaven, here Angela uses it to refer to the Gospels or the living example of Christ’s life.</note> and listen to Jesus Christ: "If, at the moment of laying your gift upon the altar, and there you remember that your brother has something against you, etc." <note>A reference to Matthew 5:23-24. Angela frequently uses "etc." to indicate that her readers—mostly monks or devout followers—would already know the rest of the scripture by heart.</note> The gift of prayer is worth nothing if it is not offered in the bond of charity. And in the Lord’s Prayer <note>original: "Oraison dominicale." From the Latin *Dominicus*, meaning "of the Lord."</note>: "Forgive us our trespasses, as we forgive, etc."

You will be forgiven just as you have forgiven. Place yourselves, therefore, in the state of the most intimate, the most <term>unitive</term> charity <note>Unitive charity refers to the highest stage of spiritual love which seeks total union with God and others, leaving no room for selfish division.</note>.

...power <note>The French text begins with the suffix "-sance," likely completing the word *puissance* (power) from the previous page.</note> penetrates the soul, the thought, and the heart like that one does; and if this force is not regulated, the soul rushes, like something weightless, into every trap, and its love becomes its ruin. I am not speaking only of the love that is absolutely evil, whose hellish danger is obvious to everyone, and which common sense itself tells us to avoid. To me, the love of God is above all suspect. If it is not armed with <term>discernment</term> <note>original: "discernement." In spiritual literature, this refers to the "discretion of spirits," the ability to distinguish between a true move of God and a mere emotional or deceptive impulse.</note>, it leads to death or to illusion; if it is not discrete, it rushes toward a catastrophe: what begins without order can lead to nothing.

Many believe they are in love, but are actually in hatred of God and in friendship with his enemies. He who loves God solely to be preserved from this or that incidental pain is not in a perfect order; for he loves himself first, and God second, though God must be loved above all else and for his own sake. He has made a god of himself, and loves God only for his own benefit. He who loves in this way loves things because of himself, seeking in them only the pleasure of his body, of which he has made a god. He loves his parents only if they bring him honor and profit; he loves in the saints not their holiness, but the help he hopes to gain from them for himself; he loves...

In Angela’s mystical theology, reason (or the higher intellect) is the faculty that should govern the emotions and keep the soul directed toward God.</note> intervenes and pushes back; for it is not yet stifled by love.

Now, one of the two victims of this ever-growing love absolutely wants to know if the other is wounded to the same degree as themselves and by the same arrow <note>A classical reference to Cupid's arrow, used here to describe the onset of obsessive, human desire in place of spiritual love.</note>. This is why love is suspicious to me above all else. It contains every evil. Therefore, beware of the <term>serpent</term> <note>In mystical literature, the serpent symbolizes the subtle, creeping nature of temptation that enters under the guise of something good or spiritual.</note>.

I suspect the love of God, and I suspect the love of neighbor, for that which was good can become evil. Even the love of God becomes evil without the armor of <term>discernment</term> <note>original: "discernement." In the spiritual life, this is the ability to distinguish between movements of the soul that come from God and those that come from the ego or from temptation.</note>. This armor is given to man in the sublime act of transformation. Now, the transformation of the soul into God has three modes of fulfillment.

->### ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-
<page-num>290</page-num>

The first <term>transformation</term> <note>In mystical theology, "transformation" refers to the process by which the soul is changed to become more like God, eventually reaching a state of union.</note> unites the soul to the will of God, the second unites it *with* God, and the third unites it *in* God and God in it.

The first transformation is an imitation of **Jesus crucified**, for the cross is a manifestation of the divine will <note>For Franciscan mystics like Angela, meditating on the suffering of Jesus on the cross was the essential starting point for aligning one's own human will with God's.</note>.

The second transformation unites the soul *with* God. Its love is then no longer merely an act of its will; for the source is opened—the source of immense feelings, the source of immense delights. However, there is still room here for speech and thought.

The third transformation so completely melts the soul into God and God into the soul that, at the immense height where the mystery is fulfilled, words die along with thoughts: he alone knows these things who feels them <note>original: "celui-là sait ces choses qui les sent." This is a common mystical trope: that true spiritual knowledge is experiential and cannot be fully described through logic or language.</note>.

The first transformation, although it contains the law of love, is insufficient and leaves room for illusion.

The second transformation, if it is properly accomplished, ensures that love takes its true direction.

The third transformation inhabits the summits where the government of love resides.

The second and the third are the gifts of **grace**. The second, in the realm of imperfection, and the third, in the realm of perfection, may both be called **wisdom**. It is...

When the soul's love is a creation of the Sovereign Essence, when it is born of this contemplation, it then knows how to ascend toward the Essence from which it draws its origin. When the soul that has seen the being of God possesses the love of correspondence <note>A "love of correspondence" is a reciprocal love where the soul's affection begins to mirror or harmonize with God's own love.</note> to a sufficient degree, it becomes strong to the point of immutability <note>original: "immutabilité" — in this context, a state of spiritual steadfastness where the soul is no longer easily shaken by worldly emotions or changing circumstances.</note>. Nothing—not even visions of another kind nor raptures—nothing shakes it. In the absence of the ineffable vision <note>The "ineffable vision" refers to an experience of God that is beyond words, description, or human understanding.</note>, a deep reflection that weighs the being of God can be enough, and is enough, to purify all love and to dull every evil edge.

As for the ineffable vision, beyond the created love it produces in the soul, because it focuses on <term>the Uncreated</term> <note>The "Uncreated" refers to God, the only being who exists without having been made; all other things are "created."</note>, it allows a love of the same nature to flow into the person. Totally absorbed by the vision, the soul does not know how to respond to the One who comes within it. But this illustrious love performs its own operations <note>In mystical theology, "operations" are the actions or movements produced by divine grace within the soul.</note>.

Notice this: At the moment the vision was given to the soul, the soul was active and gathered itself in an immense desire to deepen its union. But afterward, it is <term>uncreated love</term> that acts within the soul; it is this love that pushes the soul to withdraw from every creature <note>To "withdraw from every creature" means to detach one's primary affection from worldly things and people to focus entirely on God.</note> in order to increase the intimate union. It is uncreated love itself that performs the operations of love. Now, the principle behind the operations of this love is <term>illumination</term> <note>Illumination is the spiritual stage where the soul's intellect is cleared of darkness and filled with divine light and understanding.</note> and the gift of a new desire.

It is a certain strong and new love, which the soul would be incapable of giving to itself. Now, <term>uncreated love</term> <note>original: "amour incréé" — In mystical theology, this refers to the love that is God’s own essence, as opposed to "created love," which is the human capacity to feel affection.</note> performs all the good that is done by our hands. Without it, we are capable of every evil. All good comes from it. True humility consists in seeing in truth who is the worker of good; whoever possesses this sight possesses the <term>Spirit of truth</term> <note>The "Spirit of truth" is a biblical name for the Holy Spirit, believed here to provide the clarity needed to recognize God as the source of all virtue.</note>. The love of God is never idle. It pushes one to truly follow the <term>way of the cross</term> <note>The "way of the cross" refers to the spiritual practice of imitating the suffering, humility, and sacrifice of Jesus.</note>. This love offers the cross to the soul; it is a <term>penance</term> <note>Voluntary acts of self-discipline or sacrifice intended to show devotion and regret for past failings.</note>, long, grave, and austere, but its measure and form must always depend on the <term>universal harmony</term> <note>The idea that spiritual practices should not be chaotic or destructive, but should fit into the balanced order of God's creation.</note>. Order has its own fitness, which must be followed in all things. This true love stops every kind of disorder in one’s attitude, in drinking, and in eating. It excludes empty vanity; instead of resisting order, it creates order where it finds none.

And when love, throughout a person’s entire life and in the necessary measure, has borne the fruits of the tree of the cross—the fruits of penance in <term>austerity</term> <note>A life of strict self-denial and simplicity.</note>—it is then that the person will begin to understand that they are a useless servant, an evil servant <note>A reference to Luke 17:10, expressing the idea that no matter how much good a person does, it is nothing compared to God’s grace.</note>. They will see two parts: in God, all love; in themselves, all hatred; and this sight will introduce them into a penance in which they will not want the body <note>The text ends mid-sentence here; the author is likely leading into the idea that the soul desires the spirit to take precedence over the physical body's comforts.</note>

It emphasizes that true spiritual growth results in a profound sense of one's own insignificance compared to the overwhelming love of God.</summary>

...remains a stranger. Whether the <term>penance</term> <note>Voluntary acts of self-discipline or sacrifice intended to show devotion and regret for past failings.</note> is light or not, it is <term>uncreated love</term> <note>original: "amour incréé" — A central concept in Angela’s mysticism referring to the love that is God’s very nature, acting through the human person rather than human effort alone.</note> that performs it, and it diversifies it immensely according to the needs of each soul. May penance and the thought of penance never be a burden for you; for it is God who is at work. To stir your will and obtain your consent, Jesus Christ gave the example.

Those who are raised to the vision of the <term>Uncreated Essence</term> <note>original: "Essence incréée" — The most profound mystical state where the soul encounters God's actual being beyond all images, names, or concepts.</note> are lost in this immense rest, and, having drawn fire from the source, are pushed by it toward greater undertakings; for their flame is renewed.

Those who do not have the <term>spirit of truth</term> <note>The internal guidance of the Holy Spirit that allows a person to see things as they truly are, particularly their own nothingness compared to God’s greatness.</note>, attributing glory to themselves, become <term>idolaters</term> <note>In this context, an "idolater" is someone who worships their own spiritual achievements or character rather than God.</note> who worship their own <term>good works</term> <note>Virtuous actions or religious deeds.</note>.

They turn the <term>gifts of God</term> into idols; their light becomes their idol, their knowledge becomes their idol; they turn even their <term>prudence</term> <note>The virtue of making wise and careful decisions; here, Angela warns that even being "wise" can become a source of pride if one forgets it is a gift.</note>—which was given to them for discernment—into an idol. For all good comes from love, from uncreated love, which burns eternally and is never extinguished in its own depths.

# SIXTY-FIFTH CHAPTER

### THE WAYS OF LOVE

---

The path that leads to this love is the reading of the <term>book of life</term> <note>original: "livre de vie" — In Angela’s writing, the "book of life" usually refers to the life and suffering of Jesus Christ, which serves as a manual for the soul to study and imitate.</note>, and there is no other. O my cherished children, may our love be perfect! May our transformation be complete! For he is all love, this <term>God-Man</term> <note>original: "Homme-Dieu" — A central term in Angela's Christology, emphasizing that Jesus is both fully human and fully divine.</note>, this uncreated God, this incarnate God; he loves us entirely, and he wants us to love him entirely. He wants Him, and us through love, to become *one*.

I call "children of the Spirit" those who, through the grace of <term>charity</term> <note>The supernatural love of God that allows a person to love as God loves.</note>, live in God, in the perfection of <term>transformed love</term> <note>original: "amour transformé" — The state where a person's human will and affection are so united with God that they are "transformed" into a divine likeness.</note>. We are all sons of God by creation, but these are the <term>vessels of election</term> <note>original: "vases de l'élection" — A term derived from the Bible (Acts 9:15) referring to those chosen by God to receive and carry His grace specially.</note> and the sons of the Spirit, in whom God has placed his love, and in whom he rests, drawn by his own resemblance. It is his grace and his love that have formed his image in the soul. I call "perfect" the one who has transformed their life into the likeness of the God-Man.

<summary>Angela describes the path to divine union through the "book of life," explaining that true perfection involves a total transformation of the soul into the likeness of the God-Man, Jesus Christ.</summary>
<keywords>transformation, God-Man, children of the Spirit, book of life, vessels of election, divine love, union with God</keywords>

Now, know that God, noble by nature, asks for our whole heart and not half of our heart; he wants it without intermediary, without sharing, without contestation. It is as if God is courting the human soul. If she gives herself entirely, he takes all; if she gives herself halfway, he receives her halfway; but it is the first of these two things that makes his joy; for perfect love is a <term>jealous love</term> <note>original: "amour jaloux" — In mystical theology, this refers to God’s desire for the soul’s exclusive devotion, mirroring the "jealousy" described in the Bible (e.g., Exodus 20:5).</note>. The <term>Bridegroom</term> <note>A traditional mystical title for Christ, representing his intimate relationship with the human soul, which is referred to as the "Bride."</note>, in his love, cannot suffer in the <term>Bride</term> even the shadow of a division, neither in public nor in secret. Now, our God is a jealous God. I know, moreover, I know perfectly well that if there existed a person who had tasted the love of Jesus crucified, of Jesus the sovereign good, that person would not only tear themselves away from <term>creatures</term> <note>original: "créatures" — Referring to all created things, including other people and worldly possessions, as distinct from the Creator.</note>, they would tear themselves away from themselves to give themselves more absolutely, and that all their powers would become but one to transform them entirely into Him who is our Savior and our love, Jesus Christ, Jesus Christ!

If the soul wishes to free itself and rise toward the perfection of love which gives itself entirely, which consecrates itself not only in view of temporal or eternal reward, but also in view of the being of God, who is <term>Goodness by essence</term> <note>original: "Bonté par essence" — A theological concept stating that God does not merely possess goodness, but is the very source and definition of goodness itself.</note>, Goodness worthy of love; the soul, I say, must walk in the straight path, wal- <note>The text cuts off here at the start of the word "walking" (marcher).</note>

The first step she must take in this way is to know God in truth—not superficially, not from the outside, nor through the knowledge found in books. One must know Him deeply. For a person loves only as much as they know. If our knowledge is limited, vague, or superficial—if we think of God like someone merely performing a duty—our love will be wretched. Re-read what I have already said on this subject.

But love has properties and signs that allow it to be recognized.

**First property.** Love transforms one into the other, regarding the will.

Now, the will of Christ is, it seems to me, the life of which he gave the example: a life full of poverty, <term>contempt</term> <note>original: "mépris" — This refers to the worldly rejection or lack of status that Christ embraced, which the mystic seeks to imitate through "holy indifference."</note>, obedience, and suffering; the practice of these things is a bulwark against evil and against temptation.

**Second property.** Love transforms one into the other, regarding the constitutive qualities of Being. I will mention only three: Love inclines toward <term>creatures</term> <note>original: "créatures" — In mystical theology, this refers to all created things (people, animals, nature). The soul, transformed by God's love, begins to love the world with the same harmony God does.</note>, according to the laws of universal harmony. Love is humble and gentle. Love is <term>immutable</term> <note>original: "immuable" — Unchanging. As the soul nears the "Unchanging God," it becomes less affected by the shifting emotions or circumstances of the world.</note>. The closer the soul is to God, the more it is inaccessible to change. Shame consists in being shaken—

Sometimes <term>tribulation</term> <note>In this context, a trial or period of intense suffering intended to test or purify the soul.</note> turns a person toward God and makes them cling to Him. Knowledge of God and knowledge of oneself: this is the perfection of man. This double vision produces grace upon grace, light upon light, vision upon vision. The more your knowledge of God grows, the more your love will grow, and with it your power to act. Your actions will be the proof and the measure of your love; usually, love seeks to resemble the **Beloved** in both action and <term>passion</term> <note>In mystical theology, "passion" refers to the act of suffering or enduring, modeled on the "Passion of Christ."</note>. Christ endured poverty, contempt, and pain. The choice of wisdom reveals the true value of things.

# CHAPTER SIXTY-SIX

## THE GIFTS OF GOD

---

Here are several very sweet gifts that indicate, in the one who possesses them, the fullness and perfection of <term>consuming love</term> <note>In mystical theology, "consuming love" refers to a state where the soul's own desires and ego are "burned away" or consumed by the fire of divine love, leaving only God's will.</note>. They can serve as a measure for the soul to know the point it has reached on the path of <term>transformation</term> <note>The spiritual process of being "re-formed" or changed into the likeness of Christ.</note>.

First, the love of poverty, which delivers the soul from attachments to created things, from any possession that would not be that of Jesus Christ, and from any hope founded on another. This love must not only live in the heart; it must prove itself through actions.

Another gift is the desire to be despised by every creature, to find compassion nowhere, to live in the heart of God alone, and to count for nothing everywhere else.

I could also cite the desire to be overwhelmed and flooded in one's heart and in one's body

<summary>Angela describes specific spiritual "gifts"—such as the love of poverty and the desire for worldly contempt—that serve as indicators of a soul's transformation and progress toward perfect divine love.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, consuming love, spiritual transformation, poverty, humility, divine gifts, detachment</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the list of spiritual gifts from page 307. It concludes the description of the three primary desires—poverty, contempt, and suffering—and introduces the fourth gift: the profound sense of one's own unworthiness.</meta>

->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-
<page-num>308</page-num>

by all the sorrows of Jesus and Mary, and that every creature makes you undergo them without ceasing.

He who does not have these three desires does not possess the blessed likeness of Christ, for they accompanied Him and His mother at all times and in every act.

If you possess these three gifts, the fourth will be to feel yourself unworthy of them, to be persuaded that you do not have them by your own virtue; and the more you have them, the more you will believe they are lacking; for he loses love who declares himself satisfied with his gifts.

Know, then, that you have never "arrived"; regard yourself as one who is about to begin, who has until now done nothing and received nothing.

Then, through unceasing meditation and <term>savory prayer</term> <note>The original French "oraison savoureuse" refers to a type of contemplative prayer where the soul "tastes" or experiences God's presence with deep internal delight, rather than just reciting words.</note>, you will seek these things within the interior of Jesus Christ. You will cry out to God, asking Him for the mantle of the <term>new Elijah</term> <note>A biblical reference to the prophet Elijah; his mantle or cloak symbolized his spiritual authority and the power of the Holy Spirit. Angela uses it here to represent the soul being completely "clothed" in Christ's spirit.</note>, and you will claim nothing but the perfect transformation of yourself into Him. You will plunge yourself into this joy of joys, into the joy of your earthly life, and you will climb the ladder of contemplation to seek the fullness of Jesus; there, you will draw from the infinite abundances that His outward life did not manifest. Then you will flee like the plague everything that—

Those who possess these things in the present life, in today's struggle, shall possess God in the <term>homeland</term> <note>The "heavenly homeland" (original French: "patrie"), a common mystical term for Heaven, viewed as the true home of the soul compared to the "exile" of life on earth.</note>. # SIXTY-SEVENTH CHAPTER

## THE MOST HOLY SACRAMENT OF THE ALTAR

---

Let us speak for a moment of the sacrament of love; let us speak of the <term>Eucharist</term> <note>From the Greek word for "thanksgiving," this refers to the Christian ceremony commemorating the Last Supper, in which bread and wine are consecrated and consumed as the body and blood of Christ.</note>.

It is this which provokes ardent prayer within the soul; it is this which awakens the virtue of <term>impetration</term> <note>The act of obtaining a favor or grace from God through petitionary prayer.</note>, and the power to "wrest" things from God. It is this which digs out the abyss of humility; it is this which kindles the flames of love. I have not merely a vague thought, but the absolute certainty, that if a soul were to see and contemplate any of the inner splendors of the <term>sacrament of the altar</term> <note>Another name for the Eucharist, emphasizing its presence upon the church altar during the Mass.</note>, it would catch fire, for it would see divine love itself. It seems to me that those who offer the sacrifice <note>A reference to the priests celebrating the Mass.</note>, or those who take part in it, ought to meditate deeply on the profound truth of this thrice-holy mystery; that one must not move at a running pace through this contemplation, but remain motionless, fixed, sunken, absorbed, and <term>engulfed</term> <note>The original French "abîmé" suggests being lost in the depths or overwhelmed by the magnitude of the mystery.</note>. Although the mysteries of the sacrament are absolutely <term>ineffable</term> <note>Too great or extreme to be expressed or described in words.</note>, I shall try to present...

This mystery is absolutely new, absolutely admirable, absolutely superior to reason. It was announced in advance, as we see in <term>Scripture</term> <note>The Bible, specifically the Old Testament prophecies that the author views as foreshadowing the New Testament.</note>; but if it is ancient in terms of its <term>figure</term> <note>A symbolic representation or "type" that points toward a future reality.</note>, it is new in terms of its fulfillment, in terms of its reality. It is certain that by the virtue of the <term>consecrating words</term> <note>The specific phrases—"This is my body" and "This is my blood"—spoken by a priest during the Mass.</note>, the <term>God-Man</term> <note>A traditional title for Jesus Christ, emphasizing his dual nature as both fully divine and fully human.</note> changed the bread and wine into his body and his blood; it is certain that the priest, his minister, accomplishes at the altar, by virtue of the power he has received, the same act of power.

When he pronounces the words of consecration over the bread and wine, these materials are <term>transubstantiated</term> <note>The Catholic doctrine that the underlying reality (substance) of the bread and wine changes into Christ, even though the physical appearance remains the same.</note> into the true body and the true blood of the God-Man. There remains the color of the bread and wine, their flavor, their appearance, their <term>accidents</term> <note>A term from Scholastic philosophy referring to the physical properties of an object (like color, smell, or texture) that can be perceived by the senses, as opposed to its essential "substance."</note>; but these accidents do not belong to the body of Jesus Christ; they exist in themselves, divine power having given them orders superior to their nature. The color is therefore here in itself, the flavor in itself, the whiteness in itself: each quality detached from any substance exists in itself. This is truly the great innovation made by the arm of wisdom, armed with power and goodness: the body and the blood of the

> « I am incomprehensible to you; I am God, I act without you, and the word impossible has no meaning for me. I could have made you capable of understanding; I preferred to leave you the merit of faith: believe and do not doubt. »

Jesus Christ instituted it because his love surpasses words. As his innermost being <note>The original French "entrailles" (entrails/bowels) follows a biblical tradition where the internal organs are seen as the seat of deep, visceral compassion.</note> cried out toward us, he threw himself there entirely, entirely and forever, until the end of the ages. Sixthly, this sacrament is of supreme value: it is the gift of gifts and the grace of graces. When the all-powerful and eternal God comes to us with all the perfection of the thrice-holy humanity of the divinity, He does not come empty-handed. Provided that you have undergone the trial required by <term>the Apostle</term> <note>A reference to Saint Paul, specifically 1 Corinthians 11:28: "But let a man examine himself, and so let him eat of that bread, and drink of that cup."</note>, and that you do not have the intention to sin, He grants you the remission of <term>temporal punishments</term> <note>In Catholic doctrine, these are the "debts" or consequences of sin that remain even after the guilt of the sin has been forgiven; they are typically purified through penance or in Purgatory.</note>, strengthens you against temptations, restrains the power of your enemies, and increases your merits. This is why I recommend to you, in the reception of the <term>sacrament of the altar</term> <note>A traditional name for the Eucharist, emphasizing its nature as a sacrifice offered upon an altar.</note>, both frequency and respect. Saint Augustine says somewhere, it is true:

> “As for <term>daily communion</term> <note>original: "quotidie eucharistiae communionem accipere..." – The practice of receiving the Eucharist every day was a subject of much debate in the Middle Ages; many laypeople received only a few times a year.</note>, I neither blame nor praise it.”

> “Live in such a way as to receive communion every day.” <note>original: "Sic vive, ut quotidie possis accipere."</note>

What, then, was his thought when he spoke the first word? Seeing that in the Church the good are mixed with the bad, he did not blame daily communion for fear of turning away the good, and if he said he did not praise it, it was solely for fear of authorizing the bad <note>That is, Augustine feared that "praising" it unconditionally might encourage those in a state of sin to receive the host sacrilegiously.</note>.

# CHAPTER SIXTY-EIGHT

## THE INCARNATION

---

This is the last letter that our mother, Angela of Foligno, wrote to us before her fatal illness; these are the final lines her hand traced. She had warned us herself: "My children," our mother had said, "this is my last letter." For she knew long in advance the blessed moment when she would pass from time into eternity.

At the terrible news that Angela was speaking for the last time, the one holding the pen <note>Reference to the scribe who recorded her visions and letters, typically identified as her confessor, Brother Arnaldo.</note> required her encouragement to find the courage to write.

Before dictating, she uttered a great cry:

> "O my God! make me worthy to know something of the mystery of the <term>heights</term> <note>In mystical theology, the 'heights' refers to the supreme, transcendent nature of the Divine.</note>, something of this <term>incarnation</term> <note>The Christian doctrine of God becoming human in the person of Jesus Christ.</note> that you have performed, of this incarnation, the principle and source of <term>salvation</term> <note>Spiritual rescue from sin and death.</note>. O ineffable <note>Too great or extreme to be expressed or described in words.</note> incarnation! it is this which brings to man, along with the satisfy—

---

—action of love, the certainty of salvation. This charity is beyond words; but above it, there is nothing: the Word was made flesh, in order to make me God! <note>This refers to the concept of "theosis" or "divinization"—the mystical belief that through Christ, humans are invited to participate in the divine nature.</note> O secret of the depths of God! You emptied and stripped yourself to make something of me; you took the garment of the lowliest slave to give me the cloak of a king and a God! And, taking the form of a slave, you diminished nothing of your substance; you did no wrong to your divinity. But the abyss of your humility opens my heart and wrests these cries from me:

> "O incomprehensible one, made comprehensible for my sake! O uncreated one, here you are created! O you who are inaccessible to spirits and bodies, here you are, through a marvel of power, here you are palpable to thoughts and fingers! O Lord, touch my eyes, so that I may see the depth and the height of the charity that you have communicated to us in this <term>incarnation</term> <note>The act of God becoming human in Jesus.</note>! O happy fault! <note>original: "Heureuse faute." This is a reference to the Latin phrase *Felix Culpa* from the Easter Vigil liturgy, suggesting that the fall of Adam was "happy" because it brought about so great a Redeemer.</note> Not happy in itself, but by the virtue of divine mercy. Happy fault which has revealed the sacred and hidden depths of the abysses of love! Truly, a higher charity cannot be conceived. O Most High, make my intellect capable of your most high and ineffable charity!"

<margin>Vocabulary: Angela of Foligno, mystic, the Word, Incarnation, charity, Happy Fault, Felix Culpa, divinity, humility, ineffable</margin>

<summary>In this concluding portion of her final letter, Angela of Foligno contemplates the paradox of the Incarnation, where the infinite God becomes a finite, palpable human. She celebrates the "happy fault" of humanity that allowed such a profound expression of divine love and humility.</summary>

<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Incarnation, Felix Culpa, divine love, mysticism, humility, theosis, salvation</keywords>

"Lord, I perceive five mysteries. Enlarge my intellect, for the capacity is lacking. Blessed be you, Lord, who makes me understand that you were born for me. Oh! what glory, what glory to see and to feel—as I believe it, as I feel it—that you were born for me! To feel this in truth, that is the delight, that is the joy of joys! The same certainty that we draw from the Incarnation, we also draw from the <term>Nativity</term> <note>The birth of Jesus.</note>, for he was born to perform the work that determined his incarnation. This refers to the shame, insults, and social rejection Christ endured.</note> are documents, lessons, and books. Your birth, your life, and your death speak the same language.

# SIXTY-NINTH CHAPTER

## PRAYER

---

Then she spoke of seven gifts, of seven benefits in particular, and in these terms:
> “O most sweet Lord, among the innumerable multitude of your gifts, make me capable of understanding seven. First, the mysterious creation. Then the admirable <term>election</term> <note>The theological concept of being chosen by God for salvation or a specific divine purpose.</note> which grants us a meeting place in glory. Then the gift of Jesus Christ, who was born and died to give us life. Then the most high gift of reason. For, instead of creating a woman, you could have created a beast. Admirable <term>oraison</term> <note>Contemplative or mental prayer. Angela suggests that the gift of reason is what allows for this deep communication with the Divine.</note>! It is through it that I know you, through it that I know my sins; through it that, with your grace helping, I resist temptation. O Incomprehensible One! Your hands have made a masterpiece. You created us in your image and likeness; then you clothed us in your light, like a mantle. Then you gave us intelligence. Make me capable...

<summary>Angela of Foligno enumerates seven specific divine gifts, reflecting on the mysteries of creation, the soul's election to glory, and the sacrifice of Christ. She emphasizes the gift of human reason and intelligence as the means by which a person can know God, recognize sin, and resist temptation.</summary>

<keywords>seven gifts, creation, divine election, Jesus Christ, reason, image of God, masterpiece, intelligence, Angela of Foligno</keywords>

Make me understand love, since the angels have no other happiness than to see the One they love and to love the One they contemplate! O gift which is above every gift, since you yourself are love!

->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-

# SEVENTIETH CHAPTER
# AND LAST

## THE TESTAMENT AND DEATH

---

When our mother Angela felt she was near death—Angela who, while on earth, lived far from the earth <note>This describes her state of mystical detachment, suggesting that although she was physically present, her spirit was already focused on the divine realm.</note>—she made her testament and instructed her sons for one last time, saying to them:

"My dear children, I speak to you for the love of God, according to the promise I made: I do not want to take anything with me, nor hide anything from you that might be useful to you. For God said to the soul: 'All that is mine is yours.' By what virtue can it be that all that is His is ours? I tell you, in truth, it is <term>charity</term> <note>In this context, "charity" (from the Latin *caritas*) refers to the highest form of love—the selfless, divine love that unites the soul with God.</note> that does this. The words I am about to speak are not mine; they are God’s.

"For it has pleased the Lord to give me the love and the concern for all His sons and all His daughters, for all who breathe on this globe, on this side and beyond the sea. I have guarded them as best I could, and I have suffered for them—"

<summary>As Angela of Foligno approaches the end of her life, she begins her final testament to her spiritual followers, explaining that through divine charity, the soul comes to possess all that belongs to God.</summary>

<keywords>Angela of Foligno, testament, death, charity, divine love, spiritual motherhood, mystical union</keywords>

the sorrows that no one knows. O my God, I place them today into your hands, beseeching you by your ineffable <term>charity</term> <note>In the Franciscan tradition, "charity" is the divine love that binds the soul to God and neighbor.</note> to preserve them from all evil, and to strengthen them in all good, in the love of <term>poverty</term> <note>For Angela, poverty was not merely a lack of possessions, but a radical stripping away of the self to make room for God.</note>, of contempt, and of sorrow; to transform their life into your life, and to introduce them into the perfection of which your words and actions gave us the model when you lived the human life.

> "O my beloved sons, listen to the final word, the word and the prayer of farewell. Here is this word: 'My children, be humble! My children, be gentle!' I am not speaking of the external act; I am speaking of the depths of the heart; my children, be gentle in your core. Be in truth the disciples of Him who said: 'Learn from me that I am gentle and humble of heart.' <note>This is a direct reference to the Gospel of Matthew 11:29.</note> Do not worry about honors or dignities. The dignities that swell the soul are vanities that must be cursed. Flee them! for they are dangerous; but listen! listen! they are less dangerous than spiritual vanities. Showing that one knows how to speak of God, understanding...
