The world would laugh if I said that I wept for the sins of others more than for my own, for that is not natural. But charity is not born of the world. O my children, love and do not judge; and if you see a man sinning mortally, have horror for the sin, but do not judge the man, and despise no one; for you do not know the judgments of God. > "I leave you no other testament: Love one another, and let your humility be profound. She fell silent, then laid her hand upon each head, and said:

> "Be blessed, my children, by the Lord and by me. Be blessed, you who are present; be blessed, you who are absent. According to the Lord's command, I give to those present and those absent my blessing for eternity, and may Jesus Christ give it to you at the same time; be blessed by the hand that was raised upon the cross."

Angela, broken by the approaching death, and more deeply absorbed than usual in the bottomless abyss of the Divinity, spoke only a few rare and interrupted words. We who were there tried to gather these words. Here they are, more or less.

A moment before, she had just said: "Do you know that during the storm Jesus Christ was in the ship? <note>A reference to the biblical episode (Matthew 8:23–27) where Jesus sleeps during a storm while his disciples are afraid; Angela uses it as a metaphor for God’s apparent absence during spiritual trials.</note> In truth, He is thus in the soul when He allows temptations, when He seems to sleep. She was thinking of the current certainty of her death, and feared seeing it delayed once more. Angela desired [death].

--- Page 341 ---
<meta>This page concludes the account of Angela of Foligno's life and death, following her final visions on the eve of her passing in January 1309.</meta>

<page-num>337</page-num>
# ANGELA OF FOLIGNO

"Yes," she replied.

In this peace of body and this joy of spirit, Angela remained on Saturday evening, surrounded by the brothers, who were showing her the office of the day. <note>The "office" refers to the Liturgy of the Hours, the official set of daily prayers and psalms recited by clergy and religious orders.</note>

On that very day, the octave of the feast of the Holy Innocents, at the final hour of the evening, like someone falling into a light sleep, Angela, our mother, fell asleep in peace. <note>The "octave" is the eighth day following a major church feast; for the Holy Innocents, this marks January 4th.</note>

Released from the bonds of the flesh, her most pure soul, absorbed into the abyss of the unfathomable Divinity, received from the hands of her Spouse the robe of innocence and immortality, to reign eternally with him. <note>In mystical tradition, the "Spouse" refers to Jesus Christ, representing the soul's union with God.</note>

Through the power of the cross, through the merits of the Virgin, and through the intercession of our mother Angela, may the Lord Jesus Christ lead us where she is. Amen.

The servant of Jesus Christ, Angela of Foligno, saved from the shipwreck of this world, flew toward the heavenly joys so long promised to her desires, in the year 1309 of the Christian era, in the first days of January, under the pontificate of Pope Clement V.

---

Her body was honorably buried in Foligno, in the Church of Saint Francis of the Friars Minor <note>original: "Ejus corpus Fulginei in Ecclesiâ sancti Francisci Patrum Minorum honorifice tumulatum"</note>.

# ANGELA OF FOLIGNO

And there, resplendent with miracles, she is venerated with the deepest devotion by the faithful. <note>original: "ibique miraculis coruscans, summâ fidelium religione colitur"</note>

### PRAYER

> O God, the sweetness of hearts and the light of the Blessed, who refreshed your servant, Blessed Angela, through the wondrous contemplation of heavenly things; grant that, by her merits and intercession, we may so come to know you on earth that we may deserve to rejoice in the revelation of your eternal glory in heaven. <note>original: "Deus, dulcedo cordium et lumen Beatorum..." This is the official "Collect" or opening prayer for the feast day of Saint Angela.</note>

->*(Extract from the Roman Breviary for the use*<-
->*of the Friars Minor).*<- <note>The "Breviary" is the book containing the daily office of hymns, prayers, and lessons. The "Friars Minor" is the formal name for the Franciscan Order, to which Angela belonged as a Tertiary.</note>

### END

->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-

# Meeting of Saint Angela of Foligno and Ubertin of Casale

Ubertin of Casale, in an autobiographical essay—a kind of preface to his work *The Tree of the Crucified Life of Jesus* <gloss>1</gloss>—noted the significant role that Saint Angela of Foligno played in his life. He met her during the twenty-fifth year of his religious life, which is to say in 1298, as shown by the discussion of dates provided by Father Frédégand Callaey <gloss>2</gloss>, whose research we reproduce or summarize here.

Ubertin entered the Order of <term>Friars Minor</term> <note>The formal name for the Franciscan Order, founded by St. Francis of Assisi, emphasizing humility (the "lesser" brothers).</note> in 1273, and likely donned the Franciscan habit in a convent of the <term>custody</term> <note>An administrative district within the Franciscan Order.</note> of Montferrat, or at least in the province of Genoa, to which Casale belonged. His contemporaries frequently referred to him as Ubertin of Genoa. For fourteen years, he tells us, he devoted himself fervently to the spiritual life and strived for perfection, despite the temptations of the evil spirit and of "vain science" <note>In a religious context, "vain science" refers to intellectual study pursued for personal pride or academic prestige rather than for spiritual growth or the love of God.</note>.

---

1. *The Tree of the Crucified Life of Jesus*. <note>original: "Arbor vitæ crucifixæ Jesu"</note> Prologue. I. Venice 1485.
2. *Franciscan Spiritual Idealism in the 14th Century: A Study on Ubertin of Casale* by Frédégand Callaey, <term>O. M. Cap.</term> <note>Order of Friars Minor Capuchin, a reform branch of the Franciscans.</note> University of Louvain, 1911, Collection of works published by members of the history and philology conferences. 28th volume <gloss>XXVIII</gloss>. (280 pp. price 5 fr.).

<summary>This page introduces the meeting between the mystic Angela of Foligno and the Franciscan leader Ubertin of Casale in 1298, drawing on biographical details from Ubertin's own writings and modern scholarship.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Ubertin of Casale, Franciscan Order, Friars Minor, 13th century, hagiography, religious life, spiritual perfection</keywords>

--- Page 346 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Ubertin of Casale, focusing on his formative years and the influential figures he encountered in Tuscany and Umbria before his pivotal meeting with Angela of Foligno.</meta>

->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-

Sent by his superiors to Florence to continue his studies around 1285, he visited the sanctuaries of Rome as a pilgrim, then made his way toward Umbria. During his interactions with John of Parma at Greccio—the former Minister General of the Order—the elder warned him against spiritual laxity, initiated him into the prophecies then in circulation, and gave him a glimpse of the spiritual renovation of Christendom. At Cortona, he also saw Margaret, the holy penitent <note>Saint Margaret of Cortona (1247–1297), a prominent Franciscan penitent known for her dramatic conversion.</note>, whose son was then a member of the Franciscan convent there.

During four years in Florence, he devoted himself to his studies and his ministry. There, his spiritual directors completed "the work begun in the cloisters of Genoa—the hotbeds of mystical life—and continued at the hermitage of Greccio and at Cortona, at the feet of the austere <term>Joachimite</term> <note>A follower of the theories of Joachim of Fiore, who prophesied a new "Age of the Holy Spirit." This movement was very influential among the more radical Franciscans.</note> patriarch and the 'Magdalene of Tuscany'" <note>A reference to Margaret of Cortona, comparing her life of penance to that of Mary Magdalene.</note> (p. 11).

These souls, in whom the spirit of Christ seethed (as he tells us), were the Blessed Peter of Siena, a <term>tertiary</term> <note>A layperson who follows the rule of a religious order (like the Franciscans) while living in the secular world.</note> and a seller of combs—the *comb-seller* <note>original: "pettinagno"</note>—whose "holy prayers" <note>original: "sante orazioni"</note> Dante praised in the 13th Canto of the *Purgatory*; the pious virgin Cecilia; and even more so, Peter John Olivi, who, around 1287, arrived from Montpellier as a lecturer, but was also "venerated as a confessor of the faith by his followers. His holiness and theological knowledge made him the oracle of the <term>Spiritual Franciscans</term>." <note>A faction of the Franciscan Order that advocated for a strict, literal observance of St. Francis's rule of poverty.</note>

It does not seem unlikely to suggest that Dante, then aged 22 to 24, knew Ubertin: his preaching brought him into the public eye, and they shared a friend...

<summary>This page details Ubertin of Casale's spiritual education in Florence and Umbria, highlighting his connections to influential mystics and the "Spiritual" wing of the Franciscans, including a possible link to the poet Dante.</summary>

<keywords>Ubertin of Casale, Dante Alighieri, Peter John Olivi, Spiritual Franciscans, Joachimism, Greccio, Cortona, Margaret of Cortona, Peter of Siena, John of Parma, mysticism</keywords>

--- Page 347 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Ubertin of Casale, following his time in Florence and his transition to Paris. It details his spiritual decline during his academic pursuits and his eventual restoration through the influence of Angela of Foligno.</meta>

->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-

common, Pier *Pettinagno* <note>Peter the Comb-seller (d. 1289), a legendary holy merchant of Siena mentioned by Dante.</note>, and the arrival of a Master of Theology such as *Olivi* <note>Peter John Olivi (1248–1298), a leader of the "Spiritual" Franciscans known for his rigorous views on poverty.</note> made the convent of *Santa Croce* a highly esteemed intellectual center.

Ubertin left Florence in 1289 to go to Paris and prepare for a professorship there. It seems, if we are to believe the bitter self-reproaches he addressed to himself, his conduct was not always exemplary, and he took advantage of his privileged position to relax his fervor. But it is impossible to determine to what extent he allowed himself to be led into the abuses that *Jacopone da Todi* <note>A famous Franciscan poet known for his satirical and mystical verses.</note> pursued with his mocking wit. *Alvarez Pelayo* <note>Alvaro Pelayo (c. 1275–1349), a Franciscan writer and canon lawyer who lamented the corruption of the Church.</note> paints an even darker picture, going so far as to say that certain masters, through their neglect of the rules and of poverty, became the primary destroyers of the order: "For truly some masters and lecturers are the first and foremost transgressors of the rule and destroyers of the order." <note>original Latin: "Nam veraciter aliqui magistri et lectores primi et præcipui regulæ prævaricatores et ordinis destructores."</note> ¹

According to Ubertin, nothing less than a terrifying apparition of the wrathful Christ was required to make him come to his senses. But he also received the grace of meeting the Blessed *Angela*, who set him back on the right path; one feels, in reading his words, all the gratitude of a convert:

> "God made her known to me in a marvelous 
> way that I shall pass over in silence. He revealed to her the most 
> secret depths of my heart; there is no doubt it was 
> He who spoke to me through her mouth. She restored to me 
> a hundredfold the gifts of old, which my wickedness 

---

1. *Alvarus Pelagius*. *The Lament of the Church* <note>original title: "De Planctu Ecclesiae"</note>, book II, art. 66 (cited in *Callaey*, p. 18).

--- Page 348 ---
<meta>This page continues the testimony of Ubertin of Casale regarding his spiritual transformation. It concludes his first-hand account of how Angela of Foligno redirected his life and moves into historical commentary on the significance of his witness.</meta>

<page-num>344</page-num>

->## ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-

> ...had caused me to lose, to such an extent that from then on I was no longer the same man as before. My spirit was renewed through contact with the splendors of the truth she explained to me; the lukewarmness of my soul and my physical weakness vanished. Anyone of sound judgment who had known me before my meeting with the blessed woman could not doubt that the spirit of Christ had been born in me once again. Whether the detractors—who attack the irreproachable life of this most holy soul, Angela, and cast doubt upon the many conversions brought about by her words and example—wish it or not, God has made her a mother of fair love, of healthy fear, of greatness of soul, and of high hope for a multitude of spiritual sons. All good things have come to them with her; her hand has poured out the treasure of virtue abundantly upon them, even upon those many children of hers who at first led a disorderly life $^1$."

The Bollandists <note>A group of Jesuit scholars named after Jean Bolland, who applied historical criticism to the lives of the saints starting in the 17th century.</note> who, in their *Acts of the Saints* <note>original Latin: *Acta Sanctorum*</note>, cite Ubertin’s praise of Angela (January 4, p. 234, Antwerp 1643), highlight this great testimony given to the blessed woman by the first writer to celebrate her. "A great testimony from a holy and God-enlightened writer concerning Angela." <note>original Latin: *Magnum sancti et a Deo illuminati scriptoris de Angela testimonium.*</note>

After recalling these enthusiastic terms from the convert, who remembered a "good angel" that snatched him from the selfish pleasures of an unmortified <note>In a religious context, "unmortified" refers to a life governed by worldly desires rather than spiritual discipline or self-denial.</note> life, the recent historian of Ubertin of Casale notes the lasting effects...

***

1. *The Tree of Life* <note>original Latin: *Arbor Vitæ* (full title: *Arbor Vitae Crucifixae Jesu*), Ubertin's most famous work, written in 1305.</note>, 1, cited by Callaey, p. 20.

<summary>This page concludes Ubertin of Casale's moving account of his spiritual renewal through Angela of Foligno, which is historically significant as the earliest written tribute to her influence. It also notes how later hagiographers, the Bollandists, utilized this testimony to validate her sanctity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "admirable" results of Angela of Foligno's intervention in the life of Ubertin of Casale. The text explores Ubertin's shifting allegiances within the Franciscan Order and the recurring influence of holy women on his spiritual development.</meta>

<page-num>345</page-num>

->## ANGELA OF FOLIGNO 345<-

...mirable results of this intervention: "One must indeed believe," he writes, "that the widow of Foligno exercised a considerable influence over him: for the change of life wrought in him seems to have been serious. From then on, he oriented himself *definitively* toward the rigorism of the Spiritual Franciscans <note>A faction of the Franciscan Order that advocated for a literal and strict adherence to Saint Francis's rule of poverty, often in opposition to the more moderate "Community" of the order.</note>. No doubt his sights were set on this movement, and his preferences leaned toward it from his first years of religious profession. But he lost sight of it at times, carried away by the distractions in which he lived for quite a long time. During his years of study in Florence and his stay in Paris, the "Community" faction <note>The majority party within the Franciscan Order that accepted institutional ownership of property through various legal concessions.</note> had only reason to be pleased with him. At the Council of Vienne <note>An ecumenical council (1311–1312) where Ubertin of Casale defended the views of the Spiritual Franciscans.</note>, Ubertin testified that he was favored by them as long as he did not contradict them.

However, a voice, as persistent as remorse, came to tear him away several times from the comforts of a relaxed life <note>original: "vie mitigée," referring to a lifestyle where the original strictness of the religious rule has been softened by dispensations.</note>. At each fall, a helping hand reached out to him, raised him up, and showed him the ideal obscured by the dust of the road. In a touching detail, alongside the spiritual master who enriched his intellect with supernatural science, he always encountered a pious woman who scrutinized his heart and molded it with her fairy hands. Barely out of the hermitage of Greccio <note>The sanctuary where Saint Francis celebrated the first living Nativity scene in 1223.</note>, he made his way toward the cell of Margaret of Cortona <note>A famous Franciscan penitent (1247–1297) known for her mystical experiences and conversion.</note>. In Florence, the clear-sighted Cecilia completed the work of renewal accomplished in him by Peter Pettignano <note>A holy Franciscan tertiary and merchant from Siena mentioned by Dante in the *Divine Comedy*.</note> and Peter John Olivi <note>A brilliant and controversial theologian who was a primary intellectual leader of the Spiritual Franciscans.</note>. Upon his return from Paris, barely recovered from the fear caused by his terrible dream, the blessed Angela was there, waiting to...

<summary>This page discusses the spiritual trajectory of Ubertin of Casale, highlighting how his encounter with Angela of Foligno solidified his commitment to the Spiritual Franciscans. It also notes a recurring pattern in Ubertin's life where he was mentored by prominent holy women and theologians.</summary>

<keywords>Ubertin of Casale, Spiritual Franciscans, Angela of Foligno, Council of Vienne, Margaret of Cortona, Peter John Olivi, Peter Pettignano, Greccio, Franciscan Community</keywords>

--- Page 350 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion regarding the transformation of Ubertin of Casale under the spiritual direction of Angela of Foligno. It follows the previous page's description of Ubertin’s internal conflict between the academic life in Paris and the ascetic ideals of the Franciscan "Spirituals."</meta>

<page-num>346</page-num>

->ANGELA OF FOLIGNO<-

she comforts him with a motherly tenderness and strengthens him, for good this time, in the narrow path of Franciscan spirituality. For from now on his plan of life is definitively traced: Ubertin is entirely won over to the <term>rigorist</term> <note>A term for the "Spirituals," a faction of Franciscans who insisted on a literal, strict observance of Saint Francis’s rule of absolute poverty.</note> group which claims the heritage of the first companions of Saint Francis, and counts among its most illustrious members John of Parma, Olivi, and Conrad of Offida <note>Influential leaders and theologians of the Spiritual Franciscan movement.</note>. It is from them that he henceforth draws inspiration. But his fiery temperament, which accommodates only extremes, will often push him to exaggerate their tendencies." (p. 22).

In short, in 1298, upon his return from Paris, the career of Ubertin, then aged 39, is definitively traced. After much hesitation his mind is made up; "the deviations of which he was guilty during his long stay in the university city seem to have inspired in him a profound disgust for the <term>mitigated life</term> <note>original: "vie mitigée"; a lifestyle where the strictness of the original religious rule has been softened or relaxed by official dispensations.</note>. *At the voice of Angela of Foligno, he flings himself with all the impetuosity of his character into the footsteps of Saint Francis and his first companions.*" (p. 25).

The readers of Father Callaey <note>Frédégand Callaey (1885–1973), a Capuchin historian who wrote extensively on Ubertin of Casale.</note> will be able to follow him in his career. Let it suffice for us to have recalled, through these summaries or citations, these indications which complete the prologues of Brother Arnald <note>Angela's confessor and the primary scribe who recorded her visions and teachings.</note> and the personal revelations of the blessed woman.

Jules PACHEU.

<summary>This page describes how Angela of Foligno definitively steered Ubertin of Casale toward the "Spiritual" Franciscan movement, a strict faction advocating for radical poverty. The author, Jules Pacheu, concludes by noting that Angela's influence transformed Ubertin's impetuous character into a life of religious rigor.</summary>

# A. TRALIN, Publisher, 12, rue du Vieux-Colombier, Paris (6th Arrondissement)

**TAULER (John)**, Dominican, 1294-1361.
— *Complete Works*, literal translation of the Latin version by Surius <note>Laurentius Surius (1522–1578) was a Carthusian monk who translated Tauler’s Middle High German works into Latin, making them accessible to a wider European audience.</note> by E.-P. Noël, O.P. <note>Order of Preachers, the Dominican Order.</note>, Master of Sacred Theology. Eight beautiful octavo <term>in-8</term> <note>A book size where each sheet is folded into eight leaves, roughly 6 by 9 inches.</note> volumes in paper covers. (This work is not sold separately). **80 francs**
— *The Imitation of the Poor Life of Our Lord Jesus Christ*. Translated from German by a priest of the Diocese of Strasbourg, with a preface and notes by Father Noël, O.P. One octavo volume (same format as the works) . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . **10 francs.**

Tauler, the great mystic of the 14th century, is well known by name and reputation, but his works are today read quite little. The cause of this is primarily the lack of good editions: it is this gap that the Reverend Father Noël, O.P., Master of Sacred Theology, fortunately comes to remedy. Moreover, it is not a simple translation he offers us; in an extensive introduction, he introduces us to the life of Tauler and the essence of his doctrine; as for the text of the sermons themselves, we will first note that the translation, although literal, is easy to read and does not lack elegance. Furthermore, Tauler aims for simplicity and conciseness; he divides his subject so well that all intellects can follow him without fatigue. We do not need to analyze the mystical doctrine which has long since received the approval of the masters of the spiritual life and remains ever relevant.

->PUBLISHED SEPARATELY BY THE SAME AUTHOR:<-

— *The Institutions*. 1 duodecimo <term>in-12</term> <note>A book size where each sheet is folded into twelve leaves, smaller than octavo.</note> volume of 416 pages . . . . . . . . . **5 francs.**

**DU PONT (Father Louis, S.J.)** <note>Luis de la Puente (1554–1624), a Spanish Jesuit author of influential spiritual treatises.</note>.
— *Life of Father Balthasar Alvarez*. Soul director of Saint Teresa <note>Saint Teresa of Avila, whom Alvarez guided during a difficult period of her mystical experiences.</note>. New translation, based on the original texts, by the Reverend Father J.-B. Couderc, S.J. One beautiful octavo volume of 545 pages . . . . . . . **10 francs 50**

**PACHEU (Abbé Jules)**.
— *Jacopone da Todi*, Franciscan Friar Minor (1230-1306). Presumed author of the *Stabat Mater* <note>A famous 13th-century Latin hymn portraying the Virgin Mary’s suffering at the foot of the Cross.</note>; followed by the Italian text and a French translation of a selection of his poems. One beautiful and substantial small octavo volume, with a portrait on the cover . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . **9 francs.**

There are very pretty and very moving things in the flower-collection of the *Laudes* <note>original: "Laudes"; spiritual songs of praise or poems written in the vernacular.</note>, in these spiritual canticles of Jacopone da Todi, and the writer gives us very curious insights into the life of this holy poet.
The old poet of Todi is an essentially sympathetic figure; his very religious poetry is of the kind that goes straight to the heart. Thus, we dare to hope that he might please literary readers. Even should they be far removed from this simplicity of Faith, they will enjoy the charm of naivety, spontaneity, popular verve, and the mystical impulses of a "primitive" <note>In this context, "primitive" refers to an artist or writer from an earlier, supposedly more direct and less academic era of expression.</note>.

— *From Dante to Verlaine* (studies of idealists and mystics): Dante, Spenser, Bunyan, Shelley, Verlaine, Huysmans. New edition. One duodecimo volume . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . **5 francs.**
— *Introduction to the Psychology of Mystics*. One duodecimo volume. **3 francs.**
— *From Positivism to Mysticism*. A study of contemporary religious unrest. 2nd edition. One duodecimo volume . . . . . . . . **5 francs.**
— *The Beatitudes of the Gospel: Master Morality or Slave Morality?* <note>A likely critique of Friedrich Nietzsche's philosophy from a Catholic perspective.</note>. One duodecimo volume. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . **3 francs 50**
— *The Good Catholic at the School of Jesus Christ*. Meditation on his social life. One duodecimo volume. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . **3 francs 50**

<summary>This page is a publisher's catalog listing contemporary editions of works by famous mystics like Johannes Tauler and Jacopone da Todi, as well as modern psychological and social studies of religion by Jules Pacheu.</summary>

# **A. TRALIN, Publisher, 12, rue du Vieux-Colombier, Paris (6th Arrondissement)**

## JUST PUBLISHED:

**THE ROMAN MARTYROLOGY**, <note>original: "Le Martyrologe Romain"; a liturgical book containing the list of saints and martyrs honored by the Church on each day of the year.</note> published by the order of Gregory XIII, revised by the authority of Urban VIII and Clement X, increased and corrected in 1749 by Pope Benedict XIV. **French translation** made from the 1922 edition known as the *first after the typical edition*, <note>original: "prima post typicam"; refers to the first printing following the official master version approved by the Vatican.</note> approved by Benedict XV, with introduction and notes by <term>Dom J. Baudot O. S. B.</term> <note>Dom Jules Baudot (1857–1929) was a prominent Benedictine liturgist.</note> Monk of Farnborough. 1 volume in <term>octavo</term> <note>in-8; a book size approximately 6 by 9 inches.</note> of 640 pages, on fine paper. . . . . . . . . . . . **30 fr.**

---

— *On True Contemplation.* 3 beautiful volumes in duodecimo, adorned with portraits, each. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . **3 fr. 50**

**HILDEGARD (Saint)**. Literal translation of the Latin text into English.
— *Life of Saint Hildegard.* One vol. in duodecimo with portrait. . . . . **3 fr. 50**
— *Scivias or Revelations.* <note>Scivias, from the Latin "Scito vias Domini" (Know the Ways of the Lord), is an illustrated work describing Hildegard's 26 religious visions.</note> 2 beautiful volumes in duodecimo adorned with portraits, each . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . **3 fr. 50**

**VALUY (Reverend Father Benoît)**.
— *On Government in Religious Communities.* 8th edition, entirely recast with all currently existing Roman decisions, by the Reverend Father Vulliez-Sermet, <term>S. J.</term> <note>Society of Jesus, the Jesuit Order.</note> A large volume in octavo. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . *(In press)*
— *The Nun in Retreat.* A work comprising: 1st, A directory for the General Retreat; 2nd, A selection of Meditations and Considerations for a private Retreat; 3rd, A series of exercises for the retreat of each month. New edition, revised, improved, annotated and brought into agreement with the latest Roman decisions by Father E. Jombart, <term>S. J.</term> A beautiful volume in duodecimo of 424 pages . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . **7 fr. 50**
— *The Religious Virtues.* 19th edition, revised by Father Vulliez-Sermet. One volume in duodecimo . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . **5 fr.**
— *The Directory of the Priest in his private life and in his public life.* 18th edition. One volume in <term>eighteen-mo</term> <note>in-18; a small pocket-sized book, approximately 4 by 6 inches.</note>. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . **2 fr.**

**GROU (Reverend Father Nicolas)**. <note>Jean-Nicolas Grou (1731–1803) was a French Jesuit and influential spiritual writer.</note>
— *Meditations in the form of a retreat on the love of God, followed by his writing on the gift of oneself to God.* New edition, revised by the Reverend Father Cadrès, <term>S. J.</term> A beautiful volume in duodecimo . . . . . . . **4 fr. 50**
— *Spiritual Maxims with explanations.* New edition. A beautiful volume in duodecimo . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . **3 fr**

<note>Angela of Foligno (c. 1248–1309) was an Italian Christian mystic and tertiary of the Franciscan order. She is known for her "Book of Visions and Instructions," which records her spiritual journey and intensely emotional experiences of the divine. This specific copy appears to be a 1921 edition held in the collection of the University of St. Michael's College in Toronto.</note>

<note>This page displays the decorative cover of the book. It is adorned with marbled paper, a traditional bookbinding technique where pigments are floated on a liquid surface and swirled to create a stone-like pattern before being transferred to the paper. This specific pattern features a dark background with vibrant spots of red and ochre.</note>

<note>The volume is finished in a <term>quarter-binding</term> <note>a binding style where the spine is made of a more durable material, like leather or cloth, while the cover boards are faced with decorative paper</note>. Here, the spine is constructed from black cloth or leather, visible along the edge of the marbled design.</note>
